INFANTRY DRILL REGULATIONS CHAPTER I. INTRODUCTION. GENERAL PROVISIONS Introduction 

1. Purposes of Drill. Drill is the basis of military training and discipline, including training in leadership for noncommissioned officers and junior officers. 

It enables a commander to control his command and move it in an orderly manner. 

It provides simple formations from which combat dispositions are readily assumed. 

Close order drill instills in the soldier the habits of precision and prompt response to orders. Ceremonies enhance the morale of the troops, develop a spirit of cohesion, and afford interesting spectacles to the public. 

2. Extended order drill provides for the orderly deployment of small units for battle, and movement under battlefield conditions. It is not intended for disciplinary purposes and is not executed with precision. 

3. Scope. The drill herein prescribed is for general use, and allows latitude for adaptation to units of any size or kind. Such adaptation should avoid complications. Subordinate commanders should make common sense adjustments in minor matters not specifically covered herein, without calling on higher authority for precise interpretations. 

4. The illustrations in the text are based on the war strength organization of units. But the drill is easily adapted to the actual strength of any unit, and to the maneuver space available. 

5. Conventions in the Text. In this text commands are, for emphasis, printed in boldface, italic type, the command of execution being in CAPITALS, thus: 

1. Forward, 2. MARCH (the numerals are not used in the actual commands). 

References to illustrations are indicated thus: [6], [6-1], meaning "Plate 6." 

"Figure 1, Plate 6." 

6. Definitions. Definitions of some of the important terms used in drill will be found at the end of the course. 

GENERAL RULES FOR CLOSE ORDER DRILL 

7. Precision. Close order drill is habitually executed at Attention, with emphasis on smartness and precision in every detail, especially in the manual of arms and in marching in step with proper alignment. Drills should be frequent, of short duration, with occasional short rests. 

8. Cadence of Quick Time. Movements, including facings, steps and marchings and the manual of arms, are executed in the cadence of quick time, 120 steps or counts to the minute. 

9. Double Time. Any marching movement not specially excepted may be executed at double time, in the cadence of 180 steps to the minute. 

If a unit is at a halt or marching in quick time, the command Double time precedes the command of execution. 

10. Length of Steps. A step at quick time is 30 inches and at double time 

36 inches. Half step and back step are 15 inches, and side steps 12 inches. 

11. Movements to Right and Left. Movements that may be executed toward either flank are explained for one flank. For a movement to the other flank, substitute "left" for "right," or conversely, in the explanation and command, as indicated by words in (parentheses). 

12. Instruction by the Numbers. For purposes of instruction all movements may be divided into separate motions to be executed in detail. The instructor cautions: By the numbers. The first motion is then performed at the command of execution, and subsequent motions at the commands: TWO, THREE, and so on. 

To discontinue execution in detail the instructor cautions: Without the numbers. 

13. Numbering of Units. Squads, sections and platoons are numbered consecutively from right to left and from front to rear throughout the company, and thereafter retain their permanent designations. 

14. Partial Changes of Direction. Changes of direction of 45° are ordered by adding the word half to the preparatory command, as: 1. Column half right_(left).

For slight changes of direction the command is: INCLINE to the right (LEFT). 

The guide or guiding element moves in the indicated direction and the others conform. 

15. Posts of Officers, Noncommissioned Officers, Guidons and Special Units. 

These posts are indicated in the descriptions and illustrations in the text. When a change of post is required, the new post is taken by the most direct route, as soon as practicable after the command of execution. Those having duties in connection with the execution of a movement take their new posts on completion of such duties. Guidons and special units maintain their relative positions with respect to the flank or end of the command on which they were originally posted. 

16. Instructors go wherever their presence is necessary to correct mistakes, and insure proper performance. A noncommissioned officer commanding a unit takes the post prescribed for an officer in command. If armed with the rifle he carries it in the same position as the men in ranks, and when making reports, giving commands or drilling the unit he is at the right shoulder. 1

INFANTRY DRILL REGULATIONS Commands 

17. All movements at drill are executed by oral commands, expressing the will of the commander in the phraseology herein prescribed. When subordinate commanders give commands or cautions these are prescribed. 

18. A command consists of two parts; the preparatory command as: 1. Forward, which indicates the movement to be performed; and the command of execution, as 

2. MARCH, which initiates the movement. The interval between the parts of a command should be sufficient to allow it to be understood, and to permit subordinate leaders to give their own commands, when necessary. So far as practicable the interval should be uniform, as 2 counts in cadence, since irregular intervals may cause ragged performance. 

19. A command should be distinctly pronounced, given in a spirited tone, and loud enough to be heard by all for whom it is intended. The preparatory command should be given in a firm, even tone, and the command of execution emphasized. 

20. When giving commands the commander ordinarily faces his command. 

When, in close order drill or ceremonies, a section or platoon is part of a larger unit, its commander turns his head toward his unit in giving commands, but does not face about. 

21. If a command is to be executed simultaneously by all members of a unit, subordinate leaders do not repeat the command. Otherwise they repeat the command, or give the necessary cautions or commands for the movements of their own units. 

22. If at a halt, commands for movements involving marching, as: 1. Column right, 2. MARCH, are not prefaced by the command Forward. 

23. As close order drill is based on cadence it is essential that, when in motion, a unit shall be accurately in step at the prescribed cadence, and in alignment. 

24. To revoke a command or begin anew a movement improperly begun from a halt, the command: AS YOU WERE is given. The movement then ceases, and former positions are resumed. 

25. General Rules for the Guide. Unless otherwise announced the guide of a platoon or subdivision, in line or column, is right. The announcement of the guide, when necessary, follows the command of execution for the movement. 

26. To march with the guide other than right, or to change guide, the command is: GUIDE RIGHT (LEFT or CENTER). 

27. The guide is responsible for direction of march, cadence and length of step. In column of subdivisions the leading guide is responsible, and guides in rear maintain trace, distance and step. The leading man in each file is responsible for the interval on the side toward the guide, and for alignment. 

CHAPTER II. THE SOLDIER WITHOUT ARMS SECTION I 

28. Duties of the Instructor. The instructor briefly explains each position and movement and demonstrates it. He requires the recruits to take proper positions, touching them only when they cannot correct themselves. He avoids keeping them too long in one position or in one movement. Each movement should be understood before passing to the next. Precision and uniformity are gradually acquired. 

29. Grouping of Recruits. Recruits are divided into small groups for individual instruction, each group formed as a squad under a competent instructor. As instruction progresses they are re-grouped according to proficiency, the least proficient being placed under the best instructors. Inapt men must not be subjected to ridicule or harsh treatment. Instructors must be patient, helpful and courteous.

SECTION II POSITIONS 

30. Position of the Soldier, or Attention. Heels on line, as close together as possible. Feet turned out equally, at an angle of 45°. Knees straight without stiffness. Hips level and drawn back slightly. Body erect, resting equally on hips. 

Chest lifted and arched; shoulders square and falling equally. Arms hanging naturally, thumbs along seams of trousers, backs of hands outward; fingers curled naturally. Head erect, head and eyes squarely to front; chin drawn in so that axis of head and neck is vertical. Weight of body resting equally on heels and balls of both feet. The position is assumed quickly, the heels being brought together with a snap. Both mind and body must be at attention. Silence and immobility are maintained. [1] 

31. Rests. Being at a halt, the commands are: (1) FALL OUT; (2) REST; (3) AT EASE; and (4) 1. Parade, 2. REST. 

32. At the command: FALL OUT, the men leave ranks, but remain in the vicinity. 

They resume their former places at attention at the command: FALL IN. On

POSITIONS. FACINGS. STEPS AND MARCHINGS 

the march they fall in at ease unless they were at attention when the command: 

FALL OUT was given. 

33. At the command: REST, one foot is kept in place, but silence and immobility are not required. At the command: AT EASE, the right foot is kept in place. Silence is required, but not immobility. 

34. At the command: 1. Parade; 2. REST, move the left foot smartly 12 inches to the left, legs straight, weight of body resting on both feet. At the same 

Plate 1. Position of the Plate 2. Parade Rest 

Soldier, or Attention

Plate 3. Hand Salute time clasp the hands behind the back, palms to rear, thumb and fingers of right hand clasping left thumb, without constraint. Preserve silence and immobility. [2] 

35. Being in any position of rest (except fall out), to resume the position of attention the command is: 1. Squad, 2. ATTENTION. 

36. Eyes Right (Left). The commands are: 1. Eyes, 2. RIGHT (LEFT), 

3. Ready, 4. FRONT. At the second command turn the head 45° and the eyes fully to the right. At the fourth command turn head and eyes to front. [25]

Facings 

37. Facings are executed at a halt, in the cadence of quick time.

38. To the Flank. The command is: 1. Right (left), 2. FACE. Raise slightly the left heel and right toe; face squarely to the right on right heel, assisted by a slight pressure on ball of left foot. (TWO) Place the left foot beside the right. For left face the turn is made on the left heel. 

39. To the Rear. The command is: 1. About, 2. FACE. Carry the right toe about half a foot-length to rear and slightly to left of left heel; weight of body resting mainly on left heel, right leg being straight without stiffness. (TWO) Face to rear, turning to the right on left heel and ball of right foot; place right foot beside the left. 

40. Salute with the Hand. The command is: 1. Hand, 2. SALUTE. Raise right hand smartly until tip of forefinger touches lower part of the headdress or (if uncovered) the forehead just above and slightly to right of right eye; thumb and fingers extended and joined, palm to left, upper arm horizontal, forearm inclined at about 45°, hand and wrist straight and in prolongation of forearm. 

At the same time turn head (not more than 45°) and eyes to look directly at the person saluted. (TWO) Drop the arm quickly, but not jerkily, to its normal position at the side in one motion, at same time turning head and eyes to front. 

Execute the first movement of the salute when 6 paces from the person saluted or point of nearest approach. Hold the first position until the salute is returned or the person saluted has passed; then execute the second motion. [3] 

SECTION III STEPS AND MARCHINGS 

41. All steps and marchings executed from a halt (except right step) begin with the left foot. The instructor indicates the cadence, when necessary, by calling One, two, three, four, the left foot to strike the ground on the odd-numbered counts. Any movement may be executed from a halt or while marching, unless otherwise prescribed. 

42. To March Forward in Quick Time. Being at a halt the command is:

​7 

INFANTRY DRILL REGULATIONS 

1. Forward, 2. MARCH. At the command Forward, shift weight of body to right leg, without perceptible movement. At the command MARCH, step off smartly with the left foot, and continue the march in cadence. The arms should swing naturally, about 6 inches to the front, and 3 inches to the rear of the body. 

43. To March Forward in Double Time. Being at a halt or in march at quick time, the command is: 1. Double time, 2. MARCH. If at a halt, shift the weight to the right foot as prescribed above. At the command MARCH, raise the forearms, fists clenched, knuckles out, to a horizontal position along the waist; at same time step out smartly with left foot, and take up an easy run in the step and cadence of double time, arms swinging naturally. If marching in quick time, at the command MARCH, given as either foot strikes the ground, take one more step in quick time, and then take up the double time. 

44. To resume quick time the command is: 1. Quick time, 2. MARCH. At the command MARCH, given as either foot strikes the ground, take one more step in double time, and then resume quick time, dropping the hands to the sides. 

45. To Halt. The command is: 1. Squad (or other unit), 2. HALT. If marching at quick time, at the command HALT, given as either foot strikes the ground, take one more step and bring the foot in rear alongside the one in front. The halt is thus executed in 2 counts. 

46. If marching at double time, at the command HALT, take one more step at double time, and then halt in two counts as in quick time. 

47. To halt from side step, at the command HALT, given as the heels come together, take one more step and halt as the heels again come together. 

48. To Mark Time. The command is: 1. Mark time, 2. MARCH. If marching, at either quick or double time, at the command MARCH, given as either foot strikes the ground, take one more step, bring the foot in rear alongside the one in front, and continue the cadence, alternately raising the feet about 2 inches from the ground and planting them in place. If at a halt, at the command 

MARCH, raise and plant first the left foot, and then the right, as above prescribed. 

Halt is executed from mark time as from quick time, or double time, the steps being vertical instead of horizontal. 

49. To March at Half Step. The command is: 1. Half step, 2. MARCH. 

Executed as prescribed for full step, the steps being 15 inches at quick time and 18 inches at double time. 

50. To Resume Full Step. Being in march at half step or marking time, the command is: 1. Forward, 2. MARCH. 

51. Forward, half step, halt, and mark time, may be executed any one from any other, in either quick or double time. 

52. Side Step. Being at a halt or marking time (at quick time), the command is: 1. Right (Left) step, 2. MARCH. At the command MARCH, carry the right foot 12 inches to the right, bring the left foot alongside, and continue in the cadence of quick time, knees straight. This movement is not executed at double time. 

53. Back Step. Being at a halt or marking time (at quick time), the command is: 1. Backward, 2. MARCH. Take steps of 15 inches straight to the rear. This movement is used for short distances only, and is not executed at double time. 

54. To Face in Marching. These are very important movements. (1) To face to the right in marching and advance from a halt, at the command of execution of the movement, turn to the right on the ball of the right foot and at the same time step off in the new direction with the left foot with a half step, full step, or in double time, as the case may be. (2) To face to the right in marching and advance, being in march, at the command of execution, given as the right foot strikes the ground, advance and plant the left foot, then face to the right in marching and step off in the new direction with the right foot with a half step, full step, or in double time. (3) To face to the rear in marching, being in march, the command is: 

1. To the rear, 2. MARCH. At the command MARCH, given as the right foot strikes the ground, advance and plant the left foot; turn to the right about on the balls of both feet and immediately step off with the left foot. 

55. To March by the Flank. Being in march, the command is: 1. By the right (left) flank, 2. MARCH. At the command MARCH, given as the right foot strikes the ground, advance and plant the left foot, face to the right in marching, and step off in the new direction with the right foot. 

56. To Change Step. Being in march at quick time, the command is: 1. Change step, 2. MARCĂ. At the command MARCH, given as either foot strikes the ground, take one more full step, bring the toe of the foot in rear opposite the heel of the one in front, and step off again with the foot in front. 

57. To March at Route Step or At Ease. The command is: 1. Route (pronounced 

ROUT) step, 2. MARCH; or 1. At ease, 2. MARCH. At route step men are not required to march at attention, or in cadence, or to maintain silence. In marching at ease the same applies, except that silence is maintained.

RULES FOR CARRYING THE RIFLE. MANUAL OF ARMS 

5 

CHAPTER III. THE SOLDIER WITH ARMS SECTION I 

58. Instruction of Recruits. As soon as practicable, the recruit is taught the use, care, and nomenclature of the rifle. When fair progress has been made in instruction without arms, he is taught the manual of arms. Instruction without arms and with arms should alternate. 

59. Rules Governing Carrying of Rifles. Except where otherwise indicated, these rules will be applicable alike to the U. S. rifle, caliber .30, M1903, and to the 

U. S. rifle, caliber .30, M1. (1) The rifle M1903 is not carried with cartridges in either chamber or magazine except when specifically ordered. When so loaded or supposed to be loaded, it is habitually carried locked; that is, with the safety lock turned to SAFE. 

At all other times it is carried unlocked with the trigger pulled. The cut-off is turned off except when cartridges are actually used. [17] (2) Whenever troops equipped with the rifle M1903, are formed under arms, pieces are immediately inspected at the command: 1. Inspection, 2. ARMS. A similar inspection is made before dismissal. If cartridges are found in the chamber or magazine they are removed and placed in the belt. (3) The U. S. rifle, caliber .30, M1, is not carried with cartridges in either the chamber or the receiver except when specifically ordered. When so loaded or when supposed to be loaded, it is habitually carried locked (safety in rearmost position). (4) Whenever troops equipped with the M1 rifle are formed under arms, pieces are immediately inspected at the command: 1. Inspection, 2. ARMS, 3. LOCK 

PIECES. At the third command, each man in ranks closes the bolt, sets the safety of his piece in its rearmost position, and resumes Port arms. Rifles are again inspected at dismissal by the command: 1. Inspection, 2. ARMS, 3. UNLOCK PIEČES. 

At the third command, each man in ranks closes the bolt, sets the safety of his piece in its foremost position, pulls the trigger, and resumes Port arms. If cartridges are found in the chamber or receiver, they are removed and placed in the belt. The procedure prescribed for these inspections is intended to insure: first, that M1 rifles in the hands of troops are carried cocked and locked; and second, that hammers are released on unloaded pieces not in use to prevent damage from long compression of the hammer springs. (5) The bayonet is fixed only when so ordered. (6) Fall in is executed with the rifle at Order arms. (7) Before starting any movement for troops armed with rifles, the command: 1. Right shoulder, 2. ARMS, is given before the command for movement. (8) Movements may be executed at trail arms by prefacing the preparatory command with the words At trail, as: 1. At trail, forward, 2. MARCH. The trail is taken at the command MARCH. (9) Weapons such as the automatic rifle, light machine gun, light mortar, etc., which have no manual of arms aré slung from the right shoulder at the command ARMS of Right shoulder arms. They are kept slung until the command REST, or AT EASE is given. [16] (10) When the facings, alignments, open and close ranks, form for shelter tents, side step, back step, extend and close, are executed from order arms, the position of trail arms is taken at the command of execution, and order arms is resumed on halting. (11) At the command HALT, men armed with the rifle remain at the position of right (or left) shoulder arms. arms. (12) In marching at double time a disengaged hand is held as when without 

SECTION II MANUAL OF ARMS FOR THE RIFLE 

60. General Rules for the Manual of Arms. Except as otherwise indicated, these rules are applicable to both the M1903 and the M1 rifle. (1) In all positions of the left hand at the balance the thumb clasps the rifle, and the gun sling is included in the grip. In the M1903 rifle the balance is at the center of the rear sight leaf. In the M1 it is at a point just forward of the trigger housing. [7; 8] (2) In all positions of the rifle, diagonally across the body, the barrel is up, butt in front of right hip, barrel crossing opposite junction of neck with left shoulder. The rifle is grasped at the balance with the left hand, palm toward body, wrist straight. [7; 10] (3) Recruits are first required to give their entire attention to details of motions, cadence being gradually acquired as they become accustomed to handling their rifles. The instructor may require them to count aloud in cadence with the motions. (4) The cadence of motions (without the numbers) is that of quick time.

The first motion in coming to any position is performed at the command of execution (as ARMS), and other motions then follow in cadence. (5) The manual is not executed in marching except to pass from right shoulder to left shoulder or port arms and the converse in marching at attention. 

These movements may be used to add interest to the drill or prevent fatigue in long marches at attention. (6) The manual is taught at a halt and the movements may, for purposes of instruction, be divided into motions and executed by the numbers. In this case, the command of execution determines the execution of the first motion, and the commands TWO, THREE, FOUR, that of the other motions. (7) To execute the movement in detail, the instructor first cautions, By the numbers. All movements divided into motions are then executed as above explained until he cautions, Without the numbers. (8) Any appropriate position of the manual of arms may be ordered from a previous position. (9) Under exceptional conditions of weather and fatigue, the rifle may be carried as directed. 

61. Position of Order Arms. The butt of the rifle rests an the ground, barrel to rear, toe of butt on line with toe of, and touching the right shoe, right hand holding the rifle between thumb and fingers, left hand as in position of the soldier 

62. Being at Order Arms. 1. Trail, 2. ARMS. At the command ARMS, raise the rifle and incline the piece, muzzle forward, about 15° from the vertical, right arm slightly bent. [5] 63. Being at Trail Arms. 1. Order, 2, ARMS. Lower the rifle and resume the order. 

64. Being at Order Arms to Sling Arms. The command is: 1. Sling, 2. ARMS. 

This movement applies to any individual weapon provided with a sling. It is not executed in cadence. Loosen the sling (if necessary) and take the position in 

Plate 4. Order Arms Plate 5. Trail Arms Plate 6. Sling Arms the most convenient manner. [6] This position is authorized for long ceremonies and on other occasions when long holding of the rifle is fatiguing. When used at ceremonies the bayonet may be fixed. 

65. Being at Sling Arms to Unsling Arms. The command is: 1. Unsling, 2. ARMS, 

3. Adjust, 4. SLINGS. At the command ARMS, pieces are unslung in the most convenient and expeditious manner, not in cadence. At the command SLINGS, slings are adjusted to drill position. This will be done before any further movements are executed. 

66. Being at Order Arms. 1. Port, 2. ARMS. Raise the rifle with the right hand and carry it diagonally across the body until right hand is in front of and slightly to left of chin, barrel up, butt in front of right hip, barrel crossing opposite junction of neck and left shoulder. At the same time grasp the rifle at the balance with the left hand, palm toward body, wrist straight. (TWO) 

Carry right hand to small of stock, grasping it, knuckles out, forearm horizontal. [7]

MANUAL OF ARMS 

7 

67. Being at Order Arms. 1. Present, 2. ARMS. With the right hand carry the rifle in front of center of body, barrel to rear and vertical, and grasp it with left hand at balance, left forearm horizontal and against the body. (TWO) 

Grasp small of stock with right hand, thumb to rear. [8] 

Plate 7. Execution of Port Arms 

68. Being at Present or Port Arms. 1. Order, 2. ARMS. Let go of rifle with right hand, and regrasp it between upper sling swivel and stacking swivel. (TWO) 

Let go of rifle with left hand and lower it to side with right hand until the butt is 3 inches from the ground, barrel to rear. Steady the piece with left hand, Plate 8. Execution of Present Arms Plate 9. Next to Last Position of Order Arms forearm, wrist and fingers straight and inclining downward, fingers touching the piece above the right hand (next to last position of order arms). [9] (THREE) 

Lower rifle gently to ground with right hand, cut left hand smartly to side, take position of order arms. [4] 

69. Being at Order Arms. 1. Inspection, 2. ARMS. 

(1) M1903 rifle. Take position of port arms. of right hand grasp the bolt handle, turn it up and head and eyes sufficiently to glance into magazine. having emptied it, raise head and eyes to front. 

With thumb and forefinger draw bolt to rear. Lower 

If magazine is empty, or (2) M1 rifle. Take position of port arms. With fingers of left hand closed place thumb on operating rod handle and push it smartly to the rear until it is caught by the catch. Lower head and eyes sufficiently to glance into receiver. If receiver is empty, or having emptied it, raise head and eyes to front, and regrasp piece with left hand at balance.

70. Being at Inspection Arms. (1) M1903 rifle. 1. Order (Right shoulder, or 

Port), 2. ARMS. At the preparatory command, close and lock bolt, squeeze the trigger, and take position of port arms. At the command ARMS, complete the movement ordered (if other than port arms). 

(2) M1 rifle. 1. LOCK PIECES, 2. Order (Right shoulder), 3. ARMS; or 1. UNLOCK PIECES, 2. DISMISSED. At the command LOCK (UNLOCK) PIECES, place right side of right hand against operating rod handle, fingers extended and joined thumb on follower. (TWO) Move operating rod handle slightly to rear, depress follower with right thumb, and allow bolt to ride forward about an inch over the follower. (THREE) Remove thumb from follower and release operating rod handle. (FOUR) Lock the piece, or unlock it and pull trigger as the case may be (Par. 59-4) and resume position of port arms. When the pieces have been thus locked (unlocked), the command: 1. Order (Right shoulder), 2. ARMS, is given, or the unit is dismissed. 

71. Being at Order Arms. 1. Right shoulder, 2. ARMS. Raise and carry the rifle diagonally across the body as in the first motion of order arms to port arms (Par. 66). (TWO) Regrasp rifle with right hand on butt, heel of butt between first and second fingers. (THREE) Without releasing grasp of right hand, place rifle on right shoulder, barrel up and inclined to rear about 45° from the horizontal, Plate 10. Execution of Right Shoulder Arms trigger guard in hollow of shoulder, right forearm horizontal, elbow against side of body, rifle in a vertical plane perpendicular to the front. Place left hand, thumb and fingers extended and joined, against small of stock, first joint of forefinger touching the cocking piece (or rear of receiver of M1 rifle), wrist straight, elbow down. (FOUR) Cut left hand smartly to side. [10] 

72. Being at Right Shoulder Arms. 1. Port, 2. ARMS. Press butt of piece down quickly and throw rifle across body, rotating it to the left, into the position described in (TWO) in preceding paragraph. (TWO) Grasp small of stock with right hand. 

73. Being at Right Shoulder Arms. 1. Order, 2. ARMS. Execute the first motion of right shoulder arms to port arms as in preceding paragraph. Complete the movement as from present or port arms (Par. 68). 

74. Being at Port Arms. 1. Right shoulder, 2. ARMS. Grasp the butt with right hand as described in Par. 71. (TWO) (THREE) Execute the last two movements of order to right shoulder arms (Par. 71). 

75. Being at Order Arms. 1. Parade, 2. REST. At the command of execution, move the left foot smartly 12 inches to left of right foot, keeping knees straight, weight of body resting equally on both feet. At same time extend the right arm, right hand grasping the rifle just below upper band, muzzle inclined to the front. 

Place left hand in small of back, palm to rear. [11] 

76. Being at Parade Rest. 1. Squad, 2. ATTENTION. Take the position of order arms in one count. 

77. Being at Right Shoulder Arms. 1. Left shoulder, 2. ARMS. Execute Port arms in 2 counts. (THREE) Let go of rifle with left hand, and with right hand still grasping small of stock place it on left shoulder, barrel up, trigger guard in hollow of shoulder. At same time grasp butt with left hand, heel between first and second

MANUAL OF ARMS 

9 fingers, thumb and fingers closed on stock, left forearm horizontal, elbow against side, rifle in a vertical plane perpendicular to the front. (FOUR) Cut right hand quickly to side. [12] 

78. Left shoulder arms may be executed from order, port, present or right shoulder. First execute port arms, and then complete the movement as above described.

79. Being at Left Shoulder Arms. 1. Port, 2. ARMS. Grasp rifle with right hand at small of stock. (TWO) Let go with left hand, and at same time carry the rifle with right hand to position of port arms, and regrasp it at the balance with left hand. 

80. Being at Left Shoulder Arms. 1. Right shoulder, 2. ARMS. Execute port arms as in preceding paragraph, and then complete the movement port to right shoulder. 

81. Being at Left Shoulder Arms. 1. Order, 2. ARMS. Execute port arms, and then complete the movement port to order.

82. Being at Order or Trail Arms. 1. Rifle, 2. SALUTE. Carry left hand smartly to right side, palm down, thumb and fingers extended and joined, wrist and forearm straight, first joint of forefinger between stacking swivel and muzzle.

83. Being at Right Shoulder Arms. 1. Rifle, 2. SALUTE. Carry left hand smartly to small of stock, palm down, wrist and forearm straight, fingers extended and joined, first joint of forefinger touching end of cocking piece (or rear of receiver of M1 rifle). Turn head and eyes to look at person saluted. (TWO) Cut left hand smartly to side, turn head and eyes to front. [14] 

84. Being at Order Arms. 1. Fix, 2. BAYONETS. This movement is not executed in cadence. If the bayonet scabbard is carried on the belt, move muzzle to left front, and grasp the rifle with left hand below stacking swivel. Grasp bayonet with right hand, pressing spring with forefinger, draw the bayonet and fix it on the barrel, glancing at the muzzle. Resume position of order arms. If the bayonet is carried on the haversack it is fixed in the most convenient manner. [15] 

85. Being at Order Arms. 1. Unfix, 2. BAYONETS. This movement is not executed in cadence. Take the position for fix bayonets. Grasp handle of bayonet with right hand, pressing the spring; unfix and raise it until its handle is about 

12 inches above the muzzle, drop point of bayonet to left, and glancing at scabbard, return the bayonet, the blade passing between left arm and body. Regrasp rifle with right hand and resume position of order arms. If the bayonet is carried on the haversack it is unfixed and sheathed in the most convenient manner. 

SECTION III Loadings and Firings 

86. General Rules. Except as otherwise indicated the following instructions are applicable to both the M1903 and the M1 rifle. 

87. For ceremonial firing only the front rank of units larger than a squad executes loading and firing. The squad is formed in line preliminary to such firing. [18; 19] Rifles M1903 are loaded and locked before any orders for firing are given. Rifles M1 are loaded while locked. 

88. Except when used as a single loader the M1 rifle is habitually loaded by placing a full clip of 8 cartridges in the receiver. 

89. Except during firing, if rifles have been ordered loaded and locked, they are kept so without command until the command UNLOAD, or 1. Inspection, 2. ARMS. 

During firing, fresh clips are inserted when the magazine or clip is exhausted. 

90. To Load. The unit being in any formation, at a halt, the command is: 

1. With ball (blank, dummy or guard) cartridges, 2. LOAD. (1) M1903 rifle. At the command LOAD, each front rank rifleman faces half right and carries the right foot 12 inches to the right and to such position as will insure the greatest firmness and steadiness of the body; raises or lowers the rifle and drops it into the left hand at the balance, left thumb extending along the stock, muzzle pointed into the air at an angle of 45°, and turns the cutoff up. With the right hand he turns up the bolt and draws it back; takes a loaded clip and inserts the end in the clip slots; places his thumb on the powder space of the top cartridge, the fingers extending around the rifle and tips resting on the OFF ON 

Plate 17. Positions of the Safety Lock and Cutoff, M1903 Rifle magazine floor plate; forces the cartridges into the magazine by pressing down with the thumb; removes the clip; thrusts the bolt home, turning down the handle; turns the safety lock to the SAFE and carries the hand to the small of the stock. 

Automatic riflemen do not execute the ceremonial loadings or firings. [17; 18] (2) M1 rifle. At the command LOAD, each front rank rifleman faces half right and carries his right foot 12 inches to the right and to such position as will secure the greatest firmness and steadiness of the body, raises or lowers the rifle and drops it into the left hand at the balance, right hand at the small of the stock, muzzle in the air at an angle of 45°. With the forefinger of the right hand, he pulls the operating rod handle smartly to the rear until the operating rod is caught by the operating rod catch. With his right hand he takes a fully loaded clip and places it on top of the follower. He places the right side of his right hand against the operating rod handle and with his thumb presses the clip down into the receiver until it engages the clip latch. He swings his thumb to the right so as to clear the bolt in its forward movement. He releases the

LOADINGS AND FIRINGS 

11 operating rod handle. He then pushes forward on the operating rod handle with the heel of his right hand to make certain of complete closing of the bolt and carries his right hand to the small of the stock. 

Plate 18. Position of Load in Ceremonial Firing 91. For instruction purposes the command is: 1. Simulate, 2. LOAD. Executed as described in the preceding paragraphs for the two models of rifle, except that the handling of cartridges in simulated, and in the M1903 rifle the cutoff remains at 

OFF. [17] 

92. To Use the Rifle as a Single Loader. (1) The M1903 rifle may be used as a single loader by turning the cutoff to OFF. The magazine may be partly or wholly filled by pressing single cartridges down and back until they are in the right place. This use of the rifle is exceptional. (2) The M1 rifle is used as a single loader as follows. On taking the position of load, open the bolt. With the right hand, place one round in the chamber, seating it in place with the thumb. With the side of the right hand against the operating rod handle and the fingers extended and joined, force the operating rod handle slightly to the rear, depress the follower with the right thumb, and permit the bolt to ride forward about 1 inch over the follower. Then remove the thumb from the follower, release the operating rod handle and push forward on the operating rod handle with the heel of the hand to be certain that the bolt is completely closed. 

Carry the right hand to the small of the stock. 

93. To Unload. Being in any formation, at a halt, the command is: UNLOAD. (1) The M1903 rifle. Take the position of load, turn the safety lock up, and move the bolt alternately backward and forward until all the cartridges are ejected. 

The chamber is then closed by first thrusting the bolt forward slightly to free it from the stud holding it in place when the chamber is opened, pressing the follower down and back to engage it under the bolt, and then thrusting the bolt home. The trigger is then pulled, the cartridges are picked up, cleaned, and returned to the belt, and the rifle is brought to the order. (2) The M1 rifle. Take the position of load. Hook the right thumb over the operating rod handle, pull and hold the operating rod in the extreme rear position. 

Hold the rifle with the right hand, thumb on operating rod handle, fingers around trigger guard. Steady by pressing the stock against the right hip. Place left hand over receiver and release the clip latch with the thumb. Catch the ejected clip in the left hand, return to the belt, return the left hand to the balance. Place right side of right hand against the operating rod handle and force the rod slightly to the rear. Depress follower with right thumb and permit the bolt to ride forward about 1 inch over the follower. Remove the thumb from the follower and release the operating rod handle. Bring the rifle to the order. 

94. To Fire the Rifle. (1) M1903 rifle, fully loaded with ball (blank or guard) ammunition. Squeeze the trigger for each shot. Then draw back and thrust home the bolt with the right hand. The safety lock remains at READY. (2) M1 rifle, fully loaded with ball ammunition. Squeeze trigger for each shot.

When the 8th shot is fired the clip is automatically ejected and the bolt remains open for the insertion of a fresh clip. Fully loaded with blank ammunition.

Squeeze the trigger for each shot. Then pull the operating rod handle to the rear with right forefinger and release it. Push forward on operating rod handle with heel of right hand to insure that the bolt is fully closed. 

95. To Fire by Volley. Being in firing formation with rifles loaded, the command is: 1. Front rank, 2. READY, 3. AIM, 4. Squad, 5. FIRE. For ceremonial purposes blank ammunition is used, and only the front rank fires. [19] (1) M1903 rifle. At the command READY take the position of load, if not already in that position, and turn the safety lock to READY. At the command 

AIM, raise the rifle with both hands, butt placed and held firmly against the shoulder, left hand well under the rifle grasping it at or in front of the balance, rifle resting in palm of left hand, right elbow at height of shoulder, right cheek held firmly against the stock as far forward as it can be placed without straining. The rifle is raised 45° from the horizontal or, if ordered, held at the horizontal. The left eye is closed, right eye looking through the notch of the rear sight. At the command 

FIRE, the trigger is squeezed rapidly; the rifle is then lowered to the position of load and reloaded. (2) M1 rifle. At the command READY take the position of load, if not already in that position. At the command AIM, raise the rifle with both hands, butt placed and held firmly against the shoulder, left hand well under the rifle grasping it at or in front of the balance, rifle resting in palm of left hand, right elbow 

INT Plate 19. Position of Fire in Ceremonial Firing at height of shoulder, right cheek held firmly against the stock as far forward as it can be placed without straining, and press the safety lock to its forward position with the trigger finger. The rifle is raised 45° from the horizontal or, if ordered, held at the horizontal. The left eye is closed, right eye looking over the rear sight. At the command FIRE, the trigger is squeezed rapidly; the rifle is then lowered to the position of load and reloaded. 

96. To Continue Firing. The command is: 1. AIM, 2. Squad, 3. FIRE. Each shot is executed as heretofore prescribed.

97. To Cease Firing. The command is: CEASE FIRING. Firing stops and all rifles not in the position of load are brought to that position.

98. General Rules. Except as otherwise prescribed the automatic rifle is carried slung over the right shoulder, as shown in Plate and field exercises it may be slung over either shoulder. at ease automatic rifles remain slung, unless otherwise troops are at rest they may be unslung and the position of order arms taken. 

Men armed with the automatic rifle render the hand salute when not in ranks. 

For drill and ceremonies the commanding officer may substitute rifles for automatic rifles.

CARRYING THE AUTOMATIC RIFLE. MANUAL OF THE PISTOL 

13 99. Only the following movements of the manual are executed by the automatic rifleman. (1) Parade rest. If at sling arms, execute as without arms, keeping the right hand on the sling. (2) Inspection arms. Grasp the magazine with the left hand, press the magazine release with the right hand, withdraw the magazine and place in belt. Pull back operating handle with left hand. 

(3) Being at Inspection Arms. The command is: 1. Order (Port, Right shoulder), 2. ARMS. At the command of execution, pull the trigger, replace the magazine, and resume the position of attention, the weapon remaining slung. 

SECTION V Manual of the Pistol. Dismounted 

100. General Rules. The movements here described need not, as a rule, be executed with precision, or in cadence or unison. They are designed merely as simple, quick and safe methods of handling the pistol. Commands are prescribed for such movements as may occasionally be executed in unison by squads or larger units. 

101. In general, movements begin and end at the position raise pistol. Officers and men armed with the pistol remain at attention during the manual of arms, except when their units are presented, during ceremonies. In such case they execute the first motion of hand salute at the command 1. Present, 2. ARMS, and resume the position of attention at the next command. 

102. Commands for firing are limited to COMMENCE FIRING, and CEASE 

FIRING. 

103. The following positions and movements are illustrated in Plate 20. 

104. To Raise Pistol. The command is: 1. Raise, 2. PISTOL. Unbutton flap of 

1 -> (3 

1. Raise pistol 

Plate 20. Manual of the Pistol 

2. Withdraw magazine 

8. Pull slide down in loading 

4. Open chamber 

5. Inspection pistol 

6. Return pistol

holster with right hand and grasp the stock, back of the hand outward.

Draw the pistol from the holster: reverse it, muzzle up, thumb and last three fingers holding the stock, forefinger extended outside the trigger guard, barrel of pistol to rear and inclined to the front at an angle of 30°, the hand as high as, and 6 inches in front of, the point of the right shoulder. This is the position of raise pistol. 

105. To Withdraw the Magazine. Without lowering the right hand, turn the barrel slightly to the right; press the magazine catch with right thumb and with the left hand remove the magazine. Place it in the belt or pocket. 

106. To Open the Chamber. Withdraw the magazine and resume position of raise pistol. Without lowering the right hand, grasp the slide with thumb and first two fingers of left hand (thumb on left side of slide and pointing downward); keeping the muzzle elevated, shift grip of right hand so that right thumb engages with the slide stop. Push the slide downward to its full extent and force the slide stop into its notch with the right thumb, without lowering the muzzle of the pistol. 

107. To Close the Chamber. With right thumb press down the slide stop and let slide forward. Squeeze the trigger. 

108. To Insert a Magazine. Without lowering the right hand, turn the barrel to the right. Grasp a magazine with first two fingers and thumb of left hand; withdraw it from the belt and insert in the pistol. Press it fully home. 

109. To Load. The command is: 1. Load, 2. PISTOL. If a loaded magazine is not already in the pistol, insert one. Without lowering the right hand, turn the barrel slightly to the left. Grasp the slide with thumb and fingers of left hand (thumb on right side of slide and pointing upward). Pull the slide downward to its full extent. Release the slide and engage the safety lock. 

110. To Unload. The command is: 1. Unload, 2. PISTOL. Withdraw the magazine. Open the chamber, and see that it is empty. Close the chamber, take the position of raise pistol, squeeze the trigger, and insert an empty magazine. 

111. To Inspect Pistol. The command is: 1. Inspection, 2. PISTOL. Withdraw the magazine. Open the chamber. Take the position of raise pistol. The withdrawn magazine is held in the open left hand at height of belt. After the pistol has been inspected, or at the command 1. Return, 2. PISTOL, close the chamber, take position of raise pistol, and squeeze the trigger. Insert an empty magazine and execute return pistol. 

112. To Return Pistol. The command is: 1. Return, 2. PISTOL. Lower the pistol to the holster, reversing it, muzzle down, back of hand to the right; raise flap of holster with right thumb; insert the pistol in the holster and thrust it home; button flap of the holster with right hand.

SECTION VI 

Manual of the Guidon. Dismounted 

113. The guidon is a company identification flag, or emblem, carried at ceremonies and when prescribed by the commander. In camp it is displayed at company headquarters. In combat it is with the company baggage. The guidon bearer is a specially selected enlisted man designated by the company commander. 

114. General Rules. The guidon is brought to present arms, parade rest, and order arms with the company. At the commands of execution for other movements it is brought to the carry, except in the execution of the manual at parades. 

When executing the facings, side steps and alignments the quidon is held at carry. 

When marching at route step or at east the guidon is at carry, the pike held in either hand. When at order the guidon is brought to carry at the command of execution for any marching in quick time. 

115. Carry Guidon. The pike of the guidon is held vertically in the right hand, resting in the hollow between the thumb and first finger, back of hand to the right, arm extending downward, pike resting in the hollow of the shoulder, ferrule 6 inches from the ground. [21] Carry is the habitual position when troops are marching. 

116. Order Guidon to Carry Guidon. Grasp the pike with the left hand at the same time loosening the grip of the right hand. Raise the guidon vertically with left hand, the pike sliding through the right hand until the ferrule is 6 inches from the ground, and then regrasp the pike with the right hand. Cut the left hand away smartly to the left side 

117. Carry Guidon to Order Guidon. Allow the pike to slide through the right hand until the ferrule is on the ground on line with and touching the toe of the right shoe, right hand grasping the pike as in the carry. 

118. Order Guidon to Parade Rest. Executed as for parade rest with the rifle. 

119. Carry or Order Guidon to Present Guidon. Lower the guidon straight to the front, extending the right arm nearly horizontal until the pike, resting in the pit of the right arm, is horizontal. This movement is executed at the command of execution, ARMS, of 1. Present, 2. ARMS.

120. In passing in review the guidon bearer executes eyes right and present guidon at the second movement of the company commander's saber salute (or at the first movement of his hand salute) and returns to front and carry guidon at the last movement of the company commander's salute.

121. Present Guidon to Carry Guidon. Grasp the pike with the left hand, palm up, at a point just beyond the right hand. With left hand carry the guidon up and back to the position of carry. At the same time the right hand, retaining its grasp on the pike, is lowered to the right side. Cut the left hand away smartly to the left side. 

122. Present Guidon to Order Guidon. Executed as prescribed in Par. 121, except that when the guidon is brought to the vertical position, the pike is allowed, if necessary, to slide through the right hand, steadied by the left hand, until the guidon is in the position of order. 

123. Individual Salute by Guidon Bearer. At the carry or order the salute is rendered with the left hand in the same manner as rifle salute at the order. 

124. Double Time. At double time, the guidon is held diagonally across the body, right hand grasping the pike at the position used at carry, right forearm horizontal, elbow near body, left hand grasping the pike opposite the junction of the neck and left shoulder.

SECTION VII Manual of the Color 125. General Rules as to Care and Use of Colors. (1) The flags carried by dismounted troops are known as the colors (national and regimental). Those carried by mounted or motorized organizations are known as standards. The term "colors" includes the national and regimental flags, "color" means the national flag. (2) In garrison, the colors (standards) when not in use are kept at the office or quarters of the commanding officer, and escorted thereto and therefrom by the color guard. In camp the colors when not in use are displayed in front of the commanding officer's tent. From reveille to retreat, when weather permits, they are displayed uncased. From retreat to reveille and during inclement weather, they are cased and placed in the commanding officer's office, quarters, or tent. (Colors are cased when furled, and inclosed in a waterproof cover). (3) The colors may be carried in any formation in which two or more companies participate, and in escorts when ordered. (4) În separate organizations and commands entitled to colors and in battalions not stationed with regimental headquarters, the colors are similarly cared for and displayed at the office, quarters, or tent of the commanding officer.

(5) Battalions and squadrons stationed with their regimental headquarters do not display the colors of their organizations. (6) At regimental formations, the colors are posted with the regiment. 

When the regimental formation is broken up, as at drill or field exercises, the colors join the regimental commander or are dismissed as directed. 

126. Salutes by the Colors. The national color renders no salutes. (1) The regimental color salutes at ceremonies whenever the National Anthem or "To the Color" is played, and when rendering honors to its regimental commander or an individual of higher rank, but in no other case. (2) If marching, the regimental color salutes when six paces from the person entitled to the salute and resumes the carry when six paces, beyond him. (3) In passing in review the color guard executes eyes right at the command of the senior color sergeant, who commands: 1. Eyes, 2. RIGHT and 1. Ready, 2. FRONT at the saluting distances prescribed in (2) above. The regimental color also salutes ([1] above) at the command right and resumes the carry at the command FRONT. The man on the right flank does not execute eyes right. In those organizations which do not execute eyes right in passing in review, the color guard omits this compliment. Where applicable, the regimental color salutes at the command of the senior color bearer, who commands: 1. Color, 2. SALUTE. The return to the carry is made at the command: 1. Carry, 2. COLOR. 

127. Color Guard. The color guard consists of two color sergeants who are the regimental color bearers, and two experienced privates selected by the regimental commander, usually for long and honorable service. A position on the color guard is one of honor and responsibility. The privates so designated, when not engaged in the performance of their duties with the color guard, join their organizations. 

The senior color sergeant carries the national color and commands the color guard. 

He gives the necessary commands for movements and for rendering honors, when present, otherwise the senior remaining member does so. The junior color sergeant carries the regimental color. The regimental color is always on the left of the national color in whatever direction they face. 

128. When battalions carry the color, a battalion staff sergeant acts as color bearer, and two experienced privates are selected by the battalion commander as members of the color guard. The general rules prescribed for the regimental color guard are applicable to the battalion. 

129. The color guard is formed and marched in one rank at close interval, the color bearers in the center. The color guard does not execute to the rear march, about face, or fix bayonets. When the unit to which it is attached moves to the rear for short distances, the color guard, at the command of the senior color sergeant, executes column right (left) twice, marches to the new line, again executes column right (left) twice, and halts in its proper place. 

130. At the command of the senior color sergeant, the privates of the color guard present arms or, if armed with the pistol, execute the hand salute on receiving and parting with the colors. After having parted with the colors, the guard is brought to order arms by command of the senior remaining member who is placed as right man of the guard. 

131. Having received the colors, the senior sergeant conducts the guard to its proper position before the color company, as in Par. 132. Having parted with the colors, the guard is dismissed by the senior sergeant. 

132. At drills and ceremonies in which the colors are to participate, except escort of the color, the colors are received by the color company prior to the formation of the battalion with the following ceremony. The color company is formed, its company commander facing the front. The color guard, conducted by the senior sergeant, approaches from the front and halts at a distance of 10 paces from the company commander. The company commander then faces about, brings the company to present arms, faces to the front, salutes, again faces about, and brings the company to the order. The privates of the color guard execute present and order arms with the color company. The color guard is then marched by the senior color sergeant directly to its post, as indicated. When the color company joins the battalion, the color guard takes its appropriate post in the battalion formation. When the color battalion joins the regiment, the color guard takes its appropriate post in the regimental formation. When the color joins the color company, it takes post on the left when the company is in line or mass and in rear when it is in column. 

133. To dismiss the color guard at the conclusion of a drill or ceremony in which the colors participate, the color guard proceeds from its position and halts 10 paces in front of and facing the company commander of the color company. 

The company commander then faces about, brings the company to the present, faces to the front, salutes, again faces about, and brings the company to the order. The privates of the color guard execute present and order arms with the color company. The color guard then escorts the colors to the office, quarters, or tent of the commanding officer. The color guard is dismissed from organizations smaller than a company (funeral escort) in a similar manner.

INFANTRY SQUADS IN LINE AND IN COLUMN 

17 134. In campaign, prior to engagement of the regiment and upon direction of the commanding officer, the colors are stored with the regimental baggage under guard of one color sergeant. The other color sergeant remains with regimental headquarters. 

135. Position of the Color at the Order. At the order the heel of the pike rests on the ground on line with and touching the toe of the right shoe. The right hand at a convenient place on the pike clasps it with the thumb, back of the hand to the right, and holds it in a vertical position.

137. Position of the Color at Parade Rest. This position is similar to parade rest with the rifle, except that the pike is held vertical. The order is resumed at the command ATTENTION. Order and parade rest are executed with the color company. 

138. Position of the Regimental Color at the Color Salute. This position is assumed from the carry by slipping the right hand up the pike to the height of the eye, then lowering the pike by straightening the arm to the front. When the regimental color salutes with troops who execute present arms from the order, the position of carry is assumed at the command ARMS, and the color salute executed. 

139. Position of the Color During the Manual of Arms. The colors habitually remain at the order during execution of the manual of arms. 

CHAPTER IV. DRILL FOR FOOT TROOPS NOTE. For symbols used in illustrations see Plate 30. 

SECTION I THE SQUAD 

140. The squad is a group of soldiers organized primarily as a combat team. 

It consists of one squad leader and other personnel as authorized by appropriate tables of organization. [23] When the squad leader is absent, he is replaced by the second in command. If the second in command is also absent, the next senior member of the squad acts as leader. As far as practicable, the squad is kept intact. The normal formation of the squad is a single rank or single file. This permits variation in the number of men composing the squad. The squad in line marches to the left or to the front only for minor changes of position. [22] 

141. To Form the Squad. The command is: FALL IN. At this command, the squad forms in line. [22] On falling in, each man except the one on the left extends his left arm laterally at shoulder height, palm of the hand down, fingers extended and joined. Each man, except the one on the right, turns his head and eyes to the right and places himself in line so that his right shoulder touches lightly the tips of the fingers of the man on his right. As soon as proper intervals have been obtained, each man drops his arm smartly to his side and turns his head to the front. [24] 

142. To Form at Close Intervals. The command is: 1. At close intervals, 2. FALL 

IN. At the command FALL IN, all fall in as above, except that close intervals are obtained by placing the left hands on the hips. [25] In this position the heel of the palm of the hand rests on the hip, the fingers and thumb are extended ALFI

Plate 24. FALL IN (at Normal Interval)

CLOSE ORDER DRILL. THE SQUAD 

19 and joined, and the elbow is in the plane of the body. 

143. The squad falls in on the right file if the squad leader is not in ranks. 

If the squad is formed under arms, pieces are at once inspected. 

144. Previous Instructions Applicable. The squad executes the positions, movements, and manual of arms as prescribed in Chapters II and III, all men executing the movements simultaneously. 

145. To Dismiss the Squad. The command is: 1. Inspection, 2. ARMS, 3. Port, 

4. ARMS, 5. DISMISSED, or 3. UNLOCK pieces, 4. DISMISSED, (if armed with the M1 rifle). 

Plate 25. At Close Interval, FALL IN 146. To Count Off. The command is: COUNT OFF. Each man of the squad, except the one on the right flank, turns his head and eyes to the right. The right flank man calls out, "One." Each man in succession calls out, "Two," "Three," etc., turning his head and eyes to the front as he gives his number. This command may be given whenever it is desired that the men should know their relative positions in the squad. 

147. To Align the Squad. Being in line, the command is: 1. Dress right (left), 2. DRESS, 3. Ready, 4. FRONT. At the command DRESS, each man except the one on the left, extends his left arm (or if at close interval, places his left hand upon his hip), and all align themselves to the right. The instructor places himself on the right flank one pace from and in prolongation of the line and facing down the line. From this position he verifies the alignment, ordering individual men to move forward or back as necessary. Having checked the alignment, he faces to the right in marching and moves 3 paces forward, halts, faces to the left and commands: 1. Ready, 2. FRONT. At the command FRONT, arms are dropped quietly and smartly to the side and heads turned to the front. 

148. If in column the command is: COVER. At the command, men cover from front to rear with 40 inches distance between men. 

149. Being in Line at Normal Interval, to Obtain Close Interval. The command is: 1. Close, 2. MARCH. At the command, all men except the right flank man face to the right in marching and form at close interval, as prescribed in Par. 142. 

150. Being in Line at Close Interval, to Extend to Normal Interval. The command is: 1. Extend, 2. MARCH. At the command MARCH, all men except the right flank man face to the left in marching and form at normal interval as prescribed in Par. 141. 

151. Being in Line, to March to the Flank. The command is: 1. Right (left), 2. FACE, 3. Forward, 4. MARCH. The movements are executed as explained for the individual, all men stepping off simultaneously. 

152. To March to the Oblique. For the instruction of recruits, the squad being in column or correctly aligned, the instructor causes each man to face half right (left), points out his position, and explains that it is to be maintained in the oblique march. 

153. The squad being in any formation, the command is: 1. Right (left) oblique, 2. MARCH. At the command MARCH, given as the right foot strikes the ground, each individual advances and plants the left foot, faces half right in marching

and steps off in a direction of 45° to the right of his original front. He preserves his relative position, keeping his shoulders parallel to those of the guide (man on right front of line or column), and so regulates his step that the ranks remain parallel to their original front. 

154. The command HALT is given on the left foot when halting from the right oblique and on the right foot when halting from left oblique. At the command 

HALT, given as the left foot strikes the ground, each individual advances and plants the right foot, turns to the front on the ball of the right foot, and places the left foot by the side of the right. 

155. To Resume the Original Direction. The command is: 1. Forward, 2. MARCH. 

Each individual faces half left in marching and then moves straight to the front. 

If at half step or mark time while obliquing, the full step is resumed by the command: 

1. Oblique, 2. MARCH. 

156. To give volume to the command the word "oblique" is pronounced to rhyme with "strike." 

157. To March Toward a Flank While in March. The command is: 1. By the right (left) flank, 2. MARCH. At the command MARCH, each man individually executes the movement. 

158. This movement is used when a quick movement to the right or left for a short distance is required. Normally the unit is halted, faced in the desired direction, and started forward again by the command. 1. Forward, 2. MARCH. 

159. Being in Column, to Change Direction. The command is: 1. Column right 

(left) (half right) (half left), 2. MARCH. The leading man executes the face in marching. The other men in the column execute the same movement successively and on the same ground as the leading man. 

160. Being in Line, to Take Interval and to Assemble. (1) To take interval, the command is: 1. Take interval to the left (right), 2. MARCH. At the command

MARCH, the right flank man stands fast and extends his left arm at shoulder height, palm of hand down, fingers extended and joined, until the man on his left obtains the proper interval, then he drops his arm. Other men face to the left in marching and step out until they have an interval of two arms' lengths from the man on their right. Each man, except the one on the left who raises his right arm only, extends both arms laterally at shoulder height. Each man, except the right flank man, then turns head and eyes to the right and places himself in line so that the finger tips of his right hand touch lightly the finger tips of the left hand of the man on his right. As soon as each man aligns himself at two arms' length interval from the man on his right, he drops his right arm to the side and turns his head and eyes to the front. He drops his left arm to the side when the man on his left has obtained his proper interval. If under arms, rifles will be slung prior to the execution of this movement. (2) To assemble, the command is: 1. Assemble to the right (left), 2. MARCH.

The right flank man stands fast. All other men face to the right in marching and form at normal intervals. 

161. To Stack Arms. The squad being in line at normal or close interval, the 

Plate 26. Stack Arms

CLOSE ORDER DRILL. THE SQUAD AND PLATOON 

21 command is: 1. Stack, 2. ARMS. The second man from the right and the second man from the left of the squad make the stacks. If there are less than six men in the squad, only one stack is made, the second man from the right making it. If there are more than eight men in the squad, three stacks will be made, the center or right center man making the center stack. The stack is made as follows: 

At the command the man on the left of the stackman regrasps his rifle with the right hand at the balance, carrying it to the horizontal position, barrel up, and passes it to the stackman who grasps it with his left hand between the upper sling swivel and stacking swivel, and places the butt between his feet, barrel to the front, muzzle inclined slightly to the front, the thumb and forefinger raising the stacking swivel. The stackman then throws the butt of his own rifle two feet in advance of that of his left file and 6 inches to the right of his right toe; at the same time he allows his right hand to slip to the stacking swivel and engages his rifle with that of his left file. The man on the right of the stackman raises his rifle with his right hand, regrasps it with his right hand at the balance, steps to the left front keeping his right foot in place, and carries his rifle well forward, barrel to the front; the left hand guiding the stacking swivel engages the lower hook of the swivel of his own rifle with the free hook of that of his left file, and then turns the barrel outward into the angle formed by the other two rifles and lowers the butt to the ground so that it will form a uniform stack with the other two rifles. He then assumes the position of attention. Other rifles of the squad are passed toward the nearest stack and laid against the stack by the stackman. [26] 

162. To Take Arms.

The squad being in line behind the stacks, the command is: 1. Take, 2. ARMS. At the command ARMS, the procedure of stacking arms is reversed. The loose rifles are first passed back. In breaking the stack, the stackman grasps his rifle and that of the man on his left, so that the rifles will not fall when the man on the right raises and disengages his rifle. 

Each man, as he receives his rifle, resumes the position of order arms. 

163. Column of Twos. When marching small groups, not at drill, the group may be marched in column of twos by forming it in two ranks and giving the command: 1. Right (left), 2. FACE. 

164. To Form Column of Twos from Single File and Re-form. (1) The squad being in column, at a halt, to form column of twos, the command is: 1. Form column of twos, 2. MARCH. The leading man stands fast; the second man in the squad moves by the oblique until he is to the left of and abreast of the corporal with normal interval, and halts; the third man moves forward until behind the corporal with normal distance and halts; the fourth man moves by the oblique until he is to the left of and abreast of the third man with normal interval, and halts; and so on. (2) The squad being in column of twos, in marching, to re-form single file, the squad is first halted. The command is: 1. Form single file from the right, 2. MARCH. At the command MARCH, the leading man of the right column moves forward, the leading man of the left column steps off to the right oblique, then executes left oblique so as to follow the right file at normal distance. Remaining twos follow successively in like manner. 

SECTION II THE PLATOON 

165. Formations of More than One Squad. (1) The squads form in line, one behind the other, with 40 inches distance between ranks. (2) Squads are usually arranged to produce a three- or four-rank formation so that by facing to the right the unit will march in column of threes or column of fours depending on the number of squads. (3) A two-squad unit forms in two ranks and marches in column of twos. (4) A three-squad unit forms in three ranks and marches in column of threes. (5) A four-squad unit forms in four ranks and marches in column of fours. (6) A platoon composed of two sections of two squads each, forms in four ranks and marches in column of fours. (7) Movements are described herein for column of threes or fours and may be executed by either formation. (8) When in line, the platoon is aligned as prescribed for the squad in Par. 147. The alignment of each rank is verified by the platoon leader. (9) The platoon being in line takes interval and assembles as prescribed for the squad. This movement may be ordered after ranks are opened for the display of field equipment or for other special purpose. It is not utilized in forming for physical training. (10) The platoon is ordinarily formed for physical training from column of threes or fours in accordance with the commands and method prescribed in Chapter IV, Basic Field Manual, Volume I. The extension to the left is executed on the right squad of the column, which stands fast. The even-numbered men in all squads are designated to uncover.

166. Composition and Formation of the Platoon. The platoon consists of platoon headquarters and several squads. Platoon headquarters consists of a platoon leader and one or more assistants. For purposes of drill and ceremonies, a 3-squad or 

4-squad formation should be arranged and the size of the squads equalized. Plates 

27 and 28 show how the platoon formation applies to a rifle platoon and to the weapons platoon of a rifle company. 

167. Positions of Individuals. The platoon leader takes position 6 paces in front of the center of his platoon when in line. In march formation (column of threes or fours), he marches at the head of his platoon. [29]

168. The second in command of a platoon takes position on the left of the left man of the rear rank when squads are in line unless otherwise indicated. When squads are in column, he follows the rear man in the right squad. The second in command observes the conduct of the unit, sees that the proper formation is 'maintained, and that commands are promptly and properly executed. The platoon guide (a sergeant or other specially designated noncommissioned officer) is posted on the right of the right flank man of the front rank when in line. In column, he takes post in front of the right flank man. He is responsible for maintaining the proper direction and cadence of march of the platoon. Other noncommissioned officers (such as mess and supply sergeants, clerks, etc.) and privates (such as cooks, armorers, etc), when attached to the platoon, fall in on the left when in line, or in rear when squads are in column, and march as part of regularly organized squads. 

169. To Form the Platoon. The command is: FALL IN. The first squad forms in line, its center opposite and 3 paces from the platoon sergeant. The other squads form in rear of the first squad and in the same manner, with 40 inches distance between ranks. Members of the rear squads extend their arms to obtain their approximate intervals but cover the corresponding members in the first squad. 

The guide places himself as shown in Plate 27. 

170. To form with close interval, the command is: 1. At close interval, 2. FALL

IN. The movement is executed as prescribed above except that squads form at close interval.

CLOSE ORDER DRILL. THE PLATOON 

23 171. The platoon is ordinarily formed and dismissed by the platoon sergeant (see also Pars. 191 et seq.) 

172. To Dismiss the Platoon. The command is: 1. Inspection, 2. ARMS, 3. Port, 

4. ARMS, 5. Dismissed, or 3. UNLOCK PIECES, 4. dismisseD (if armed with the M1 rifle.) 

173. To March the Platoon. The normal formation for marching is in column of threes (or fours) with squad columns abreast, squad leaders at the heads of their squads. The platoon in line marches to the left or to the front only for minor changes of position. [29] 

174. The_Platoon Being in Line, to March to the Right. The command is: 

1. Right, 2. FACE, 3. Forward, 4. MARCH. This marches the platoon in column of threes (or fours) to the right. 

175. Guide in Marching. Except when otherwise directed, men in ranks keep the proper distance and interval and align themselves on the men toward the flank on which the guide is marching. When it is desired to guide toward the left, the command is: guide lefT. The guide and the platoon leader then change their relative positions.

DIRECTION OF MARCH

Plate 29. Platoon in Line and Marching to Right 

176.

Being in Column of Threes (or Fours) at Normal Interval Between Squads, to March (or Form) at Close Interval. The command is: 1. Close, 2. MARCH. At the command MARCH, the squads close to the center by obliquing until the interval between men is 4 inches. The center squad (or squads) take up the half step until the dress has been regained. The distance, 40 inches, however, remains unchanged. 

Should this movement be executed from the halt, the squads close toward the center by executing right or left step until 4-inch intervals are reached. If in column of threes, the first and third squads left and right step two steps. If in column of fours, the second and third squads left and right step one step, the first and fourth squads left and right step three steps. 

177. Being in Column of Threes (or Fours), at Close Interval Between Squads, to March (or Form) at Normal Interval. The command is: 1. Extend, 2. MARCH. 

The squads open to the right and left from the center by obliquing until the interval between men is one arm's length. The center squad (or squads) take the half step until the dress has been regained. If this movement is executed from the halt, the

squads execute right or left step until they have secured the proper interval by reversing the procedure explained in Par. 176. 

178. Being in Column of Threes (or Fours), to Change Direction. The command is: 1. Column right (left), 2. MARCH. At the command MARCH, given as the right foot strikes the ground, the right flank man of the leading rank faces to the right in marching and takes the half step until the other men of his rank are abreast of
