4 PACES "OFFICERS CENTER" Plate 54. Battalion Ceremonial Parade; Companies in Line 

362. The company commanders in succession from the right salute and report, "A (or other) company, present or accounted for"; or, "A (or other) company, (so many) officers or men absent." The adjutant returns each company commander's

SPECIAL CEREMONIAL PARADES 

53 salute after the report is made and understood. He returns to the carry after acknowledging each salute. The reports received, the adjutant turns about, salutes, and reports, "Sir, all present or accounted for"; or "Sir, (so many) officers or men absent," including among the absentees those from the band and field music reported to him by the band leader prior to the parade. 

363. The battalion commander then directs, "Publish the orders, Sir." The adjutant turns about and commands: ATTENTION TO ORDERS. He publishes the orders and then commands: 1. Officers, 2. Center, 3. MARCH, turns about and takes his post with the battalion commander. 

364. At the command officers, all company officers execute carry saber, guidon bearers execute carry guidon. When companies are in line, at the command center, all company commanders, officers commanding platoons, and guidon bearers face to the center. When companies are in mass formation, at the command center, all company commanders and guidon bearers face to the center; officers commanding platoons step one pace forward and face to the center; officers second in command move around the flank nearest the center and take post in the column of platoon leaders. At the command MARCH, the band plays, officers and guidon bearers close to the center, halt, and individually face to the front; company commanders when moving to the center, oblique to the front and close on a line four paces in advance of the line of guidon bearers; guidon bearers close on their own line, each taking post in rear of his own company commander; all other officers close on the line of platoon leaders. [54] 

365. The officers and guidon bearers having closed and faced to the front, the senior commands: 1. Forward, 2. MARCH. The officers and guidon bearers advance, the center officer of the leading rank being the guide, and march on the battalion commander. They are halted by the senior with the leading rank 6 paces from the battalion commander. They salute the battalion commander, who returns the salute. The battalion commander then commands: 1. Carry. 2. SABER. At the first command, the guidon bearers grasp the lance with the left hand, executing the first position of carry guidon, and officers execute order saber. At the second command, the officers execute carry saber, the guidon bearers completing the carry guidon. The battalion commander then gives such instructions as he deems neces sary, and commands: 1. Officers, 2. Posts, 3. MARCH. 

366. At the command posts, all officers and guidon bearers face about. At the command MARCH, they step off with the guide as before, the center officer of the leading rank being the guide. The senior commands: 1. Officers, 2. HALT, so as to halt the leading rank 6 paces from the line of companies when companies are in line and 3 paces when companies are in mass formation. He then commands: 

1. Posts, 2. MARCH. 

367. At the command posts, officers and guidon bearers face outward, and at the command MARCH, officers step off in succession at four paces distance, resume their posts, and execute order saber; guidon bearers step off with their company commanders, resume their posts, and execute order guidon. The music ceases to play when the last officer has resumed his post. During the execution of officers, center and officers, posts, all officers and guidon bearers remain at carry saber and carry guidon except when saluting the battalion commander. 

368. The battalion commander then gives the commands for the battalion to pass in review, and returns saber. The battalion passes in review according to the commands and principles given for a review. When the last company has passed, the ceremony is concluded. The band continues to play while the companies are in march upon the parade ground. After passing in review, companies are marched to their respective parades and dismissed. 

369. Regimental Parade. The regiment is ordinarily formed in line of companies with companies in mass formation. The parade proceeds as for the battalion with the following exceptions: 

(1) "Regimental commander" is substituted for "battalion commander," and "regiment" for "battalion" in the description; and "battalions" for "battalion" in the commands. (2) In moving across the front of the regiment, the band passes midway between the adjutant and the line of battalion commanders. (3) The battalions execute present arms, order arms, parade rest, and come to attention at the command of execution of their respective commanders, starting with the center (or right center) battalion and continuing simultaneously toward each flank. Reports are made by battalion instead of company commanders. (4) After making his report, the adjutant commands: 1. Officers, 2. Center, 3. MARCH. Battalion commanders and their staffs close on the line of battalion commanders, company commanders oblique to the front and close on a line 4 paces in their rear, guidon bearers oblique to the front and close on a line 4 paces in rear of the company commanders, other officers oblique to the front and close on a line 4 paces in rear of the guidon bearers. (5) The officers and guidon bearers are returned to their posts and the regiment marches in review as in battalion parade.

SECTION IV INSPECTIONS 

370. The company is the basic unit for inspection. Battalion, regimental and higher commanders or inspecting officers inspect each company in its own area or have each company march to a designated place at a specified time. Under special conditions an entire battalion or regiment may be formed and inspected in one large formation. 

371. Company Inspection. The company forms in line or in an inspection formation of column of platoons (Par. 376.) If transportation is included in the formation it forms in line 3 paces in rear or as directed. Drivers of motor vehicles remain with their vehicles. Drivers and mule leaders of animal transportation stay with their animals. Drivers or animal leaders lay out their equipment as directed. 

Transportation may be inspected separately from foot elements. 

372. The company being in column of platoons the company commander com mands: PREpare for inSPECTION. At this command, platoon leaders cause ranks to be opened. They then place themselves, facing to the front, three paces in advance of the right flank of their platoons. Ranks having been opened, the company commander commands: REST, and returns saber. He then inspects the company. During the inspection, officers, noncommissioned officers, and guidon bearers not in ranks come to attention at order arms as the inspecting officer approaches, and after being inspected resume the position of rest. The company commander may direct the second in command and/or the first sergeant to join him and take notes as he inspects. Commencing at the head of the column, he then makes a minute inspection of the arms, equipment, dress, and appearance of the personnel of the company. As he approaches each platoon, its leader brings it to attention and salutes. As soon as inspected, the platoon leader returns saber, places himself on the right of the company commander, and accompanies him throughout the inspection of the platoon. The inspection is made from right to left in front and from left to right in rear of each rank. 

373. Each man executes inspection arms as the company commander or inspecting officer reaches his position. The company commander takes the rifle, grasping it with the right hand just above the lower band, the man dropping his hands. He inspects the rifle and, with the hand and rifle in the same position as in receiving it, hands it back to the man, who takes it with the left hand at the balance and executes order arms. Should the piece be inspected without handling, each man executes order arms as soon as the company commander has passed to the next man. A man armed with the automatic rifle executes inspection arms as the company commander reaches his position. The inspection completed, he pulls the trigger, replaces the magazine, and resumes the position of the soldier, the automatic rifle being slung. 

Enlisted men armed with the pistol execute inspection pistol. When the company commander has passed, they execute return pistol. 

374. Upon completion of the inspection of each platoon, its leader takes his post, faces down the line, and commands: 1. Close ranks, 2. MARCH, and adds REST, after ranks have been closed. He then takes his post in front of the center of the platoon. 

375. The company commander may direct the platoon leaders to make the detailed inspection of arms or other equipment of the men of their platoons. 

376. Inspection of Personal Field Equipment while in Ranks. The company forms in column of platoons (with each platoon in line) by forming extended mass formation to the right at 24 paces [34-3] and then executing left face. The company commander, after the inspection of arms has been completed causes each platoon to take interval and prepare for inspection of equipment. Intervals having been taken the platoon leader commands: 1. Unsling equipment, 2 DISPLAY EQUIP- 

MENT. At the command unsling equipment, each man draws his bayonet and with his left hand thrusts it into the ground, ring to the front, the bayonet outside of and against his left heel near the instep to mark the line for the rear edge of equipment when displayed. Men not armed with the rifle mark the place with the left heel. Each man then lays his rifle on the ground, muzzle to the front, barrel to the left, butt near the toe of his right foot, unslings his equipment and places it on the ground at his feet, haversack to the front, the pack one foot in front of his toes At the command display equipment, packs are opened and equipment displayed. Equipment is displayed in the interval to the left of each man. When equipment is displayed, each man resumes his original position in ranks. 

377. The company commander then passes along the ranks as before, inspects the equipment, and directs the platoon leader to have packs rolled and the platoon assembled. The platoon leader then commands: ROLL PACKS. Each man assembles his equipment and, leaving the equipment in its position on the ground at his feet, resumes the position of attention. All equipment being assembled, the platoon leader commands: SLING EQUIPMENT. Packs are slung and belts fastened and rifles taken. The platoon leader then causes the platoon to assemble. The inspection is completed as already explained.

INSPECTIONS 

55 378. In units which have special combat equipment such as machine guns, mortars, signal or command post equipment, the company commander, after packs have been opened (or after the individual inspection has been completed), directs: 

Lay out machine gun (mortar or other) equipment for inspection. Gun squads under the direction of their leaders break ranks and lay out their weapons and accessories for inspection as prescribed in the gun drill for the weapon. Headquarters personnel lay out the fire-control, communication, or other combat equipment. The gun or equipment is laid out in rear of its vehicle. If no transportation other than that drawn by hand is present, the equipment is displayed 3 paces from the flank of each squad on the side from which interval was taken The rear of the equipment is placed on line with the rear edge of the individual field equipment. 

379. Battalion Inspection. The battalion is formed in column of companies, each company as prescribed for the company. Any other convenient formation may be used. Before the inspection, the battalion commander indicates whether heavy weapons and special equipment are to be laid out for inspection or left on their transportation. The headquarters, rifle, and weapons units are prepared and inspected in the manner prescribed for the company. 

380. The battalion being in column of platoon, with all personnel dismounted, the battalion commander commands: PREPARE FOR INSPECTION. At this command each company and the band, if present, are prepared for inspection. Buglers rejoin their companies. The color bearer and the color guard proceed to the head of the column and take position three paces in rear of the staff. The battalion commander then commands: REST, returns saber, and inspects his staff and the color guard. As he approaches the staff, the officers, without command, come to attention at order saber. When they have been inspected, they return saber and accompany the battalion commander. As the battalion commander approaches the color guard, the color bearer commands: 1. Color guard, 2. ATTENTION. The color guard may be dismissed as soon as inspected. 

381. The battalion commander commencing at the head of the column makes an inspection of the arms, accouterments, dress, and appearance of the personnel of the band and the companies. When the inspection of the band has been completed, it may be dismissed or it may take position at the rear of the column and play during the inspection. 

382. As the battalion commander approaches each company, its commander faces toward it and commands: 1. Company, 2. ATTENTION, faces to the front, and salutes. As soon as he has been inspected, the company commander faces about and commands: REST, returns saber, and accompanies the battalion commander. 

The inspection proceeds as prescribed for company inspection. 

383. The battalion commander may direct the company commanders to make the detailed inspection of the arms or other equipment of their companies. He may require officers of his staff to assist in the inspection, especially by checking equipment. When a company has been inspected, the battalion commander directs that it be dismissed or otherwise occupied. When desired, the battalion commander may cause companies not under inspection to stack arms, fall out, and resume their places in time to be inspected. 

384. If the inspecting officer is other than the battalion commander, the latter prepares the battalion for inspection as prescribed in the preceding paragraphs. 

Upon the approach of the inspecting officer, the battalion commander brings the battalion to attention, faces to the front, and salutes. The inspecting officer inspects the battalion commander who then commands: REST, returns saber, and accompanies the inspecting officer. The inspection then proceeds as previously prescribed.

SECTION V FUNERALS 

385. Classes. A military funeral may include: (1) Chapel (church or home) service, march to the grave (crematory or place of shipment or disposition) with prescribed escort. (2) Funeral procession from at or near the entrance to the cemetery, and graveside service. (3) Graveside service only. A full military escort usually includes: Band; prescribed escort with firing party and bugler (A R 600-30); colors; clergy; caisson (hearse) and casket bearers; caparisoned horse (for mounted personnel); honorary pallbearers. 

386. Clergy and Pallbearers. The services may be conducted by an army chaplain, a civilian clergyman, or both. Honorary pallbearers may be selected by the family of the deceased, or by the commanding officer. 

387. Conduct of Personnel. All persons in military service attending a military funeral, in uniform or civilian clothes, stand at attention, with headdress held over the heart, whenever the casket is being moved, and during the service at the grave. 

During prayers they will also bow their heads. During inclement weather they

may remain covered, and will execute hand salute whenever the casket is moved, and during the firing of volleys and sounding of taps. 

388. Honors. Whenever arms are presented at the funeral of a person entitled to honors, the prescribed ruffles and flourishes will be sounded, followed by the National Anthem, or "To the Color," or the march appropriate to the rank of the deceased, except where arms are presented after the benediction at the grave. 

389. Chapel Service. Prior to the beginning of the service, members of the family, friends and relatives of the deceased, enter the chapel and take seats. The escort, in line, faces the chapel entrance, band on the flank toward which it will march. The conveyance bearing the remains having arrived, the commander of the escort brings it to attention and commands: 1. Present, 2. ARMS. The band plays an appropriate air, the remains are removed from the conveyance by the casket bearers, and borne into the chapel. The band then ceases playing, and the escort is brought to order arms, and at ease. 

390. Honorary pallbearers, if present, in two ranks, facing each other, form an aisle from the conveyance to the chapel entrance. They follow the casket in column of twos, and take seats at the left front of the chapel. When the casket is placed on the chapel truck the casket bearers form behind the honorary pall bearers, if present, and take seats behind them. 

391. At the conclusion of the chapel service the honorary pallbearers, in column of twos, follow the chaplain to the entrance, and again form an aisle from the entrance to the conveyance of the remains. When the remains have been placed on the conveyance they form columns of files on each side, the leading men opposite the front wheels of the vehicle. If riding, they enter their own conveyances at this time. 

392. The casket bearers follow the honorary pallbearers to the entrance, and when the pallbearers are in position they carry the casket to its conveyance, and then form in column of twos behind it. 

393. The procedure of the escort and band is the same while the casket is being borne out of the chapel as when it is borne in. The band again ceases playing when the casket is placed on its conveyance. 

394. The family group follows the casket to the chapel entrance, remains there until the honorary pallbearers have taken their positions or start to enter their conveyances, and are then conducted to their own conveyances. 

395. Procession to the Grave. The procession forms in the following_order: 

(1) Band. (2) Escort, including colors, firing party, bugler. (3) Clergy. (4) Caisson or other conveyance of remains, and honorary pallbearers, if walking. (5) Casket bearers. (6) Caparisoned horse, if present. (7) Honorary pallbearers, if_riding. (8) Family of deceased. (9) Patriotic or fraternal organizations. (10) Friends.

When the procession has formed the commander of the escort puts it in motion. 

It marches slowly, to solemn music. If the distance is great, part of the march may be at ease. 

396. On arrival at the grave the band and escort form line on one side, as designated, facing the grave. Other marching elements are placed in designated positions, as near as practicable. The clergy, family and others, are on the opposite side of the grave. The firing squad takes post, usually at the end of the grave, where they will not fire over the heads of the mourners. 

397. When the caisson is halted the pallbearers form an aisle from it toward the grave. The casket bearers remove the casket, bear it to the grave, and place it on the lowering device. They remain facing the casket. They raise the flag from the casket, hold it waist high until the conclusion of "Taps," and then fold it and deliver it to the superintendent of the cemeterv. 

398. As soon as the casket, preceded by the clergy and funeral director, has passed, the honorary pallbearers follow it to the grave, being followed in turn by the family and friends. The pallbearers form line in a convenient position, facing the grave.

As the casket is removed from the conveyance the commander of the escort commands: 1. Present, 2. ARMS. The band plays. When the casket has been deposited near the grave the escort is brought to order arms, and parade rest. 

399. The graveside service then proceeds and the remains are lowered. After the benediction the chaplain steps back or aside. The commander of the escort calls it to attention, and commands: 1. Escort, less firing party, 2. Present, 3. ARMS. 

4. Firing party, 5. FIRE THREE VOLLEYS. The firing party, at the commands of its leader, fires 3 volleys of blank cartridges, takes the position of ready, and remains so while taps is played. The bugler takes position at the head of the grave and sounds taps immediately after the last volley. Rifles of the firing party are then locked. 

400. The escort is then brought to order arms. The band and escort march in quick time from the cemetery. Other elements conform. The band does not play. At the first halt, rifles of the firing party are unloaded and inspected. 

401. Procession and Graveside Service. The officer in charge superintends the transfer of the casket from the hearse to the caisson.

Honorary pallbearers, if

FUNERALS. GUARD MOUNTING 

57 present, form line facing the caisson, on the side opposite the hearse. While the casket is being transferred the escort is at present arms, and the band plays. The procession is then formed and the ceremony proceeds as heretofore described. 

402. Graveside Service. This service may include all the elements of a full military funeral, but if troops are not available or not desired, the following will suffice: clergy, casket bearers, firing party, bugler. All military elements will take positions at the grave prior to the arrival of the remains. 

403. Cremated Remains. When cremated remains are interred the foregoing instructions, with necessary modifications, are applicable. One casket bearer carries the receptacle containing the ashes. Four others are designated as flag bearers. They follow the bearer of the receptacle in column of twos, the leading right hand man carrying the folded flag. When the receptacle is placed on a stand at the chapel or on the caisson, the folded flag is placed beside it. If the caisson is equipped with a casket container the open flag will be laid over the container as over a casket. 

404. When remains are borne to a crematory to be interred later with military honors, the ceremony will consist only of escort to the crematory. Arms are presented as the remains are borne into the crematory, but firing and sounding of taps are omitted. If the funeral ceremony is conducted at the crematory and no further honors are expected, the volley may be fired and taps sounded. 

405. Shipment of Remains. When the remains of a deceased officer or soldier are taken to a railway station or other point for shipment, the funeral services will be conducted generally as heretofore prescribed, with such modifications as necessary. If no further military honors are expected the volleys may be fired and taps sounded. 

406. Cannon Salute. When the funeral of an officer entitled to a cannon salute takes place at or near a military post, minute guns will be fired while the remains are borne to the place of interment or other disposition, the first gun as soon as the procession is put in motion. After the benediction at the grave a salute appropriate to the rank of the deceased is fired, followed by three salvos of artillery or three volleys of rifle fire.

SECTION VI FORMAL AND INFORMAL GUARD MOUNTING 

407. Guard mounting may be formal or informal. The commanding officer prescribes the uniform, arms and equipment for guard duty. 

408. Formal Guard Mounting. At the command of the first sergeant, men going on guard fall in on the company parade, as prescribed for the squad, noncommissioned officers on the right. The first sergeant verifies and inspects the detail, replaces men unfit for the duty, and turns the detail over to its senior noncommissioned officer, or to one designated to march the detail on guard. At the sounding of assembly the detail is reported to the senior noncommissioned officer of the guard at a designated place off the parade ground. Here the details are formed into a platoon. [55] Noncommissioned officers to be assigned to posts (Par. 413) are formed as the rear rank in inverse order of rank from right to left. Other noncommissioned officers, if any, are placed on the left to equalize the ranks. The platoon is then faced to the right. 

409. The band takes post with the left of its front rank on line with and 12 paces to the right of the point where the right of the front rank of the guard is to form. 

At the adjutant's signal adjutant's call is sounded, followed by march music. The adjutant, with the sergeant major on his left, at the first note of the music, marches from a position opposite the center of the guard when formed. He halts 18 paces in front of and facing the point where the center of the guard will form. The sergeant major continues on for 18 paces, moves by the left flank, halts at the position where the right of the front rank is to form, and faces about. The guard detail, at the first note of the music, marches in column in quick time to the place where it is to form. The noncommissioned officer in command is 3 paces to the left of the center man on the left column of files. The detail approaches from the left, and is halted a little in rear of the line on which it is to form, when the head of the column is opposite the sergeant major. The band then ceases playing. 

410. The commander of the detail, remaining at right shoulder arms (if armed with the rifle), faces the detail and commands: 1. Order, 2. ARMS, 3. Left, 4. FACE. He then faces right in marching, and proceeds to a position one pace to the left front of the left file of the front rank, faces about and places himself on line with the sergeant major and the front rank of the band. He then commands: 1. Dress right, 2. DRESS. The right flank man of the front rank places the middle of his chest against the left arm of the sergeant major, and the detail dresses to the line thus established. The commander then commands: 1. Ready, 2. FRONT, salutes and re ports, "The guard is correct," or "(So many) privates (corporals) absent." The sergeant major returns the salute. The commander then marches parallel to the front of the detail 2 paces beyond the seregant major, executes by the right flank, clears

the rear rank of the detail, again executes by the right flank, marches to a point one pace beyond the left man of the rear rank, again executes by the right flank, halts on line with the rear rank, and comes to order arms. 

411. When the guard has been reported the sergeant major faces right, moves 2 paces to the front, faces left, and commands: COUNT OFF. If there be more than 

14 men per rank he divides the guard into 2 platoons, leaving his post, proceeding to and designating the dividing line between platoons. From this position he then commands: 1. Second platoon, 2. Left step, 3. MARCH, 4. Platoon, 5. HALT, creating an interval of 5 paces between platoons. He then passes completely around the guard by its left and rear, executing by the left flank at each turn, and takes post 3 paces in front and 2 paces to the right of the front rank, facing left. 

412. The sergeant major then commands: 1. Open ranks, 2. MARCH, and aligns the guard as prescribed for a platoon. He then moves parallel to the front rank until opposite the center, executes by the right flank, halts 6 paces in front of the adjutant, salutes and reports, "Sir, the guard is correct," or "(So many) sergeants, corporals, etc. absent." The adjutant returns the salute, orders, "Take your post,' and draws saber. The sergeant major faces about, moves by the right oblique to a point on line with and 3 paces to the left of the front rank, halts and faces the front. 18 P -000 6 P. 12 P. 

BAND Plate 55. Formal Guard Mounting 

As the sergeant major leaves his post to report to the adjutant, the officer of the guard takes post 6 paces in rear of the right rear rank man, with the junior officer of the guard (if any) on his left. When the sergeant major has reported to the adjutant the officer of the guard passes around its right flank and takes post facing the front, 12 paces in front of the center. The junior officer of the guard (if any), moves with the senior and takes post facing the front, 6 paces in front of the center of the first platoon. If there is but one platoon the senior officer takes post 6 paces in front of the center, with the junior 3 paces to his left. Both officers then draw saber and come to order saber. 

413. The adjutant then commands: 1. Officer (Officers) and noncommissioned officers, 2. Front and center, 3. MARCH. At the command center the officer (officers) execute carry saber. The noncommissioned officers in the rear rank face right and come to right shoulder arms. At the command MARCH, the officers (if any) advance, the junior closing in to position on the left of the senior, and halt 3 paces in front of the adjutant, remaining at carry saber. The noncommissioned officers, in column, pass around the right of the guard, twice execute column left, form column parallel to the front and 6 paces in front of the adjutant, halt and face front at the command of the senior, remaining at the right shoulder. The adjutant, at carry saber, then passes along the fronts of the officers and noncommissioned officers, and assigns them to posts according to rank, thus: "Commander of the guard; leader of the 1st platoon, leader of the 2nd platoon, guide of the 1st platoon, guide of the 2nd platoon, right file rear rank 1st platoon, right file 2nd rank 1st platoon, right file front rank 1st platoon.' If the guard is not divided into platoons the assignments are: "Commander of the guard, junior officer of the guard, Guide, right file rear rank, etc." A 

---

GUARD MOUNTING 

59 noncommissioned officer designated as commander of the guard takes post as prescribed for an officer. 

414. The adjutant returns to his post and commands: 1. Officer (Officers) and noncommissioned officers, 2. Posts, 3. MARCH. At the command posts, all face about. At the command MARCH each proceeds by the most direct route to the post assigned him, and halts. Noncommissioned officers execute order arms and about face at the command of the guide. If the guard is not divided the junior officer takes post on the left of the rear rank. The adjutant then orders the commander of the guard: "Inspect your guard, Sir," and executes return saber. The commander of the guard faces about, commands: Prepare for INSPECTION, and executes return saber. 

The guard is inspected as prescribed in Section IV, Chapter IX, Infantry Drill Regu lations. The junior officer may be directed to assist in the inspection. During the inspection the band plays. The adjutant makes a general inspection, causes men who for any reason are unfit for duty to fall out, selects the necessary orderlies and color sentinels. Substitutes for men removed report to the guardhouse. If there is no officer of the guard the adjutant makes the full inspection. 

415. The inspection being completed, the adjutant takes post facing the guard, 36 paces in front of its center, and draws saber. The new officer of the day takes post opposite the center, 36 paces in rear of the adjutant, the old officer of the day 3 paces to his right and 1 pace in rear. They stand at attention without drawing saber. The commander of the guard takes his post in front of the guard, and (if an officer) draws saber and executes order saber with the adjutant. The adjutant then commands: 

1. Parade, 2. REST; and SOUND OFF, and executes parade rest. At the command, the band sounds off in place, and then moves forward in quick time, playing, passes down the front of the guard between the adjutant and the officers of the guard, and back to its post, where is again sounds off. The adjutant then comes to attention, executes carry saber, and commands: 1. Guard, 2. ATTENTION, 3. Close ranks, 4. MARCH. Platoon leaders do not repeat these commands. The adjutant then commands: 1. Present, 2. ARMS, faces the new officer of the day, salutes and reports, "Sir, the guard is formed." The new officer of the day returns the salute, and orders, “March the guard in review, Sir," or "March the guard to its post, Sir." 

416. The adjutant executes carry saber, faces about and brings the guard to order arms, and commands: 1. Pass in review, 2. Right, 3. FACE. As the guard faces right the band turns to the right and places itself with its rear rank 24 paces in front of the guard. The adjutant takes post on line with and 3 paces to the left of the commander of the guard. The sergeant major takes post on line with and 3 paces to the left of the rear file of the left column. The adjutant then commands: 1. Right shoulder, 2. ARMS, 3. Forward, 4. MARCH. The band and guard march at quick time past the officers of the day, saluting, as prescribed for reviews. The guide or leading guide maintains the distance of 24 paces from the rear of the band. The adjutant and the commander of the guard salute together, platoon leaders and the drum major salute with their units, the sergeant major with the unit with which he is marching. Having passed the officers of the day the band turns left out of column, takes position opposite and facing them, 18 paces from the left flank of the guard, and continues to play until the guard has left the parade ground. The field music detaches itself from the band as the latter leaves the column, remains in front of the guard, and begins to play when the band ceases. Having passed 25 paces beyond the officers of the day, the adjutant halts. The sergeant major halts one pace to his left. The adjutant executes return saber, he and the sergeant major face each other, salute and retire. The commander of the guard conducts it to its post. While the band is sounding off and during the march in review the officers of the day continue at attention. The new officer of the day returns the simultaneous salutes of the adjutant and commander of the guard. At the conclusion of the review the officers of the day face each other and salute. 

417. If the officer of the day commands that the guard be marched to its post, the adjutant repeats the command. The commander of the guard marches the guard to its post by the prescribed route, and the band and field music are dismissed. 

418. Informal Guard Mounting. Informal guard mounting is held on the parade ground of the organization from which the guard is detailed; or if there are several details, at such place as the commanding officer may direct. The details are formed and inspected as heretofore prescribed, and reported to the commander of the guard at the place designated. 

419. The commander of the guard forms it as a platoon (whatever its size) as heretofore prescribed, and takes post 6 paces in front of the center, facing the front. 

The officer of the day takes post 18 paces in front of the center, facing the guard, draws sober, and commands: 1. Officer(s) and noncommissioned officers, 2. Front and center, 3. MARCH. The officers and noncommissioned officers execute front and center, and are assigned to their posts as prescribed for formal guard mounting. 

The officer of the day then commands: PREPARE for inspecTION. The commander of the guard faces about and commands: 1. Open ranks, 2. MARCH. He proceeds to the right flank, verifies the alignment, commands: 1. Ready, 2. FRONT, 3. PREPARE FOR INSEPCTION, and takes post 3 paces in front of the right guide,

facing the front, at carry saber. The officer of the day inspects the guard, removes men unfit for duty, and selects orderlies and color sentinels. The officer of the day, after guard mounting, reports to the adjutant any replacements needed. The inspection being completed the officer of the day resumes his post, and directs the commander of the guard to march the guard to its post. Ranks are closed, and the guard marches without music to its post. If the guard is commanded by a noncommissioned officer he takes post as prescribed for an officer, except that in marching front and center he takes post on the right of the line of noncommissioned officers. 

The officer of the day gives the commands for opening and closing ranks, and verifies the alignment. 

420. At formal guard mounting the bugler of the new guard marches with the field music to the guardhouse. At informal guard mounting he forms abreast of and on the left of the front rank. At the guardhouse he relieves the old bugler, and with him reports to the new officer of the day. 

421. March of the New Guard to the Guardhouse. As the new guard approaches the guardhouse (post of the guard), the old guard is formed in line with its field music 3 paces to the right. When the field music at the head of the new guard arrives opposite the last of the old guard, the commander of the new guard commands: 

1. Eyes, 2. RIGHT, and the commander of the old guard commands: 1. Present, 2. ARMS. The new guard marches past the front of the old guard, and the commanders exchange salutes. When the commander of the new guard is opposite the field music of the old guard he commands: 1. Ready, 2. FRONT. When the new guard has cleared his front the commander of the old guard commands: 1. Order, 2. ARMS. When the field music of the new guard is 3 paces beyond that of the old guard, it changes direction to the right and again to the left when on line with the old guard. These changes of direction are made without command, the guard following the field music. The commander of the new guard halts on the line of the old guard, allows his guard to march past him, halts it 3 paces to the right of the field music of the old guard, forms line and dresses his guard to the left. The field music of the new guard halts on line with it and 3 paces to its right. 

422. Presenting the Old and New Guards. The commander of each guard, in front of and facing its center, commands: 1. Present, 2. ARMS, faces the front, salutes the other guard, executes carry saber, faces about and commands: 1. Order, 2. ARMS. 

If a guard is commanded by a noncommissioned officer he proceeds as prescribed for an officer, rendering the proper salutes. 

423. Presenting the Guards to the Officers of the Day. The guards having exchanged salutes, each is presented to its officer of the day. If but one officer of the day is present both guards are presented to him. Each is then brought to order arms. 

424. Dispositions of the New Guard. The commander of the new guard now causes bayonets to be fixed, if so ordered by the commanding officer. Bayonets remain fixed during the hour of duty unless otherwise ordered, except when the guard is marching. The commander directs men detailed for detatched posts to fall out, and places them under charge of a noncommissioned officer. He divides the guard into reliefs. 

425. Salutes by the Guard. If persons entitled to salutes approach while the old and new guard are in formation, each commander brings his own guard to attention, if not already so, and commands: 1. Old (New) guard, 2. Present, 3. ARMS. When the salute has been acknowledged the guard is brought to order arms. 

426. Relief of the Old Guard. The sentinels and detachments of the old guard are promptly relieved by the new guard, the two guards standing at rest or at ease while this is done. The commander of the old guard transmits to the commander of the new guard all of his instructions. The commander of the new guard then takes possession of the guardhouse, and verifies the prisoners and property charged to the guard. 

427. Dismissing the Old Guard. If considerable time is required to relieve members of the old guard who are on post, the commanding officer may direct that, as soon as the instructions and property are turned over, that portion of the old guard at the guardhouse be marched off and dismissed. Remaining detachments are inspected by the commander of the new guard as they arrive at the guardhouse. He then directs the senior noncommissioned officer with them to march off the detachments and dismiss them.

CHAPTER X. EXTENDED ORDER SECTION I 428. General Rules for Extended Order Drill. Extended order movements are habitually executed at ease. They should be held on ground affording concealment and cover. When such terrain is available within reasonable distance, only such preliminary training is given on flat or bare drill grounds as necessary to teach the mechanism of deployment and illustrate the dispositions and movements. 

429. Straight lines are avoided. The dispositions, distances and intervals given should be modified according to the existing terrain, enemy fire, and space available. 

The usual distance or interval between men in extended order is 5 paces, as indicated in the descriptions and illustrations. These distances and intervals are variable and leaders will order distribution, distances, and intervals suited to the situation. 

430. The commander prescribes the distribution of his unit and may initially prescribe the dispositions of subordinate units. Thereafter during the advance, subordinate leaders vary their dispositions to make best use of ground and cover. 

431. Platoon, section, and squad leaders deploy, assemble, and maneuver their units as far as practicable by arm signals. Whistle signals are sparingly used. In general, use of the whistle is limited to occasions when it is impossible to attract attention by other means. Its habitual use as a preliminary to a command is prohibited. 

432. A deployed unit advances, halts, moves to the flank or to the rear, passes from a walk to a run and the converse, at appropriate signals or commands. 

Changes in the direction of march are usually effected by the assignment of a new march direction to the base squad. Movements may be interrupted by the signal or command HALT or DOWN. On halting, a deployed line faces to the front (direction of the enemy). If halted by the command DOWN, the men take cover. 

Deployment as skirmishers is made at a run. Other movements are made at a walk unless otherwise ordered.

433. Individual Instruction. The soldier is taught how to set the sight of his rifle, to fire, to lie down, to crawl, to make the best use of ground and cover, and the duties of a scout prior to or concurrently with the instruction in battlefield formations and movements. (1) To set the sight. The unit or group being in any firing formation, the command is: RANGE, SIX hundred or BATTLE SIGHT.

63 (2) To commence firing. Being in any firing formation, the command is: 

COMMENCE FIRING. Each man independently, aims carefully and deliberately at the aiming point or target, fires, reloads, and continues firing until ordered to cease firing. (3) To increase (decrease) the rate of fire. The instructor commands or signals: FIRE faster (SLOWER). 

(4) Use of the bayonet. The bayonet is fixed at any time when so ordered. 

If marching, kneeling, or prone, it is fixed and unfixed in the most expeditious and convenient manner, and the rifle returned to the original position. (5) Prone position. Being at a halt, or moving, to lie down, the command is: DOWN. All drop to the prone position flat on the belly. (6) High port. When men are moving, they carry their rifles at a high port or in other convenient manner. High port is a modification of the position of port arms, with the rifle held somewhat higher, and farther from the body. In this position it is ready for instant use. г-х

SECTION II RIFLE SQUAD 

434. Distances and Intervals Between Men when Deployed. Five paces between men is the usual interval, but is variable. The squad leader may direct lesser or

greater intervals and distances and individual skirmishers vary their positions to take full advantage of cover or concealment. Close grouping in the open is avoided, but two or three men may group together to take advantage of any good cover. 

435. Dispositions. Extended order dispositions of the rifle squad are: (1) Squad column. (2) As skirmishers. (3) As skirmishers right (left). (4) Squad wedge. (1) Squad column. [56] At the command, given when the squad is disposed in any manner, its members form in an irregular column behind the squad leader. The disposition of individuals in the column will be adapted to the situation, and may vary from a widely spaced and staggered column suitable for crossing very open country to a column of files closed up behind the leader. (2) As Skirmishers. [57] At the signal or command, usually given from squad column but permissible from any disposition, the squad deploys in an irregular line. Even numbers go to the right, odd numbers to the left. The squad leader is in front of his squad when it is advancing. When it halts to fire, he drops behind the line to a position from which he can control his squad and observe to the front. If there is an automatic rifleman in the squad, he joins the squad leader and waits orders for any special fire mission. (3) As skirmishers right (left). [58] At the signal or command, usually given from squad column but permissible from any disposition, the squad deploys to the right. (4) Squad wedge. [59] At the command, usually given from squad column but permissible from any disposition, the men dispose themselves in an irregular

436. Advance by Individuals. To advance by individuals the squad leader indicates the objective, the man or men who are to start the movement, and commands or signals: FORWARD. Each man chooses the time and method for his own advance so as to take advantage of cover and avoid enemy fire. If the objective is not indicated, the leading men select the next cover or firing position. They do not advance so far as to lose contact with their squad leader. The squad leader follows the first three or four men, directing their advance if necessary. The second in command sees that the others follow, and is usually the last to go forward. 

437. Rushes. To move the entire squad simultaneously from cover to cover, the leader commands:1. Prepare to rush, 2. FOLLOW ME. All men spring forward, following the example of the leader. If necessary for safety, the squad leader may first command: CEASE FIRING. The rush terminates when the squad has closed with the enemy or when the leader commands or signals DOWN. The rush may be executed by the entire squad as a unit, or by any fraction or individuals

EXTENDED ORDER. RIFLE PLATOON 

65 thereof. In the latter case the leader designates the part of the squad, or the individuals, who are to make the rush, and the next position. 

438. To Follow the Squad Leader. The leader places himself in front of his unit and commands: FOLLOW ME. The other members of the squad follow him, retaining the approximate disposition of the unit at the time the command was given. 

439. To Assemble the Squad. At the signal or command the squad assembles on the leader, in column with 40 inches distance between men. 

440. Scouts. In each rifle squad, Nos. 2 and 3 are designated as scouts and receive special training. When advancing in the presence of the enemy, the squad (platoon or company) is preceded by its scouts, who seek out the enemy and prevent surprise. Scouts precede the squad at such distance that it will not be subjected to surprise small arms fire. To cause the scouts to precede their unit, the command is: SCOUTS OUT. The scouts move forward in front of their units and maintain contact with them.

SECTION III RIFLE PLATOON 

441. Direction of Deployment. The platoon is trained to deploy in any direction in silence and without confusion. The direction is indicated by the leader pointing or moving in the desired direction. 

442. Direction of Advance. The direction of advance may be indicated by the leader pointing or moving in that direction; by designating prominent terrain features as buildings or lone trees; by compass azimuth; by indicating to the leader of the base unit the route to be followed; or by any combination of the above. Where an advance is to take place in an assigned direction for a considerable time, both a distant direction point and a compass azimuth should be designated. 

443. The Base Squad. The center squad is the base or guiding squad, except that: (1) When in platoon column, the leading squad is the base squad. (2) When deployed in squad columns or as skirmishers with one squad back, the right flank squad is the base squad unless otherwise indicated. The other squads of the platoon maintain direction and relative positions by regulating on the base squad. 

444. Dispositions. Extended order dispositions of the platoon are: (1) Column of threes. (2) Column of twos. (3) Platoon column. [60-1] (4) Line of squads. 

[60-2] (5) Two squads forward, one back. [60-3] (6) One squad forward, two back.

Any squad may be in squad column, squad wedge, or as skirmishers. 

445. Frontage. The platoon deploys with sufficient intervals between squads (1 2 SQUAD SQUAD SQUAD SQUAD O SQUAD 凶​, SQUAD OSQUAD SQUAD SQUAD 

Plate 60. Extended Order Dispositions of the Rifle Platoon Figure 1. Platoon column. Figure 2. Line of Squads. Figure 3. Center squad back. 

to permit minor maneuvers. Unless otherwise directed, squad columns or wedges keep about 50 yards apart. When deployed as skirmishers, adjacent squads deploy with about 20 yards interval between squads unless otherwise directed. 

446. Posts of Platoon Leader and Second in Command. The platoon leader and second in command are not restricted to fixed posts. When advancing, the platoon leader usually precedes his unit, the second in command follows in rear and assists in control. During forward or flank movements, the platoon leader is on the side of the platoon toward the enemy. During movements to the rear, he is on the side away from the enemy. When the platoon is halted in a firing position, he is in rear of his platoon. When otherwise halted, he is in front. The second in command takes positions from which he can best assist the platoon leader in controlling the platoon, and watch for signals from the company.

447. Platoon Column. At the command, the center squad moves out in squad column, followed in order by the right and left squads, or as indicated by the platoon leader. 

448. To Deploy into Squad Columns. (1) Squad columns in line. The command is: SQUAD COLUMNS. The center or leading squad moves in squad column to the front or in the direction indicated. The other two squads move in squad columns to positions approximately 50 paces to its right and left. Leaders of the flank squads guide on the base squad but make no effort to maintain exact alignment. (2) Squad columns with one squad forward. At the command SQUAD

squad moves in squad column to the front or in the direction indicated. The other two squads move in squad column to the right and left until about 50 paces apart and follow the leading squad at about 50 paces unless otherwise indicated. If the leading squad is halted by the platoon leader, the other two squads move to

The center (or designated) squad follows the leading squads in squad column at about 50 paces. If the leading squads are halted by the platoon leader, the rear squad moves to the rear if necessary to get a distance of about 50 paces behind the leading squads. 

449. From column of threes, the center squad is usually designated as the forward or rear squad. In other dispositions, the squad most conveniently located is designated. 

450. To Deploy the Platoon as Skirmishers. The command is:: AS SKIRMISHERS. 

The squads move out as indicated for squad columns (Par. 448-1) and deploy as skirmishers at the command of the squad leader as they reach their positions. 

To deploy as skirmishers with one squad forward (or back) the command is: AS 

SKIRMISHERS, CENTER (or other) SQUAD FORWARD (or BACK). The squads move out as indicated in Par. 448, deploying as skirmishers as they reach their positions. 

451. To Deploy the Squads as Skirmishers. The platoon being in squad columns, the platoon leader may deploy the squads as skirmishers by the command: AS SKIR- 

MISHERS. At this signal or command the squads deploy, retaining their relative positions within the platoon. 452.

To Assemble the Platoon. To assemble the platoon, the leader signals or commands: ASSEMBLE, and points to the place where the assembly is to be made. 

The platoon assembles in column of threes. 

453. To Advance the Platoon. The leader, assisted by the second in command, directs the advance of the platoon. To advance individual squads, the platoon leader signals: FORWARD, to the squad leaders concerned, leaving the method of advance to them; or he may go to the squad leaders or assemble them and give them direct oral orders, or send orders by his second in command or messenger. To cause the entire platoon to rush, the leader commands: FOLLOW ME, and springs forward, leading the platoon to the new position. 

454. Antiaircraft Deployment. Being in column of threes at the command:

PLANE(S) FRONT (RIGHT, LEFT, or REAR), the right squad moves to the right at top speed. The leading and rear men of the right squad go about 10 paces and the center man about 50 paces. Other men of the right squad go to such distances that when halted the squad forms a rough semicircle. The left squad moves to the left in like manner. The men of the center squad take cover between the right and left squads, odd-numbered men to the left and even-numbered men to the right. [61] In deployments against air attack, men upon halting assume the antiaircraft firing position, facing the approaching airplanes. 

455. Being deployed to meet air attack, to continue the march without assembling on the road, squad columns are formed and the march continued off the road. 

456. In a 4 squad unit in column of fours, the same method is used. The two center squads deploy to the right and left. In column of twos the same method is used, the right column deploying to the right and the left column to the left. 

457. Posts of the Guide and of the Messenger. The platoon guide and messenger join the platoon leader on deployment. 

458. Use of Scouts. When contact is imminent the platoon leader sends out scouts from the leading squads. No more scout pairs than necessary to cover the front of the platoon will be used.

SECTION IV RIFLE COMPANY 

459. The company does not execute extended order movements by any special signals or commands. The platoons are usually disposed and maneuver as directed by the company commander. Such directions are usually given in the form of oral combat orders. Commands analogous to those prescribed for the platoon may be used when appropriate.

SECTION V WEAPONS SQUADS, SECTIONS, AND PLATOONS 

460. Squad. Weapons squads represent teams serving a single weapon (machine gun, mortar, or antitank gun). Therefore their deployment is limited to taking up a dispersed formation to prevent casualties when moving. When firing, the crew is at the weapon. Carriers bearing ammunition operate between the ammunition vehicle and the weapon. The usual deployment for weapons squads is therefore an irregular column. When desired, a formation similar to that of adjacent units may be presented. Specific instruction for the organization, duties, and equipment of crews are given in drills for the particular weapon. Movements prescribed in this section are executed at ease, and at a walk unless otherwise directed. 

461. Duties of the Squad Leader. The squad is deployed and led by its leader.

During movement, his usual position is at the head of his squad. It is his duty to watch his section leader for signals and to lead his squad in the assigned direction, controlling the movements of his men to insure the skilful use of ground and cover. When resuming the advance after a halt, he will make sure that all members of the squad have understood the order to move forward. In case of casualties, he will prescribe such redistribution of the loads as will insure the arrival at the next firing position of all equipment necessary for the immediate opening of fire. 

462. Squad Column with Transportation. At the command SQUAD COLUMN, the squad forms in a single file in rear of its transportation. The distance between men is 5 paces unless otherwise directed. This is varied to meet existing conditions. [62-1,2] 

463. Squad Column Without Transportation. The squad leader directs: UNLOAD TRUCK (OFF CARTS or OFF PACKS). Each man takes the equipment as prescribed in the drill for the weapon and forms in numerical order in single file on the right

Figure 1. The squad with animal drawn carts or pack animals 

Figure 2. The squad with motor truck or carrier (Position of carrier and numbers assigned to individuals will vary for different weapons.)

The men form in an irregular column behind the squad leader. The driver of the vehicle (or mule leader) remains with his vehicle (or mule) and places it under any available cover or concealment. 

464. To Maneuver the Squad. The squad leader commands: FOLLOW ME. The squad follows its leader in squad column. If the weapon has been taken from the truck (or carts), the driver of the vehicle (or mule leader) places the transportation under cover. 

465. To Halt the Squad. At the signal or command: HALT, all men and the transportation seek the nearest cover. All watch the squad leader for further orders or signals. 

466. To Load Transportation. The squad leader calls or sends for the transportation, halting it under cover (if available) near the squad. At the command: LOAD 

TRUCKS (ON CARTS or ON PACKS) the squad assembles at the transportation, loads its equipment and forms squad column as shown in Plate 62.

467. Section. Duties of the Section Leader. The section is deployed and led by its leader. During movement he occupies no fixed position, but, generally will be in advance of his unit. When leading his section, he selects routes of advance and reconnoiters for gun positions and targets. When the section is halted in firing position, he places himself where he can best control its fire, usually in rear of and between his weapons. When otherwise halted, he is usually in front of his section. 

He keeps in touch with the platoon leader at all times. 

468. Dispositions. The section may be disposed in line of squad columns or in section column. [62-3,4] The commands for these dispositions are: LINE OF SQUAD COLUMNS 50 (or so many) PACES or SECTION COLUMN 20 (or so many) 

PACES. Each squad leader leads his squad so as to take the disposition directed. 

To maneuver without transportation, the section leader first gives the necessary

EXTENDED ORDER. 

69 

WEAPONS UNITS 

commands to unload transporation, and after it is unloaded gives the command for the disposition desired. The transportation takes cover or moves as directed. 

469. To Assemble. To assemble the section, the section leader takes post at or designates the point at which the section is to assemble and commands or signals: 

ASSEMBLE. Each squad leader assembles his squad, moves it to the point of assembly, and forms as directed. 

470. Platoon. Duties of the Platoon Leader. The platoon leader does not remain in a fixed position, but goes wherever his presence is required. During movement, he is generally on the side toward the enemy. When the platoon is in action, he occupies a position from which he can observe the effects of fire and control his sections. The platoon leader deploys the platoon and directs the advance of and assigns firing positions to his sections. 

471. Duties of the Platoon Sergeant. The platoon sergeant is second in command. He commands the platoon when the leader goes ahead on reconnaissance. 

When the platoon leader is within such distance of his unit as to be able to control its advance, the platoon sergeant follows in rear of the platoon during movement and insures its orderly advance. 

472. Duties of the Transport Corporal. The transport corporal marches (or rides) 

at the rear of the pack animals, carts, or vehicles of the platoon and insures their orderly advance. After off carts (or off packs or unload) has been executed, he takes charge of the carts or vehicles and disposes them in accordance with orders from the platoon leader. In case orders are not issued, he conducts the transportation to the nearest available cover. 

473. Duties of the Corporal Agent. When the platoon is deployed, the messengers are formed as a group under command of the corporal agent. The corporal agent conducts this group at the head of the platoon, unless otherwise directed, conforming to the disposition of its other elements. 

474. Extended Order Dispositions of the Weapons Platoon. The platoon deploys in the same manner as the squad and section, in platoon column, line of sections, or antiaircraft deployment. The dispositions, intervals, and distances will depend upon the requirements of the tactical situation, the nature of the ground, and the fire of the enemy. Each section leader disposes of his squads so as to make maximum use of cover. The dispositions, intervals, and distances may be changed

Carts or motor vehicles clear the road and stop under available cover. 

475. Line of Sections. At the command: LINE OF SECTIONS, the leading or right section moves straight to the front or in the designated direction. The other section moves to the left and forward until the required interval has been gained and the section is on line with the base section. If no interval is announced, the platoon will deploy with 75 paces between sections. 

476. Platoon Column. At the command: PLATOON COLUMN, one section follows the other in column as indicated by the platoon leader. 

477. To Assemble the Platoon. To assemble the platoon from any disposition, the platoon leader takes post at or designates the point of assembly, and signals or commands: ASSEMBLE. At this signal or command, each section leader assembles his section and conducts it to the point of assembly. The platoon is then assembled in column of fours or as directed. The sections of a platoon can be assembled by the command: 1. By sections, 2. ASSemble.

CHAPTER XI. DRILL AND COMBAT SIGNALS SECTION I 

478. Purpose and Kinds. Signals are used to transmit essential commands in situations where oral commands are inadequate or it is inadvisable to use them. To avoid confusion the number of signals is limited, and they are used for simple movements only, especially in combat. Additional signals may be improvised for particular occasions. Signals are made by whistle, bugle, horn, flags, panels, etc., and by arm and hand. 

479. Whistle signals are made with the whistle prescribed for the commander concerned, and limited to: (1) ATTENTION TO ORDERS (or to other signals), a short blast, to fix the attention of the troops. (2) cease firinG, a long blast, to be promptly confirmed by arm-and-hand signal, or other means. (3) IMMEDIATE 

DANGER (as of an impending air, gas, or mechanized attack), three long blasts, repeated several times. This signal may also be given by any man who perceives the danger, by whistle, horn, or three equally spaced rifle shots. The man giving the signal points toward the impending danger. The signal is reserved for cases of immediate and grave danger. 

480. General Rules for Signals. (1) All signals must be plainly visible or audible. (2) All persons fix their attention at the first note or motion of the signal. (3) A signal includes both preparatory command and command of execution. The movement commences as soon as the signal is completed or understood, unless otherwise prescribed. (4) Signals will be repeated when necessary to insure prompt and correct execution. (5) On the firing line, or in exercises simulating firing line conditions, subordinate leaders will repeat back at once each signal, to show that it is understood. (6) Arm-and-hand signals may be made with either arm, except as otherwise prescribed. Preferably they are made with the right arm. (7) When practicable the signal should begin with the arm at the side, and finish with its return to the side. (8) Except as otherwise indicated the fingers are extended and joined, and the wrist is straight.

SECTION II ARM-AND-HAND SIGNALS 

481. The signals most commonly employed are shown in Plate 64, and described below. The numbers in parentheses refer to the figures in the plate. (1) Forward; To the right (left); To the rear. Face and move in the desired direction; at same time extend the hand vertically to the full extent of the arm, palm to front, and lower arm and hand in the direction of movement until horizontal. (2) Quick time; Walk. Decrease the gait or speed. Raise the elbow above and to the right (left) of the shoulder, extend forearm to left (right), with hand above head, palm to front. (3) Double time; Rush; Increase the gait or speed. Carry the hand to front of shoulder, fist closed; rapidly thrust the fist upward vertically to full extent of arm, and back to the shoulder, several times. (4) Halt. Carry the open hand to foot of shoulder, palm to front; thrust the hand upward vertically to full extent of arm, and hold in that position until the signal is understood. (5) As skirmishers. Raise both arms laterally to the horizontal, arms and hands extended, palms down. If necessary to indicate direction of advance, follow with signal (1). (5) As skirmishers, right (left). Same as above; then swing the arm on the side toward which deployment is to be made upward to the vertical position, and back to horizontal; repeat several times, holding the other arm in the horizontal position until the signal is completed. (6) Down; Take cover. Turn toward the unit to be signaled; raise the hand in front of the elbow, palm down, forearm horizontal, wrist straight; thrust hand downward and back to horizontal position, several times. (7) Range; Change elevation. Extend arm fully, toward group for whom signal is intended, fist closed. This is the signal for battle sight. Open fist, exposing one finger for each hundred yards of range. To change elevation the complete new range is signaled. (8) Change direction. Carry the hand on the side toward which the change is to be made across the body to front of opposite shoulder, palm down, forearm horizontal; swing the forearm in a horizontal plane, extending arm and hand to point in the new direction. (9) Are you ready? I am ready. Extend the arm horizontally to the front toward the leader signaled; hand raised to a vertical position, fingers extended and joined, palm toward the person signaled. (10) Commence firing. Extend arm and hand to full extent, horizontally in front of body, palm down; move the arm several times through a wide horizontal arc. 70

DRILL AND COMBAT SIGNALS 

71 2 3 5 10 11 12 

Plate 64. Arm-and-Hand Signals 

13 (10) Fire faster (slower). Execute rapidly (slowly) the signal commence firing. For machine guns a change to the next higher (lower) rate is indicated. (11) Assemble. Raise the hand vertically to full extent of arm, fingers extended and joined, and describe large horizontal circles with arm and hand. (12) Cease firing. Raise the forearm until hand is in front of forehead, palm to front; swing hand up and down in front of the face, several times.

(13) Enemy in sight. Grasp the rifle with right hand at small of stock, left hand between the bands; raise and hold rifle horizontally above the head, both arms extended. (14) Fix bayonets. Simulate the movement of the right hand in fixing the bayonet. (Not illustrated). 

Additional Arm-and-Hand Signals for Weapons Units 

482. (1) Action. Thrust the fist several times in the direction in which it is desired to go into action. (2) Out of action. Strike the fist of one hand rapidly against the open palm of the other, several times. (3) Elevate; Depress. Extend one arm toward the gunner(s) concerned; move hand in short upward (downward) movements by flexing the hand at the wrist; with palm of hand toward gunner(s); expose one finger for each mil the gun is to be elevated (depressed). (4) Shift right (left). Extend one arm toward the gunner(s) concerned; swing hand and arm horizontally in the direction in which fire is to be shifted, palm turned in that direction and toward the gunner(s); expose one finger for each mil the fire is to be shifted. (5) Off carts. Face the unit for which the signal is intended; raise the elbow laterally the height of shoulder and touch shoulder with fingers. (6) On carts. Face the unit for which the signal is intended, raise both elbows laterally to height of shoulders and touch shoulders with fingers. (7) Fire one round. Extend one arm above the head toward the gunner for whom the signal is intended. Cut the hand sharply downward. (8) Fire five rounds. Extend one arm above the head, hand open; flex the wrist, making a quick, choppy, lateral movement with the hand. (9) Traversing fire. Face the target, extend one arm fully to the front; move it upward and downward with a chopping motion, at same time swinging arm to right (left) in the direction it is desired to distribute the fire. 

Arm-and-Hand Signals for Motor Vehicles 

483. Sound signals are of little value except at a halt. In rear areas certain bugle signals may be used. Whistle and voice may be used at halts to fix attention. There follows a complete set of arm-and-hand signals for the control of motor convoys, which can be used at a halt or while moving. All personnel must be thoroughly familiar with these signals, and the necessity for prompt transmission. In movement, signals are given by the assistant driver, who makes certain that the assistant drivers in front and rear receive the signals, and promptly transmit them. 

484. The standard signals follow. (1) Attention. Move hand above head rapidly from side to side. (2) Start engines. Simulate cranking. (3) Ready to start. Senior in truck stands on running board, faces leader, and gives the arm signal I am ready. Forward. See Par. 481(1). In starting a convoy, this signal is a warning signal. Immediately after giving the signal, the convoy commander or serial or section leader mounts and directs the driver of his vehicle to start. (4) Drivers turn around simultaneously. Extend both arms horizontally toward the drivers and describe small vertical circles then signal forward in the desired new direction. When the distance between vehicles permits and the convoy is long, this signal may be given by a mortorcycle messenger passing back along the column. (5) Mount. Extend the arm horizontally to the side, palm up, and wave arm upward several times. (6) Dismount. Extend the arm diagonally upward to the side, palm down, and wave arm downward several times. (7) Stop engines. Cross arms in front of body at the waist and then move them sharply to the side. Repeat several times. (8) Assemble. See Par. 481(11). (9) Close up. Extend the arms horizontally, straight to the front, palms in. 

Move the hands together and then resume first position. Repeat several times. (10) Increase speed. See Par. 481(3). 

485. Driver Signals. (1) Slow or stop. Extend the left arm outward, at an angle of 45° below the horizontal. (2) Turn right. Extend the left arm outward at an angle of 45° above the horizontal. (3) Turn left. Extend the left arm outward horizontally. (4) Keep going and pass. Extend the left arm outward horizontally, and describe small circles to front with hand.

CHAPTER XII. INDIVIDUAL EQUIPMENT. SHELTER TENTS SECTION I INDIVIDUAL EQUIPMENT 

486. The individual equipment of the foot soldier includes everything necessary for field service, and has been designed so as not to exceed about 40% of the weight of the man. Some of this is on his person or carried in his hands, such as his clothing and rifle. Part of it is made into a pack, carried in much the same way that an Indian squaw carries her papoose. In order to lighten the load, part of the equipment (the pack carrier and roll) is carried in the trains whenever practicable. The soldier should know every item of his equipment, he should make certain that he has it, and should maintain it in serviceable condition at all times. 

487. Equipment Common to All Infantry Soldiers. The following items of equip ment are issued to and carried by all infantry soldiers: 

Clothing on person 

Extra clothing 

Overcoat and raincoat 

Hat and steel helmet

Toilet equipment 

Mess equipment 

Canteen, cover and cup Rations 

Identification tag 

First aid packet, in pouch 

Gas mask, in carrier

Blanket 

Shelter tent half, pole and pins

Haversack, pack carrier 

488. Combat Equipment of the Infantry Rifle Squad. The following items of equip ment, mostly for combat use, are issued to and carried by the various members of the infantry rifle squad. 

Adhesive plaster

Automatic rifle* 

Axe (or bolo) and carrier

Bandoleer, or loop. AR* 

Bayonet and scabbard 

Cartridge belt

Cleaning rod Compass

Magazine belt, AR

Panel, signal 

Pickmattock and carrier Rifle Screwdriver 

Shovel and carrier 

Spare parts case, AR* 

Wire cutter and carrier

In squads armed with automatic rifle. 

Assembling and Attaching the Equipment 

489. To Assemble Cartridge or Magazine Belt. Place adjusting strap on ground, eyeletted edge to front; pocket sections in prolongation of adjusting strap, pockets down, tops to front. Insert end of adjusting strap in outer loop of metal guide, from upper side; carry it under middle bar and up through inner loop; engage wire hook on end of adjusting strap in eyelets on inner side of belt. Adjust belt to fit. 

490. To Fill Cartridge and Magazine Belt. Unsnap flap of a pocket and retaining strap (if provided), pull retaining strap out to full length. Insert a clip, bullets down, in front of retaining strap, and press down to bottom of pocket. Snap retaining strap to its fastener. Insert a second clip, in rear of first clip and retaining strap, and press down. Close and secure flap. The other nine pockets are similarly filled. When using 

M1 ammunition only one clip is inserted in each pocket. To fill magazine belt: unsnap flap of each pocket and insert 2 automatic clips, base plates up, catches toward buckles of belt. Refasten flap. The other pockets are similarly filled. 

491. To Attach First Aid Pouch. When haversack is carried, attach pouch under fourth pocket from front of left section of cartridge belt. When haversack is not carried, attach under fifth (rear) pocket of left section. On magazine belt attach pouch under third pocket from front of left section of belt. [65-3,4] 

492. To Attach Canteen Cover. (1) To cartridge belt. Under fourth pocket from front of right section of belt; [65-4] (2) To magazine belt. Under third pocket from front of right section of magazine belt. The concave surface of canteen and cup are toward the body, and the flaps of the cover are secured. 

493. To Attach Pack Carrier to Haversack. Spread haversack [65-1] on ground, inner side down, outer flap to front; place buttonholed edge of pack carrier, letter side up, under buttonholed edge of haversack, buttonholes superimposed upon the corresponding ones of haversack. Lace carrier to haversack by passing ends of coupling strap down through corresponding buttonholes nearest center of carrier, bringing ends up through next buttonholes and continuing to right and left to the sides. [65-2] 

494. To Attach Haversack to Cartridge or Magazine Belt. Place assembled haver sack and pack carrier on ground, inner side down; place_cartridge belt, pockets down, tops to front, along junction of haversack and carrier. Insert hooks on rear belt suspenders in upper rear eyelets of each belt section, points of hooks on outside of belt; twist front belt suspenders toward ends of belt, and insert in eyelets between first and second pockets from each end of belt, points of hooks outside of belt so suspenders will lie flat on body. [65-3,4] 

495. To Attach Bayonet Scabbard to Haversack or Cartridge Belt. Pass lower end of scabbard through loop on the side of haversack body, then engage double-hook attachment in eyelets on outer flap of haversack, inserting hooks from underside. Insert bayonet, ring to rear. [3] When haversack is not carried, attach scabbard to cartridge belt under third pocket from front of left section, removing first-aid pouch from under fourth pocket and reattaching under fifth pocket. 497. To Make the Roll. Spread shelter half on ground and fold in triangular end, to form a rectangle. Make a second fold by carrying folded edge to opposite edge. 

Fold blanket twice parallel to long dimension (one-fourth previous width), then fold once at middle. This forms a rectangle about 17 by 42 inches. Place blanket symmetrically in center of folded shelter half; place underwear, tent pole and pins on end from which rolling is to begin (near end). Fold sides and then near end of shelter half snugly over blanket, fold 10 inches of far end of shelter half toward blanket, and beginning at near end, roll as tightly as possible into folded end of shelter half, making an envelope roll. [66-1]

Place two cans of meat component end to end, parallel to and in front of hard bread. Place other can of hard bread in front of meat component, and toilet articles and socks in front of hard bread. INDIVIDUAL EQUIPMENT 

75 499. To Assemble the Pack. Place roll in pack carrier and haversack, one end against bottom of haversack. Place knee against roll, pull carrier down (by suspension rings), and force roll up. Without removing knee, pull lower pack carrier binding strap over roll and secure by passing end of strap up through near opening of buckle, over center stud, down through opening next to attachment, and back under the strap. In like manner secure third (upper) haversack binding strap and then upper carrier strap. Engage snap hooks of suspenders in lower suspension rings. [66-3] 

500. To Attach Overcoat, Raincoat and Helmet. Lay overcoat flat on ground, outside down, collar extended, sleeves inside out and extended toward pockets. Move inside flap of tail 6 inches under outside flap, and gather slack in one fold along middle seam, tapering toward collar. Fold front edges of coat about 12 inches toward center seam, forming a rectangle about 42 inches by 52 inches. Fold collar end down about 15 inches again at top, and roll tightly toward tail; turn tail up about 9 inches, and roll entire coat into pocket thus formed. Place overcoat, open side of roll down, on top of haversack, in rear of bayonet so that center back seam is over center of top of haversack. Secure at top with a shelter half rope. Bind ends of overcoat down and along sides of haversack. Lash ends of roll to haversack with the rope, using a half hitch near each end of overcoat, and passing rope around haversack over outside flap. 

Fold raincoat, outside out, into a rectangle about 10% by 8½ inches. Place between inner and outer haversack flaps and secure with lower haversack binding strap, by passing it under bottom haversack binding strap and fastening tightly to buckle on under side of outer haversack flap. Attach helmet by placing chin strap over meat can pouch and tying on with twine or a shoe lace. [66-4; 67] 

Assembling the Full Equipment, Without Rations

Roll toilet articles and socks in inside flap, top of articles on line with top of haversack. Fold lower haversack binding strap against roll thus formed.

Attach overcoat, raincoat and helmet.
