Lace coupling strap through button holes in upper edge of carrier. 

Adjusting the Equipment to the Soldier 

506. Cartridge and Magazine Belts. Adjust belt to fit loosely, well down over hip bones and below pit of abdomen. The adjustment should be made equally from both ends of adjusting straps so that center eyelet will be in middle of belt. 

507. Full Equipment. Slip arms through pack suspenders, as in putting on a coat.

By adjusting buckles on belt suspenders, adjust belt as above described. The rear adjusting strap should lie smoothly across small of back. By adjusting straps on pack suspenders adjust load until top of haversack is level with shoulders, and pack suspenders from points of attachment on haversack to shoulders are horizontal. The full equipment, less roll, is similarly adjusted. [67] REAR

508. Special Equipment. Certain items of special equipment are carried as follows: (1) Bandoleer, magazine, for automatic rifle: Suspended on either side by strap over opposite shoulder. (2) Bugle: Suspended on left side by strap over right shoulder. (3) Compass: On belt in front of right hip. (4) Cutter, wire: On left side of belt immediately in front of first-aid pouch. (5) Gas mask: Under left arm and over all other equipment, suspended by strap over right shoulder. (6) Pockets, web, for automatic rifle: Attached to belt in front of right hip. (7) Pockets, web, double: In front of left hip. 

509. Display of Equipment. At all inspections equipment of dismounted men will be displayed as shown in Plate 68, except that rifles may be stacked. The blanket

PITCHING SHELTER TENTS 

77 is folded within shelter half, as in making the roll. If shelter tents have been pitched the equipment is displayed on the blanket, in front of tent, one corner of blanket touching center pole, raincoat on top of overcoat. Articles worn on belt remain so. Other articles, not shown in plate will be displayed in space between overcoat and tent pins, bandoleers with buttons of pockets flush with rear edge of blanket. 

SECTION II SHELTER TENTS 

510. Formation. The formation for pitching shelter tents is prescribed in Par. 

183 of the text (Infantry Drill Regulations). 

511. To Pitch Tents. The command is: 1. Pitch, 2. TENTS. Each man steps off obliquely to the right with his right foot, a full pace, lays his rifle on the ground muzzle to the front, barrel to the left, butt near the toe of his right foot. He then steps back into place, unslings his equipment and places it on the ground in front of him. He opens his pack and takes out his shelter half, pole and pins. 

512. Each pair of men spread their shelter halves on the ground the tent is to 

Occupy, triangles to rear, buttons to center, the even numbered man's half on the right. They button the halves together. The odd-number inserts the small end of his pole through the front eyes of the tent, and holds the pole upright in position beside the bayonet (stuck in the ground). The even-number pins down the front corners in line with the bayonets, and drives the front guy pin a rifle length or half a tent 

Plate 69.

Shelter Tents width in front of the pole. He places the loop of the guy rope over the front guy pin, runs the other end through the loops of the shelter halves, and secures it in such position that the pole is vertical when the rope is taut. 

513. Both men then proceed to the rear of the tent. The even-number adjusts his pole as described, while odd-number pins the tent corners, the triangle, and the rear guy rope with its pin a bayonet length in rear of the triangle, or as prescribed for the front guy pin. The even-number man then drives the remaining tent pins on the right, and the odd-number those on the left. Hand axes or pick mattocks may be used to drive tent pins, but shovels, bolos and bayonets will not be so used. 

514. Each man then arranges the contents of his pack for display, and takes position at attention at the side of the tent, toes on line with the front pins, oddnumbered men on the left of their respective tents, even-numbered men on the right. 

515. To Pitch Double Tents. Double shelter tents, accommodating 4 men, afford more warmth and shelter in cold or stormy weather, and also occupy less space. A double tent is two single tents buttoned together at the square ends, and supported on three poles. Two guy ropes are placed at each end, the pins in line with the sides of the tent. Each tent is erected by a group of 4 men, as 1-2-3-4 and 5-6-7-8. The four men spread their shelter halves on the ground, odd-numbers with triangles to the front, even-numbers with triangles to the rear; and button them together, first along the ridge and then across the square ends. The odd-numbers insert the front and rear poles. The even-numbers pin down the front and rear corners. No. 2 enters the tent and sets the center pole, inserting its upper end through the tent eyelets in the order: lower half of front tent, lower half of rear tent, etc. He is assisted by 

No. 4. The even numbers then set the guy ropes, and all drive the remaining pins. 

516. To Strike Tents. The unit is brought to attention, with the men in the positions prescribed after pitching tents. At the command: 1. Strike, 2. TENTS, the men remove equipment from tents, pull pins, remove poles and unbutton the tents. 

They then make up their packs, sling their equipment, and take their positions at intervals, as when formed to pitch tents.

ADDENDA MANUAL OF THE SABER FOR OFFICERS 

1. General Provisions. (1) For garrison service all officers and warrant officers are equipped with the saber. The saber is not carried in the field. (2) Dismounted, the scabbard is carried on the left side, attached to the belt by the sling or chain, and hooked in the upper ring, guard of saber to rear. Mounted, the scabbard is carried in the saber carrier, attached to the offside of the cantle of the saddle, guard of saber to rear. (3) Staff officers draw and return saber with the commander. (4) The manual of the saber is executed in the cadence of quick time, and without command, except at saber drill. When marching At ease or at Route step the saber may be sheathed. (5) The position of Order saber [6] is assumed by dismounted officers when in formation at Attention, except as otherwise provided below. When troops execute the manual of arms during ceremonies officers remain at the position of Order saber. (6) The position of Carry saber [3] is assumed: (a) To give commands. (b) To change position at quick time. (c) When officially addressing or addressed by another officer, the saber being drawn. (d) To return saber. (e) At the preparatory command for, and while marching in quick time. (7) The position of Present saber [4; 5] is assumed: (a) To salute with saber drawn. (b) By officers of a unit, at the command_Present arms to the colors, the national anthem, another unit or any person. (c) On executing Eyes right (left) when passing in review. (Officers in the interior of a mass formation do not execute 

Present saber.) (8) The position of Port saber is assumed when marching at double time, the hand being allowed to swing slightly. [7] (9) The position of Parade rest is assumed by dismounted officers when their units execute Parade rest. [8] (10) Mounted officers do not execute Order or Port saber or Parade rest. Dismounted 

2. Draw Saber. The command is: 1. Draw, 2. SABER. At Draw, unhook saber with left hand, thumb on hook of belt, first two fingers lifting upper ring. Grasp scabbard with left hand at upper band, and tilt forward about 45°, guard down. Turn head and look at hilt, grasp hilt with right hand, press left hand against thigh and draw blade about 6 inches out of scabbard. Turn head and eyes to front. [1]. At command SABER, draw saber smartly, extending right arm to right front, about 45° from horizontal, saber in prolongation of arm, edge down. [2] 

Make short pause and lower hand to side, bringing back of blade against point of shoulder, edge to front, arm nearly extended, elbow back, grip clasped between thumb and first two fingers, other fingers back of grip. [3] At same time hook up scabbard with left hand, thumb through upper ring, first two fingers supporting it. 

Drop left hand to side. This is the position of Carry saber. Dismounted officers do not use the saber knot except when necessary in reading orders or calling roll. 

3. Present Saber. Being at Carry saber, the command is: 1. Present, 2. SABER (ARMS). At command Present, raise and move saber to left until base of hilt is at height of and 3 inches in front of chin, edge to left, point up and about 6 inches farther to front than hilt, thumb extended on left of grip, all fingers clasping grip. [4] At command SABER (ARMS), lower saber smartly until point is in prolongation of right foot and near ground, edge to left, arm extended, hand near side. [5] If marching (Eyes right) both arms are allowed to swing naturally. The rules as to when to execute the hand salute apply also to saber salute. To return to Carry saber from Present saber, execute Order saber, followed by Carry saber, in cadence. 

4. Order Saber. Being at Carry saber, the command is: 1. Order, 2. SABER 

(ARMS). At command SABER, drop point directly to front, on or near ground, edge down, thumb along back of grip. To execute Order from second position of 

Present, rotate saber to left, blade down, thumb along back of grip. [6] 

5. Parade Rest. Being at Order saber, the command is: 1. Parade, 2. REST. 

At REST, move left foot 12 inches to left of right foot, keeping legs straight, weight of body resting equally on both feet. Place left hand behind body in small of back, palm to rear. At command ATTENTION, resume position of Order saber in one motion. [8] 

6. Port Saber. Being at Carry saber, the command is: 1. Port, 2. SABER. At command SABER, place the blade diagonally across breast, edge to front, right hand in front of right hip and at height of waist, left hand steadying scabbard. [7] 

7. Return Saber. This should be executed at a halt if practicable. Being at Carry saber, the command is: 1. Return, 2. SABER. At command Return, carry right 78

MANUAL OF THE SABER 

79 hand opposite to and 6 inches in front of left shoulder, saber vertical, edge to left. 

At same time unhook, grasp and tilt scabbard with left hand, as in Draw saber. 

At SABER, drop point to rear, passing blade across and along left arm. Turn head ་ག 

Plate 1. Manual of the Saber and look at scabbard, raise right hand, insert and sheathe blade. Disengage saber knot (if used), turn head and eyes to front and drop right hand to side. Hook up scabbard and drop left hand to side. [9; 10] Mounted 8. Draw Saber. Executed as when dismounted, except that left hand is not used, and at Carry saber right hand rests on thigh. The right wrist is engaged and secured in saber knot before loosening blade in scabbard. 

9. Present Saber. Executed as when dismounted, except that in the second position the hand is held slightly in rear of the thigh, point of saber a little to the right of and in front of the stirrup. 

10. Return Saber. At command Return, carry right hand to height of and 6 inches in front of right shoulder, blade vertical, edge to front, grip clasped so that pommel rests in hollow of hand. At command SABER, turn head and look to scabbard, raise saber vertically to full extent of arm, lower blade forward, insert point in scabbard and sheathe. Disengage wrist from saber knot and resume position of Attention.

DEFINITIONS OF TERMS USED IN DRILL DEFINITIONS OF COMMON TERMS USED IN DRILL 

Alignment. A straight line upon which several elements are formed or are to be formed; or the dressing of several elements on a straight line. 

Base. The element on which a movement is regulated. 

Battle sight. The position of the rear sight of the M1903 rifle when the sight leaf is down. It corresponds to a range of about 550 yds. 

Cadence. Time interval between steps or other movements, expressed as SO many steps per minute.

Center. The middle point or element of an organization. 

Column. A formation in which elements are placed one behind another. 

Cover in file. To be directly in rear of the corresponding file in front. 

Depth. The space from front to rear of any formation, or of a position, including the front and rear elements. The depth of a man is assumed to be 12 inches. 

Deploy. To extend the front (or depth) of a formation by increasing intervals (or distances), especially to change from close to extended order. Deployed formations with wide intervals are characteristic of battle. 

Distance. Open space between individuals or elements, in the direction of depth. 

It is measured from the back of the element in front to the front of the element in rear. Platoon leaders, guides, and others in a line, are considered as a rank of the formation, but the commander of a unit and those accompanying him are ordinarily not considered in measuring distances. The distance between ranks of dismounted men at close order, in either line or column, is 40 inches. 

Double time. Cadence in marching at the rate of 180 steps per minute.

Element. A squad, section, platoon or larger unit forming part of a still larger unit. 

File. A column having a front of only one man.

Flank. The right or left of a formation, or the element on the right or left. 

The space to the right or left. The side of a force or position to the right or left when facing the enemy. See also Left and Right.

Follow in trace. To march exactly in rear of and maintain correct distance from an element or guide in front. 

Formation. Arrangement of the elements of a command. The placing of all fractions in their order, in line or column, or for battle. 

Front. The space occupied by a command or any element, measured laterally from one flank to the other. The front of a man is taken as 22 inches. direction in which a formation faces. The direction toward the enemy. The Guide. An individual upon whom a command (or elements thereof) regulates its march.

Head. The leading element of a column in the direction in which it faces or marches.

Interval. Open space between individuals or elements in the same line. For dismounted men it is measured from shoulder to shoulder or elbow to elbow. The normal interval between men is arms length, close interval is 4 inches. Between troops interval is measured from the left flank of the element on the right to the right flank of the element on the left. A color guard or individuals posted in an interval is not considered in measuring the interval. 

Left. The left extremity or element of a formation.

Line. A formation in which the elements are abreast or on the same line. 

Mass formation. A formation of a company or larger unit with squads in column abreast of one another. A close order formation with reduced intervals or distances. 

Order, close. Drill in which the elements are arranged with normal (close) intervals and distances.

Order, extended. Formations used in combat, in which elements are separated by intervals and distances greater than those of close order. 

Pace. A step of 30 inches; the full step at quick time. Employed as a unit of measure for intervals and distances. 

Piece. A rifle or automatic rifle. 

Pivot. An individual on whom a change of direction is made in marching. 

Quick time. Cadence at the rate of 120 steps per minute. It is the cadence of the manual of arms and all other movements performed in unison, except marchings at double time.

Rank. A line of men side by side. 

Rear. The direction opposite to that in which a unit faces or marches. The direction away from the enemy.

Step. The distance from heel to heel between the successive footprints of a man walking. Full steps in quick and double time are 30 and 36 inches, respectively. 

Half step and back step are 15 inches. Side steps are 12 inches. 

Tail. The last element of a column in the direction of march.

PHYSICAL TRAINING 1. Outdoors. 

SETTING-UP EXERCISES 

Physical training should be conducted outdoors when possible. 

2. Uniform. Loosely laced breeches without leggins, cotton undershirts (olive drab shirts in colder weather), gymnasium shoes. No blouses or headdress. Gymnasium costume may be worn. 

3. Breathing. Breathing should be unimpeded. Inhalation should accompany that part of an exercise which tends to expand the chest. Inhalation should be through the nose; exhalation through the mouth. If breathing becomes labored a rest should be taken. 

4. When one part of the body is being exercised other parts should remain as nearly as possible immobile and in their normal positions. The chest is elevated in all appropriate movements, such as raising the arms and bending backwards. 

5. Eating and Drinking. Exercise should not be taken within a half hour before or after a meal, and there should be no strenuous exercise before breakfast. There should be no drinking, especially of ice water, during or immediately after exercise. 

Allow the body to cool off first. 

6. After Exercising. Do not remove clothing to cool off, on the contrary cover the body more warmly immediately after exercise. Allow body to cool slowly before bathing. Baths following exercise should be in tepid or warm (never cold) water, followed by a brief cool shower. If impossible to bathe, sponge body with tepid water, rub thoroughly with a coarse towel, and put on fresh clothing. 

7. Period of Instruction. Setting-up exercises should generally be limited to a period of 20 minutes or less. 

8. Number to be Instructed. For instructional purposes groups should preferably be not larger than a platoon. For disciplinary and exhibition purposes mass drill should occasionally be given. 

9. Commands. The commands include a preparatory command which describes the movement in simple words, and the command of execution. For static exercises, where a position is held for an appreciable time, words of command are used, as: 

1. On toes, 2. RISE, 3. Knees, 4. BEND, etc. At command RECOVER, starting position is resumed. For continued or repeated exercises the preparatory command explains the entire exercise, and the command of execution is EXERCISE. The instructor then counts, ONE, TWO, etc. The number of counts should correspond to the number of separate motions in the entire exercise, the count ONE, coinciding with the first motion. The exercise is continued as long as desired by repeating the count. 

10. The emphasis on commands of execution or numbers indicates and regulates the manner in which the motions are to be made, thus: O-n-e (drawling), TWO! (sharp)_indicates that the first motion is to be executed slowly and the second with snap. The cadence of the commands or count regulates the cadence of the exercise. 

11. To Discontinue an Exercise. Being in any starting position at command Arms, DOWN, arms are brought down smartly, without touching the body, and the position of attention is resumed. To discontinue an exercise executed by count, the command HALT is given in place of the last number, when the position of attention is resumed, thus: ONE, TWO, THREE, FOUR, ONE, TWO, THREE, HALT. At com mand RECOVER, the starting position is resumed. 

12. Severity of Exercises. Instruction begins with the simpler exercises and proceeds to those more difficult and severe. The difficulty and severity of an exercise may also be increased by longer continuation: by quicker cadence and more vigorous execution, regulated by quicker cadence and more vigor in commands or count; and by difficult combinations of motions, requiring accurate coordination of mind and body. 

13. Instructor. The instructor explains and as necessary demonstrates each exercise, and states its object. He adapts the instruction to the physical condition, aptitude and previous training of the men. 

The 

14. Formation. The platoon (group) under instruction is formed in equalized columns of threes (fours) (See Sec. II, Chapter IV, IDR), short men in front. At the command 1. Extend to the left, 2. MARCH, the right squad stands fast. other squads face to the left in marching and double time out to an interval of 2 paces between squads. They then halt, face to the front, and all extend arms sideways. This gives an interval of about 12 ins. between fingertips of men in the same rank. At the command 1. Arms, 2. DOWN, all come to attention. At the command 1. Even numbers to the right (left), 2. UNCOVER, each even number jumps sideways from one foot, lighting on the other in the middle of the interval to his right (left), and comes to attention. If there be several platoons, the instructor arranges them in any desired positions. An elevated platform, from which the instructor can see and be seen by all the men, is desirable. To assemble, the command is: 1. Assemble, 2. MARCH. All return at double time to their original positions. 81.

SETTING-UP EXERCISES 

15. Starting Positions. All arm exercises begin from and their count finishes at a starting position. The starting positions are: (1) Attention. The position of the soldier. (2) 1. Arms forward, 2. RAISE. Raise arms to front horizontal, palms down, fingers extended and joined, thumbs under index fingers. (3) 1. Arms sideward, 2. RAISE. Raise arms to side horizontal, palms and fingers as in (2). (4) 1. Arms forward (sideward) upward, 2. RAISE. Raise arms forward (sideward) and extend vertically upward, palms inward. (5) 1. Arms to thrust, 2. RAISE. Raise forearms to horizontal position, elbows forced back, upper arms close against body, hands closed, knuckles down. (6) 1. Hands on hips, 2. PLACE. Place hands on hips, finger tips in line with breeches seams, thumbs to rear, elbows pressed back. (7) 1. Hands on shoulders, 2. PLACE. Raise forearms to vertical position, palms in; raise upper arms laterally to horizontal position; at same time drop hands until finger tips rest lightly on shoulders. (8) 1. Fingers in rear of head, 2. LACE. Raise arms as in (7); lace fingers behind lower part of head, elbows well up and back. 

16. Any of these positions may be assumed from any other without returning to position of attention, by appropriate command. 

EXERCISES 

17. Each lesson should include exercise for all parts of the body, which is possible because of the variety of exercises and an unlimited number of combinations. The following sequence is most efficacious in the harmonious development of the entire body, and should be adhered to in planning lessons: (1) Introductory arm movement; (2) Leg exercise; (3) Trunk exercise, turning; (4) Foot or leg exercise; (5) Trunk exercise, dorsal, bending forward; (6) Shoulder exercise; (7) Trunk exercise, lateral, bending sideways; (8) Arm exercise; (9) Trunk exercise, abdominal, bending backward; (10) Balancing exercise; (11) Breathing exercise. For exhibition purposes any combinations may be used. 

Course for Recruit Instruction 

18. The following course of 10 lessons, progressively arranged, is given in Basic 

Field Manual, Volume I. Each lesson should be mastered before the next is undertaken. Each lesson should include the essential exercises of the preceding lesson. 

In the following descriptions the commands of execution and discontinuance, PLACE (for starting position), EXERCISE, and HALT, are omitted. Each lesson begins by repeating the starting positions described in Par. 15. 

19. First Lesson. (a) Arms forward: Swing arms downward and forward. Count two. (b) Hands on hips: Rise on toes. Count two, emphasizing first count. The body is raised smartly and lowered gently. (c) Arms to thrust: Bend neck forward (backward). Count two, drawling first count. Chin is drawn in and neck bent slowly, and recovery is quick. (d) Hands on hips: Turn trunk right (left). Count two, drawling first count. 

Trunk is turned slowly as far as possible to right (left); hips, knees and feet are not moved. (e) Hands on hips: Quarter bend knees. Count two, emphasizing second count. 

Knees are bent outward over feet; heels remain on ground; head, trunk and shoulders are held erect and square. (f) Hands on hips: bend trunk forward, back arched. Count two. Body is bent slowly forward at waist, about 30°, and back to starting position. (g) Arms to thrust: Raise and lower shoulders. Count two, emphasizing both counts. The shoulders are raised as high as possible, and returned to starting position. (h) Hands on hips: Bend trunk sideward right (left). Count two, drawling first count. Trunk is bent slowly at waist, back arched, head moving with trunk. (i) Arms forward: Stretch arms sideward. Count two, drawling first count; recover smartly. Arms fully extended are stretched to sides horizontally, palms down. (j) Hands on hips: Bend trunk backward. Count two, drawling first count; recover smartly. Trunk is bent backward at shoulders, as far as possible. Chin is drawn in and neck is forced back. (k) Hands on hips: Raise knees forward, alternately. Count four, emphasizing first and third counts. Thigh is raised smartly to front until horizontal, lower leg at right angles to thigh, foot depressed.

Count four, emphasizing first and third counts. Heels remain together, head and trunk erect. (c) Hands on hips: Bend neck forward and backward. See (c), first lesson. 

Count four, drawling first and third counts. (d) Arms sideward: Turn trunk sideward, right and left. See (d), first lesson.

Body is raised on toes, knees are bent as much as possible, then straightened, and heels lowered. (f) Fingers in rear of head: Bend trunk forward, half bend. Count two, drawling first count. Body is bent forward slowly at waist until horizontal, back flat, elbows in plane of shoulders. (g) Arms to thrust: Move shoulders forward, upward, backward, and recover. 

Count four, drawling first and third counts. Shoulders are relaxed, moved forward as far as possible, then raised, then slowly forced back, and lowered to normal position. (h) Hands on shoulders: Bend trunk sideward, right and left alternately. See (h), first lesson. Count four, drawling first and third counts. (i) Attention: Stretch arms sideward, upward, sideward, and recover. Count four, drawling first three counts, and making last snappy. The arms are stretched laterally in plane of shoulders, palms down. (j) Arms to thrust: Bend trunk backward. See (j), first lesson. (k) Hands on hips: Extend legs backward, right and left alternating. Count four, making first and third counts snappy. Legs, with toes depressed, are extended smartly to rear, foot about 6 inches above ground, held momentarily, and then replaced. (1) Breathing exercise: Inhale, raising arms forward and upward; exhale lowering arms sideward. 

22. Fourth Lesson. (a) Hands on shoulders: Extend arms forward, swing sideward, forward, and recover. Count four, emphasizing first and fourth counts. Palms are up.

(b) Hands on hips: Rise on toes in quick succession. See (b), first lesson. Count two, quickly. (c) Hands on hips: Turn head right and left alternately. Count four, drawling first and third counts. 23. Fifth Lesson. (a) Arms to thrust: Thrust arms forward, swing downward, and forward, and recover. Count four, emphasizing first and fourth counts. Arms are thrust to front knuckles up, then downward and to rear, forward again, and back to starting position. (b) Fingers in rear of head: Rise on toes and rock. Count two, in quick succession. Being on toes body is raised and lowered by quick extensions and flexions of toes, heels together and not touching ground. (c) Hands on hips: Bend head forward and backward. first and third counts. See (c), first lesson. 

Count four, drawling (d) Hands on shoulders: Turn trunk right and left alternately, and stretch arms sideward. See (d), fourth lesson. (e) Attention: To the squatting position, BEND; Extend right and left legs alternately backward. At BEND, a full knee bend is executed, and hands placed on ground between knees. This is the squatting position. Legs are extended alternately fully to rear, resting on toes, and returned to squatting position. (f) Hands on shoulders: Bend trunk forward, back arched, and stretch arms sideward. Count two, drawling first count. As trunk is bent, arms are stretched sideward, palms down, in place of shoulders. (g) Attention: Curl shoulders forward and backward. Count two, drawling both counts. Shoulders are curled forward, arms rotating until backs of hands are in; then backward until palms are out. (h) Hands on shoulders: Bend trunk sideward, right and left alternately, and stretch arms sideward. Count four, drawling first and third counts. See (h), second lesson. RIFLE TRAINING U. S. RIFLES M1903 (SPRINGFIELD) AND M1 (GARAND) CHAPTER I. INTRODUCTION. SCOPE OF THE COURSE Introduction 

1. The Rifle. The rifle is the most important weapon of war. Nothing can take its place except a better rifle. The rifle has been developed from a crude, single shot, muzzle loader, to the breech loading magazine rifle of today. The latest development is a semiautomatic or self-loading rifle, which will ultimately replace the M1903 or Springfield Rifle. Both are described in this course. 

2. Each rifleman is responsible for the care of the piece with which he is equipped. 

Experience proves that many more rifles become unserviceable from lack of proper care than from long continued use. 

3. Scope of the Course. The course of training in the use of the rifle has three phases, as follows: 

(1) Individual training of the rifleman. This includes extended order drill, signals, individual movement and the use of cover (as taught in Scouting and Patrolling), and individual marksmanship training, including fire on moving ground and air targets. (2) Technique of fire. This is team training of the rifle fire unit (usually the rifle squad) in the application of collective fire. (3) Combat firing. This is the application of the individual and combined training of the first and second phases to tactical instruction. 

4. This course deals with the first and second phases. Individual training includes the mechanics of the rifle, and marksmanship. The course in marksmanship training in the U. S. Army is the result of long experience, and based on the proven fact that any man of normal physique can be made a good shot in a remarkably short time by the simple training herein described. 

5. M1903 and M1 Rifles. At the present time the U. S. Army is equipped with two rifles, the M1903 and M1. The latter is in process of manufacture, and is intended ultimately to replace the former. The two weapons are very similar. They are about the same size and weight, use the same ammunition, bayonet and sling, and have the same ballistic properties. The principal differences between the two weapons are as follows: The M1903 is operated entirely by hand, whereas the M1 is semiautomatic or self-loading. The M1 is thus subject to stoppages, whereas the M1903 is practically immune to stoppage. The magazine capacity of the M1903 is 5 cartridges, that of the M1 is 8 cartridges. The M1 has a more modern sight than the M1903, changes in both elevation and windage being made by turning small knobs provided with click detents. 

6. Instruction in individual marksmanship, technique of fire, landscape target and field target firing, are thus virtually the same for the two weapons, and are treated as a single course, with notations as to minor differences, due to the differences in the two weapons. Separate sections are devoted to the mechanics, care and cleaning of the two weapons. 

CHAPTER II. THE U. S. RIFLE, M1903, MECHANICAL TRAINING Description and Nomenclature 

7. This weapon is commonly called the Springfield Rifle, after the armory in 

Springfield, Mass., where it was manufactured. It is a breechloading, magazine (repeating) rifle of the bolt type. Its caliber is .30, which means 30 hundredths of an inch, measured between lands of the rifling. Its overall length is 43 inches, and its weight is 8.7 lbs., not including the bayonet, which weighs one pound. It has a maximum range of 2 miles, but as a rifleman cannot see a small target at such a distance, the usable range in battle seldom exceeds 600 yds. The normal rate of rapid fire by a well trained man is 10 to 15 aimed shots per minute, rapid fire. 

There is no mechanism for absorbing any of the heavy shock of recoil, which is sustained by the shoulder of the rifleman. The important external parts of the weapon are shown in Plate 1. Their purposes will usually be evident. The safety lock locks the firing pin and prevents discharge when the piece is cocked, when turned to the right, exposing the word SAFE. When turned to the left it exposes the word READY and the piece can be fired. The cutoff renders the magazine inoperative and permits use of the weapon as a single loader when turned down, exposing the word OFF. 

8. The Sight. The front sight is merely a thin blade, fixed to the muzzle and protected by a cover. The rear sight is a hinged leaf, which can be raised to perpendicular position. It is graduated at 25 yd. intervals up to a range of 2,850 yards. The drift slide, which is moved up and down, has notches (open sights) and a circular hole called the peep sight. It automatically corrects for the drift of the 89

46. Blade bullet to the right at any range at which it is set. When the leaf is down the battle sight notch corresponds to a range of 547 yds. The sight is provided with a wind gauge, one quarter point of which causes a lateral deviation of the point of impact of 1 inch for each 100 yds. of range. [1] 9.

Ammunition. The cartridge consists of a brass case, with a primer in its base, and containing the propelling charge of smokeless powder and bullet, all in one piece. Ammunition of this kind is known as fixed ammunition. The case also serves as a gas check, preventing the escape of gas from the breech by expanding to tight contact with the chamber wall. The bullet is elongated and pointed. It is made of a lead and tin alloy, with a jacket of gilding metal or cupro-nickel. Cartridges weigh about 0.05 lb., and are assembled for loading on clips holding 5 cartridges (8 for the M1 rifle). Various types of cartridge (bullet) for various purposes are provided. They include: (1) Ball, for use against personnel. (2) Tracer, for observation of fire and incendiary purposes (painted red on point of bullet). (3) Armor-piercing for use against armored vehicles (painted black_on point of bullet). (4) Dummies and blanks for training and special purposes. The present standard cartridge is the M2, having a maximum range of about 2 miles. The M1906 is very similar to the M2. The high-powered M1 (boattail) ball cartridge, having a maximum range of over 3 miles, is not used with either the M1903 or M1 rifle. 

Tracer bullets have small pellets of incendiary material in their bases, which ignite on discharge. This enables the flight of the bullet to be followed (traced). They are used for observation of fire, target designation, and incendiary purposes. Practice dummy cartridges are used in preliminary training. They have a distinctive shape and color to distinguish them from live ammunition. Range dummy cartridges, closely resembling ball cartridges, are used only on the range. Ammunition should be kept clean but not polished, dry and cool. 

10. Individual Safety Precautions. Never leave a rifle around loaded, and assume any rifle to be loaded until you have examined it. Never point a rifle in a direction where an accidental discharge would cause injury or damage. If a rifle must be carried with a cartridge in the chamber be sure it is cocked, and the safety

MRIFLE. CARE AND CLEANING 

91 lock set at SAFE. Make sure there is no obstruction in the bore before firing. 

Never use ammunition that is defective in any way, dirty, wet, or warm. Never oil or grease ammunition or chamber of piece while firing. For precautions on the range, see also Par. 163.

Care and Cleaning 

11. In Garrison and Camp. This includes ordinary care to preserve the serviceability and appearance of the rifle. It should be cleaned and protected after every march, drill or tour of guard duty, especially if exposed to rain or dust. No plug in the muzzle, and no protective covering of any kind will be used, except a sheet while barracks are being swept. The rifle should not be touched unnecessarily with damp or sweaty hands, nor exposed to damp air. The disassembly permitted for cleaning purposes is stated in Par. 16. 

12. Remove the bolt. Rub all accessible parts, including the sling and stock, with a slightly greased or oily rag, and wipe clean with a dry rag. Swab the chamber, and the bore from the breech end, with an oily flannel patch and then a dry patch. To clean the bore use the cleaning rod, if available, otherwise the thong and brush. Clean out all screwheads and crevices with a small, soft brush, or toothpick. Polish the stock once a month with a little linseed oil in the palm of the hand. After cleaning, to protect the rifle, swab the bore thoroughly with a flannel patch saturated with a medium rust preventive compound; wipe all metal parts, including bolt and magazine, with an oily rag; and apply a few drops of thin lubricating oil to all working surfaces. 

13. After Firing. A rifle that has been fired must be well cleaned not later than the evening of the same day, and thereafter similarly cleaned daily for at least three consecutive days. To clean the bore insert the muzzle in a vessel containing hot water and issue soap, hot water, or cold water. The cleaning rod with a cloth patch attached, is inserted in the breech end and the water pumped back and forth through the barrel several times. While the bore is still wet, run a brass wire brush back and forth through it several times and again pump water through the bore. The bore and chamber are then thoroughly dried and cleaned with fresh patches. The bore should then be turned to the light and carefully examined for metal fouling from the jackets of bullets, which appears as little specks. If there be any metal fouling the rifle should be turned in for expert cleaning. Metal fouling is unusual with the ammunition now issued. 

14. On the Range. Wipe the bore and chamber with a clean patch before the first firing. Never leave a patch, rag, cork or other obstruction in the bore. Take care to see that no dust, mud, water, snow, etc. get into the working parts of the rifle. 

15. In the Field. The rifle is cared for in the field as in garrison. If a cleaning rod is not available the thong and brush (or a rag), are used instead. Always draw the brush or rag through from the breech end to the muzzle. 

Disassembling and Assembling 16. Disassembly for Cleaning. For purposes of cleaning the following parts may be removed by the soldier: Front sight cover, floor plate and follower, gun sling, oiler and thong case, and the bolt. To remove floor plate and follower insert point of cartridge through hole in floor plate, and press on floor plate catch. This releases the floor plate. Raise rear lower end of magazine spring until it clears lug on floor plate, and draw it out of its mortise. Similarly separate the spring from the follower. To reassemble, proceed in reverse order. 

17. The Bolt. To remove and disassemble the bolt place cutoff at center notch, cock piece (by raising and lowering bolt handle), turn safety lock to vertical position, raise bolt handle and draw out the bolt. Press sleeve lock with thumb, unscrew sleeve by turning to left, and remove it. Hold sleeve between forefinger and thumb of left hand, draw cocking piece back slightly with thumb and middle finger of right hand, turn safety lock down to left with forefinger of right hand,

Place cocking piece against the breast, draw back firing pin sleeve with right thumb and forefinger, and hold it thus while removing striker with left hand. Remove firing pin sleeve and mainspring, pull firing pin out of sleeve.

Turn extractor to right, forcing its tongue out of groove in front of bolt, and force extractor forward and off the bolt. [2-1, 2] 

18. To assemble and replace the bolt, turn extractor collar until its lug is on line with safety lug on bolt, insert lug on collar in undercuts in extractor by pushing extractor to rear until its tongue comes in contact with face of bolt, turn extractor to right until it is over right lug, press hook of extractor forward until its tongue enters its groove in the bolt. With safety lock turned down to left, assemble sleeve and firing pin, place cocking piece against the breast and put on mainspring, firing pin sleeve and striker. Hold cocking piece between left thumb and forefinger, and by pressing striker against some rigid object, force cocking piece back until safety lock can be turned to vertical position with right hand. Insert firing pin in bolt and screw up sleeve until sleeve lock enters its notch on the bolt. 

Pull cocking piece back and turn safety lock to vertical position, place cutoff at center notch. Hold the piece under floor plate with left hand, thumb extending over left side of receiver. Take bolt in right hand with safety lock in vertical position and safety lug up. Press rear end of follower down with left thumb and push bolt into receiver. Lower bolt handle and turn safety lock and cutoff down. 

Functioning and Operation 

19. The M1903 rifle is operated entirely by hand. The cutoff being at ON, the magazine is loaded by drawing the bolt fully to the rear, placing one end of a loaded clip of cartridges in the clip slots and forcing them into the magazine with the thumb, until the last cartridge is caught by the receiver. When the magazine is empty the floor plate rises and stops the forward movement of the bolt, thus warning the rifleman of an empty magazine. The magazine can be cut off and the piece used as a single loader by turning down the magazine cutoff. 

20. After firing, the bolt is unlocked by pulling up its handle. It is then drawn fully to the rear, which extracts and ejects the empty cartridge case. The bolt is then pushed to the front, which forces the top cartridge from the magazine into the chamber, and locked by pushing the handle all the way down. The operation of the bolt also cocks the piece, and it is now ready to fire. The piece may also be cocked by pulling back the cocking piece. To send the bolt home on an empty chamber and uncock the rifle, cut off the magazine before extracting the empty case. close and lock the bolt and pull the trigger. To unload the magazine operate the bolt without pulling the trigger until the magazine is empty. 

CHAPTER III. THE U. S. RIFLE, M1. MECHANICAL TRAINING Description and Nomenclature 

Then 

21. This weapon is commonly called the Garand Rifle, after its inventor, John

C. Garand. It is now the standard weapon of the U. S. Army. It is a semiautomatic or self-loading weapon, gas operated, clip fed, and air cooled. In general this weapon is very similar to the M1903 (Springfield) Rifle. It has the same exterior dimensions, fires the same ammunition, and has the same ballistic properties. (See 

Chapter II). Its weight is slightly greater, being about 9 lbs. without the bayonet. 

The rifle is capable of firing up to 30 aimed shots per minute in the hands of a properly trained soldier. Considerably higher rates have been attained by experts. 5 6 2 89 27 1415 13 12 16 17 22 21 20 26 25 24 23

MRIFLE. MECHANICAL TRAINING 

93 The mechanism of the rifle absorbs much of the shock of recoil, reducing fatigue of the rifleman. The principal characteristic of this weapon is its automatic operation, which enables it to deliver instantly a large volume of accurate fire on any target. Its effective range is limited by the vision of the rifleman to about 600 yards. 

22. The names of the parts referred to in mechanical training are readily learned as instruction proceeds. The principal external parts are shown in Plate 3. Other parts are shown in Plates 4, 5, 6, and 7. 

23. The Sight. The sight is the usual tangential type, adjustable for range and windage, with automatic correction for drift. The front sight is a thin blade, fixed in position. The elevating knob of the rear sight, on the right, is graduated in hundreds of yards up to 1200. The windage knob, on the left, has graduations each representing 4 minutes of angle. Both knobs are provided with click detents, each "click" corresponding approximately to one minute of angle or 1 inch at 100 yds. One click of the windage knob corresponds to a quarter point of windage, as in the tables in the scorebook. Arrows on the knobs indicate the direction of rotation for desired changes. The rear sight is mounted on the rear part of the receiver, bringing the aperture close to the eye and giving a long sight radius (distance between front and rear sights, about 27½ ins.). The rear sight may be set for correct range reading (usually at 200 yds.) as explained in Par. 162. Battle sight for this weapon corresponds to a range of 300 yds. 

24. Ammunition. The M1 rifle uses the same ammunition as the M1903. (Par. 9.) Guard and blank ammunition do not develop sufficient pressure to operate the mechanism, and must be fired singly. 

25. Individual Safety Precautions. If the M1 rifle is not to be at once fired it should be cleared. A rifle should never be carried or left loaded unless ordered by proper authority. If a rifle is carried loaded and not to be at once fired (on the firing line) the safety is set in the rear position. Any rifle should be assumed to be loaded until it has been examined and found to be clear. Never point a rifle in such direction that an accidental discharge would cause injury or damage. 

Never leave obstructions of any kind in the bore, and make sure there are none before firing. Never use ammunition that is defective in any way, dirty, wet, or warm. 

Never oil or grease ammunition or chamber of piece while firing. For precautions on the range see also Par. 163. 

26. Scope of Course. Mechanical training includes assembling and disassembling, care and cleaning, functioning, operation, immediate action and stoppages, spare parts and accessories. It is the training required to insure that the soldier will be able to maintain his weapon in good working order. It should be taken up as soon as possible after he receives his rifle, and must be completed before he does any firing. 

27. Care to Be Exercised. Force is unnecessary in disassembling and assembling the rifle, and its use is prohibited. Neither will the piece be disassembled or assembled against time (within a short time limit). Instruction blindfolded may be given to men who have passed their tests. When disassembling the piece all parts will be laid out on a smooth, clean surface in the proper order for assembling. 

28. Organization and Methods of Instruction. Men are organized into groups under their officers or selected noncommissioned officers as instructors. Other noncommissioned officers supervise and assist as directed. Corporals supervise their squads. The instructor explains each step and demonstrates it or causes it to be demonstrated. He then causes one man of the group to perform the step while he again explains it. All men then perform the step under the supervision of the noncommissioned officers. This is continued until all are proficient in the particular operation. Men whose progress is very slow may be placed under special instructors. 

Care and Cleaning 

29. In Garrison and Camp. This includes ordinary care to maintain the serviceability and appearance of the rifle. It should be cleaned and protected after every march, drill, or tour of guard duty, especially if exposed to rain or dust. 

No plug in the muzzle, and no protective covering of any kind will be used, except a sheet while barracks are being swept. The rifle should not be touched unnecessarily with moist hands, nor exposed to damp air. The disassembly permitted for cleaning purposes is stated in Par. 40. 

30. The bore is cleaned from the muzzle with a cleaning rod. If the rod used is longer than that prescribed, the face of the bolt must be protected, as by blocking the follower and covering the face of the bolt with a rag stuffed into the receiver. Attach a cloth patch to the cleaning rod, and run back and forth in the bore several times, taking care not to foul the gas port. Repeat with fresh patches until several come out perfectly clean. Saturate a patch with sperm oil or a light weight machine oil, and push through the bore, the muzzle being up so that some oil will enter the gas port. Clean out all screwheads and crevices with a small,

RIFLE TRAINING 

soft brush, or toothpick. Polish the stock occasionally with a little linseed oil in the palm of the hand. Clean all metal surfaces with a dry cloth, and then wipe with a cloth moistened with a little sperm oil. Outer surfaces are wiped clean and dry. 

31. Preparatory to Firing. The procedure is similar to that just described, except that lubricating grease (in a tube) is applied lightly with a cloth to many of the moving parts, instead of sperm oil. (See list below.) 

32. The procedure is as follows: (1) Dismount main groups. (2) Clean the bore and oil lightly. Do not oil the chamber. (3) Remove carbon from gas cylinder plug and piston head. (4) Thoroughly clean and lightly oil metal parts. Use sperm oil in warm weather, aircraft lubrication oil in cold weather. (5) Apply a thin, uniform film of the lubricating grease (mentioned above) to the following parts: (a) Bolt lugs (locking and operating). (b) Bolt guides. (c) Cocking cam on bolt. (d) Compensating spring. (e) Contact surfaces of barrel and operating rod. (f) Operating rod cam. (g) Operating rod guide groove in receiver. (h) Operating rod spring. DO NOT apply grease to the follower slide or under surface of the bolt. (6) Assemble the piece and wipe outer surfaces with a lightly oiled rag. 

33. After Firing. The bores of rifles that have been fired should be cleaned not later than the same evening, and this should be repeated on each of three following days. The cleaning should be done as soon as possible after firing. The procedure is as follows: Hold the rifle muzzle down so no water will enter the gas port. Run several patches saturated with warm, soapy water, or warm water, through the bore. Remove the patch holder from the cleaning rod (M3) and substitute the cleaning brush. Run it back and forth all the way through the bore several times. Remove the brush and again run wet patches through the bore, following this with dry patches until they come through clean and dry. 

Remove patches at breech end. Saturate a patch with sperm oil and push it down the bore, the muzzle being up so oil will enter the gas port. If metal fouling is detected in the bore, turn the rifle in for proper cleaning. 

34. This immediate cleaning will be followed as soon as possible by a further cleaning, repeated on three following days. If the rifle is to be fired next day, apply the cleaning prescribed as preparatory to firing. If the rifle is not to be fired for several days repeat the procedure described in the preceding paragraph, and also perform the following.

Chamber. Insert cleaning patch deep in slot of chamber cleaning tool. 

Lay tool with patch in palm of left hand, close hand over it, and give tool three turns to the right, thus wrapping patch around all its metal surfaces. Make sure the patch is securely wrapped, and clean by twisting the tool, with patch, in the chamber.

Gas Cylinder and Plug. Excess deposits of carbon must be removed as often as the amount of firing makes necessary. SPLINE TYPE. Remove the gas cylinder lock screw, and scrape off carbon with the screwdriver blade of the combination tool. The gas cylinder lock may be removed, and the lock screw reinserted in the cylinder and turned in enough to break loose the carbon. The inside of the gas cylinder should be wiped clean and oiled. A few drops of oil between the rear gas cylinder lug and the operating rod, with the muzzle depressed, will be sufficient if the rifle is to be fired next day. Hand-operate the rod a few times to spread the oil. The exterior finish should be cleaned and lightly oiled, and the sight wiped clean. SCREW-ON TYPE. Scrape carbon from exposed surface of gas cylinder, plug, and piston head, after protracted firing. Clean the gas cylinder plug and grooves in the cylinder. A sharp bladed instrument, as a mess kit knife, should be used to remove carbon from plug and piston head. DO NOT remove the gas cylinder. It is cleaned with the rod and a patch in the same manner as the bore. 

Exterior Surfaces. Rub off the exterior parts with a dry cloth. Wipe all metal surfaces with sperm oil, the stock and hand guards with linseed oil, and the sling with neatsfoot oil. 

35. On the Range and in the Field. The rifle should be kept clean, and in particular any dirt or obstructions, patches or plugs, should be kept out of the chamber and bore. If the rifle gives indication of insufficient lubrication, as when empty cases are ejected to the right rear, apply lubricating grease to the parts listed in Par. 32(5). Keep a light film of sperm oil on all other metal parts. Remove carbon as necessary. Usually it will be unnecessary, in the field, to remove any parts for cleaning except the trigger housing group and the gas cylinder plug. 

But if the rifle becomes very dirty it may be disassembled into its three main groups. (Par. 40). 

36. During range firing specially qualified men should be in charge of the cleaning of rifles. 

37. Preparation for Storage. The rifles should be very thoroughly cleaned and dried, and thereafter only wooden parts should be touched by hand in moving and packing. If the rifles are to be in storage not over six weeks sperm oil is most suit-

MRIFLE. DISASSEMBLING AND ASSEMBLING 

95 able as a preservative for metal parts. Rust preventive compound will preserve the metal parts for a year under reasonable climatic and storage conditions. It is best applied to the bore with the cleaning brush. 

38. Cleaning Rifles Received from Storage. Use light oil or dry cleaning solvent to remove all oil or rust preventive compound. Every part and crevice should be thoroughly cleaned, and the solvent thoroughly removed. Then clean the rifle as prescribed for garrison or preparatory to firing. (Pars. 29 and 31)

Disassembling and Assembling 39. Authorized Disassembling. The disassembling that may be performed by the individual soldier is restricted to that necessary for the proper care of his rifle, and this course is limited to such authorized disassembly. The soldier is ordinarily prohibited from performing the following operations: Disassembling the stock group, follower assembly, rear sight, or trigger housing group; or removal or dismounting of the clip latch, gas cylinder, gas cylinder lock, or front sight. 

40. The authorized operations which may be performed by the soldier without supervision, will be conducted in the following sequence. 

Dismounting the three main groups. (1) Remove trigger housing group. (2) Separate stock group from barrel and receiver group. 

Disassembling the barrel and receiver group. (1) Remove follower rod with compensating spring attached. (2) Remove compensating spring from follower rod. (3) Remove operating rod spring. (4) Remove follower arm pin. (5) Remove bullet guide, follower arm and operating rod catch assembly. (6) 

Remove follower with slide attached. (7) Remove operating rod. (8) Remove bolt assembly. [4] 

Disassembling_the_bolt assembly. (1) Remove extractor. (2) Remove extractor spring and plunger. (3) Remove ejector with spring attached. (4) Remove firing pin. 

41. Dismounting the Main Groups. Grasp rifle with left hand near balance, base of trigger housing included in the grip. Place butt against left thigh. Grasp rear part of trigger guard with right thumb and forefinger, press downward, unlatching trigger guard from trigger housing. Swing trigger guard away from housing to extreme open position. Pull out trigger housing group. Grasp rifle with left hand over rear sight, muzzle down and barrel to left. Strike and grasp small of stock with right hand, separating stock from barrel and receiver group. 

42. Disassembling the Barrel and Receiver Group. [4] Place group on a smooth surface, barrel down and muzzle to left. Grasp follower rod with left thumb and forefinger, and disengage from follower arm by pressing toward muzzle.

Withdraw follower rod, with compensating spring attached, to right. If necessary, remove compensating spring from follower rod by grasping spring in left hand, and twisting rod toward body with right hand, exerting a slight pull to the right.

43. Drift follower arm pin from its seat by starting with point of a cartridge or drift of combination tool, held in right hand and applied on far side of receiver. 

Pull out pin from near side, with left hand. Grasp bullet guide, follower arm, and operating rod catch assembly, and pull to left until these parts disengage. Lift them out and separate them. (Do not remove accelerator from operating rod catch assembly.) Lift out follower with slide attached. Do not separate them. 

44. Turn barrel and receiver assembly up, muzzle to front and operating rod handle to right. Grasp handle with right hand, thumb on top and pointing to front, and forefinger underneath and bent to point to rear. Pull operating rod slowly

.96

RIFLE TRAINING 

to rear, forcing handle up and away from receiver. This allows operating rod to disengage from bolt when lug on rod is aligned with dismount notch in upper surface of operating rod guide groove. When rod is out of guide groove, swing handle down about one inch and remove rod and piston assembly downward to rear. (The operating rod is intentionally bent. Do not try to straighten it.) 

45. Push bolt assembly to about one inch from extreme forward position, where front surface of left locking lug of bolt just clears rear of locking lug on receiver. 

Grasp bolt by lug on right front and lift up and out to right, with a slight rotary motion. 

46. Disassembling the Bolt Assembly. Grasp bolt assembly in left hand, holding firing pin in place with little finger, extractor to right, front end up; thumb on front of bolt to prevent ejector flying out. Insert point of cartridge or screwdriver of combination tool between lower edge of extractor and cartridge seat flange on bolt, and pry extractor out until ejector snaps against thumb. (Be careful that ejector is held by thumb, as its spring is compressed enough to inflict injury). Remove extractor. Lift out extractor spring plunger, with spring attached. Separate only when necessary. Lift out ejector, with spring attached. Separate only when necessary. Remove firing pin from rear of bolt. 

47. The rifle and its component groups are assembled in the reverse order of disassembly. 

48. Assembling the Bolt Assembly. Insert firing pin in bolt, making sure that tang enters slot in rear of bolt. Grasp bolt in left hand, top up, extractor recess to right, holding firing pin in place with little finger. Insert ejector with spring attached. Insert extractor spring and plunger. Set stud of extractor in its hole in the bolt, without forcing it against ejector spring. Place drift of combination tool in left groove of bolt with ejector in cut on face of tool. Press down on combination tool so as to compress ejector spring and align ejector, then with left thumb push extractor in until extractor plunger is seated. This operation should be performed only with the combination tool. 

49. Assembling the Barrel and Receiver Group. Incline barrel and receiver group about 45°, sights up, muzzle up and to front. Holding bolt assembly by operating lug with front of bolt slightly above and to right of its extreme forward position in receiver, insert rear end in its bearing on bridge of receiver, rotating it (counterclockwise) sufficiently to allow tang of firing pin to clear top of bridge. Rotate bolt (clockwise) slowly, at same time sliding to rear. Guide left locking lug of bolt into groove just to rear of lug on left side of receiver, and right locking lug onto its bearing in receiver, seating the bolt. Slide bolt assembly to extreme rear position. 

50. Turn barrel and receiver assembly in left hand, bottom up. With right hand grasp operating rod handle, hold it up, and insert piston head about % inch into gas cylinder, making sure operating rod handle is to left of receiver. Hold barrel and receiver assembly in left hand and rotate it to the right until barrel is uppermost. 

With right hand adjust operating rod so that camming recess on its rear end fits over operating lug of bolt. Press operating rod forward and downward until bolt is seated in its forward position. 

51. Place barrel and receiver assembly on a smooth surface, barrel down and muzzle to left. Replace follower, with slide attached so that its guide ribs fit into their grooves in receiver, square hole in follower to the right. Follower slide rests on bottom surface of bolt when assembly is correct. 

52. With left hand replace bullet guide so that its shoulders fit in their slots in receiver, and hole in projecting lug is in line with holes in receiver. 

53. With left hand replace follower arm by passing studded end through slot in bullet guide, and inserting studs in their grooves in front end of follower. Place forked end of follower arm in position astride projecting lug on bullet guide with the pin holes in alignment. Insert rear arm of operating rod catch through clearance cut in bullet guide, making sure that its rear end is underneath forward stud on clip latch, which projects into receiver opening. Align holes in operating rod catch, follower arm and bullet guide, with those in receiver. Insert follower arm pin in near side of receiver and press pin home. 

54. If separated, insert operating rod spring in operating rod. Assemble follower rod and compensating spring by grasping spring in left hand and inserting rod with right hand, twisting the two together so that spring is fully seated. Grasp knurled portion of follower rod with left thumb and forefinger, forked end to right, hump down. Insert left end of follower rod in operating rod spring, push to left and seat forked end against studs on follower arm. Hump on follower rod must be in slot in operating rod catch. 

55. Replacing the Main Groups. Insert U-shaped flange of stock ferrule in its seat in lower band. Pivoting on this point, guide and press barrel and receiver group into position in stock. Insert trigger housing group, trigger guard in open position, into opening in stock. Seat it securely and close and latch trigger guard. 

56. Test of Assembly. Pull back operating handle and hold in rearmost position; press down on follower and allow bolt to go fully home. Set safety in rearmost

MRIFLE. FUNCTIONING 

97 position, and pull trigger; hammer should not fall. Set safety in foremost position, and pull trigger. Hammer should fall. Functioning 57. Introduction. Functioning means the mechanical operations by which the rifle fires and reloads itself. These operations are simple, and described in nontechnical language, that can be readily understood by the soldier. Instruction in functioning should follow instruction in care and cleaning, disassembling and assembling. 

58. Dummy Cartridges. Practice dummy cartridges are used for instruction in functioning. The use of range dummies is not permitted. 

59. Cycle of Operations. Functioning consists of a cycle of operations, continuously repeated for each successive shot in the same manner and sequence. The cycle begins with the piece loaded, cocked and ready to fire. The rifleman initiates 1 2 11 10 

1. Bolt 

2. Receiver 

3. Barrel 

4. Bore

Plate 5. Principal Operating Parts of M1 Rifle 

5. Gas cylinder plug. Gas port 

6. Gas chamber 

7. Gas piston 

8. Operating rod 

9. Operating rod spring 

10. Slotted cam 

11. Extension of bolt locking lug the cycle by pulling the trigger. The rifle then fires, its moving parts move to the rear, extracting and ejecting the empty cartridge case. They then move to the front, loading a fresh cartridge into the chamber and closing the breech, after which the automatic action ceases until the rifleman again squeezes the trigger, when the cycle is repeated. Thus the cycle consists of two principal phases: (1) Discharge and rearward movement of the moving parts (recoil). (2) Forward movement of the moving parts (counterrecoil). There is also a secondarv cycle which occurs every 8 shots, or every time a clip of 8 cartridges is fired. The rifle automatically ejects the empty clip, and then remains with its moving parts locked in the rearward position until the rifleman inserts a fresh clip. This releases the moving parts, which resume their automatic action. 

60. Rearward Movement. When the rifle is loaded and the bolt closed, the hammer spring is compressed and the trigger lugs are engaged in the hammer hooks, holding the hammer in the cocked position. This is the condition at the beginning of the cycle. When the trigger is squeezed, the trigger lugs are disengaged from 2 213 6 13 12 1 -11 10 13 12 2 2 12 3 -11 -11 -10 10
