RIFLE TRAINING 

the hammer hooks, and the released hammer, actuated by its spring, rotates about the hammer pin and strikes the firing pin, which transmits the blow to the primer of the cartridge. [6-1] The bolt must be fully rotated to locked position before discharge occurs, as the tang of the firing pin is blocked by the bridge of the receiver, except when aligned with the slot in the bridge. Also, until the bolt is fully rotated to locked position, the hammer is prevented from striking the firing pin by the bolt camming lug on the face of the hammer striking the cam surface in the rear face of the bolt. Finally the safety must be in its forward position where it does not prevent discharge. 

61. The cartridge being fired the bullet passes the gas port, allowing some of the gas generated by the discharge to pass through into the gas cylinder, where it strikes the piston end of the operating rod, forcing it to the rear and compressing the operating rod and compensating springs. 

62. The initial rear movement of the operating rod, about 5/16 inch, is independent of the bolt mechanism, the operating lug sliding in the straight section of the recess in the rod. The operating lug then comes in contact with the cam surface of the recess which cams it up, rotating the bolt counterclockwise and disengaging the locking lugs on the bolt from the corresponding recesses in the receiver. The initial movement of the rod allows the bullet time to leave the muzzle, thus reducing the gas pressure before the bolt is opened. The rotation of the bolt cams the hammer back from the firing pin, and withdraws the firing pin point into the bolt. 

63. As the operating rod continues to the rear it carries the bolt, which slides along the receiver. The empty cartridge case is withdrawn from the chamber by the extractor. [7] When the empty case clears the breech the ejector, pressing continually on the base of the cartridge, ejects the case to the right front by action of the compressed ejector spring. The rear end of the bolt forces the hammer 1 

Plate 7. Rearward Movement of Moving Parts 

2 1. The piece has been fired. The bolt is moving to the rear, extracting the empty shell. 

2. The bolt is in its rearmost position. The last round in the clip has been fired, and the empty clip is being ejected. back and rides over it, thus compressing the hammer spring, and finally stops near the rear end of the receiver. With the bolt in its extreme rearmost position the magazine is uncovered. The follower, actuated by the follower arm and follower rod which transmit pressure from the operating rod spring and compensating spring, then forces the cartridges upward, so that the top cartridge is in the path of the bolt. The rearward movement of the operating rod terminates when the rear of its broad curved section meets the front face of the receiver. 

64. Forward Movement. As the bolt moves forward, actuated by the compressed springs, its lower front face_engages the base of the top cartridge and slides it forward into the chamber. The hammer, under pressure of its spring, rides on the bottom of the bolt, but is caught and held by the trigger lugs, which engage the hammer hooks if pressure on the trigger has been released. [6-2] If pressure on the trigger is NOT released after firing, the sear will engage the rear hammer hooks. Subsequent release of the trigger disengages the sear, and the hammer then slides into engagement with the trigger lugs. 

65. As the bolt moves forward the rim of the cartridge is engaged by the extractor, and the base of the cartridge forces the ejector into the bolt, compressing the ejector spring. The operating lug is cammed downward by the rear surface of the cam recess in the operating rod, rotating the bolt clockwise to engage the locking lugs in the receiver, thus locking the bolt. The operating rod then continues forward for the last 5/16 inch, when the rear of the straight section of the recess in the rod contacts the operating lug of the bolt. The compensating spring prevents the hooks on the follower rod being disengaged from the follower arm during the forward movement. This completes the major cycle, and the rifle is now again ready to fire. 

66. Ejection of the Empty Cartridge Clip. When the last round of a clip is fired the rearward motion takes place as heretofore described. In its movement to the rear the bolt clears the top of the receiver, and as the clip is now empty, the follower is pushed to its extreme upper position by the action of the follower arm, follower rod, and the compensating and operating rod springs. In this process the follower rod cams the forward end of the operating rod catch upward to engage the notch in the operating rod, holding the rod in its rearmost position. At the

MRIFLE. OPERATION 

99 same time the rear arm of the operating rod catch pivots about the follower arm pin and forces down the stud on the front end of the clip latch, rotating the latch and disengaging it from the clip. Thereupon the empty clip is ejected by the clip ejector. [7] With the operating rod held in its rear position, and the receiver empty, a loaded clip is inserted by the rifleman. 

67. Release of the Operating Rod Catch. When the loaded clip is forced down into the receiver it depresses the follower. As the follower nears the bottom of the receiver, the front end of the follower arm contacts and rotates the accelerator. 

As the accelerator rotates about its pin in the operating rod catch, it bears on a lug on the bullet guide and forces down the front end of the operating rod catch, which releases the operating rod. The operating rod moves forward under the action of the springs. The rear arm of the operating rod catch swings upward and releases the stud on the front end of the clip latch. The clip latch then rotates under the pressure of its spring, its rear lug moving inward to engage the notch in the clip, locking the clip in the magazine. 

68. Instruction in operation of instruction in care and cleaning. instruction.

Operation the rifle is given at any convenient time after Practice dummy cartridges are used for this 69. To Load the Cartridge Clip. Insert 8 cartridges in the clip, with the base of each close to the rear wall, and the inner rib of the clip engaging the extractor groove in the cartridge. The top cartridge when loaded may be either on the right or left side, but the clip is more easily inserted if it is on the right. 

70. To Load the Rifle. Loading is performed with the piece locked (safety in rear position), except in sustained firing. Hold the rifle with the left hand at the balance. With the right forefinger pull the operating rod smartly to the rear until it catches. With the right hand place a fully loaded clip on top of the follower. 

Place the right side of the hand against the operating rod handle, and with the thumb press the clip into the receiver until it engages the clip latch. Swing the thumb to the right to clear the bolt and release the operating rod handle. Closure may be assisted by pushing the handle forward with the heel of the hand. The receiver is loaded only with full clips. 

71. To Unload the Rifle. To unload a cartridge from the chamber hook the right thumb over the operating handle and pull it fully to the rear, thus extracting and ejecting the cartridge. 

72. To remove a loaded clip from the receiver (magazine), hold the piece in the right hand, thumb on the operating rod handle and fingers around the trigger guard, holding the operating rod in its rearmost position. Place the left hand over the receiver, back of hand to the right, and press the clip latch with the thumb. 

The clip will then be ejected upward into the hand. To close the bolt on an empty chamber, press down the top cartridge and allow the bolt to ride forward over it, making sure the bolt closes fully. To clear the rifle means to remove all cartridges from chamber and magazine. 

73. To Operate the Rifle as a Single Loader. The receiver being empty, hold the piece in the left hand, and pull the operating rod to the rear until it catches. 

Place a cartridge in the chamber and seat it with the thumb. With the right side of the right hand against the operating rod handle and the fingers extended and joined force the handle slightly to the rear, depress the follower with the thumb, and allow the bolt to ride forward about an inch over the follower. Remove the thumb from the follower, release the operating rod handle and push it forward with the heel of the hand. Make certain that the bolt is fully closed. 

74. To Fire the Rifle. Squeeze the trigger for each shot. After the 8th shot the empty clip is automatically ejected, and the bolt remains open. 

75. To Set the Rifle at Safe. A loaded rifle should be kept locked until the instant of firing. To lock the rifle set the safety in its rearmost position inside the trigger guard. The trigger then cannot be pulled. The rifle, when locked, can be loaded and operated by hand, but cannot be fired. To unlock the piece, set the safety in its foremost position. [6] 

76. To Set the Rear Sight. The rear sight is set for range by turning the elevating knob on the left side. Force the knob outward while turning. The knob is graduated in hundreds of yards from 0 to 1200, the even numbers (200, 400, etc.) being numbered. The sight is set for windage by turning the windage knob on the right side. Each windage graduation represents an angular movement of 4′ of arc. 

Both knobs are provided with click detents. Each click corresponds to about one minute of arc or one inch on the target for each 100 yds. of range. Arrows on the knobs indicate the directions in which to turn to move the strike of the bullet up or down or to right or left.

Immediate Action and Stoppages 

77. Introduction.

The purpose of this training is to enable the rifleman to rem-

RIFLE TRAINING 

edy by simple operations the majority of the stoppages or failures to fire, that may occur during combat. If the stoppage cannot be readily reduced by the procedure prescribed, the piece should be turned in for examination and repair. Extreme measures will not be taken by the rifleman. It will be noted that all the common stoppages are caused by dirt, lack of lubrication, loose, defective or broken parts or ammunition. Instruction in immediate action and other measures for reduction of stoppages must be completed before any firing is done. 

78. Immediate Action. Immediate action is the unhesitating application of a probable remedy for a stoppage, without investigation or only a very cursory in vesigation of the cause of the stoppage. The procedure is as follows. 

(1) The rifle fails to fire when trigger is squeezed. With palm up, and using little finger, pull operating rod fully back. Release rod, and if bolt goes fully home, aim, and attempt to fire. Hold the hand so that it cannot be struck by the operating rod in case of a hangfire. (2) The bolt cannot be fully locked after performing above operation. 

Pull operating rod fully back. Look for battered cartridge, or obstruction or dirt on face of bolt, in chamber, or in locking lug recess. Discard cartridge, remove obstruction, reload, aim, and attempt to fire. (3) The bolt locks, but the rifle still fails to fire. Pull operating rod fully back. If no cartridge is ejected, reduce obstruction in clip by pressing and rotating upper cartridge. Reload, aim, and attempt to fire. (4) The rifle fails to feed. Keep it in operation by manipulating the rod, until there is opportunity to investigate the cause of the malfunctioning. 

79. The above procedure of immediate action is almost always sufficient to keep the rifle in operation. If it does not, the more detailed examinations listed below are made as opportunity permits. 

80. Stoppages. Prevention is better than cure in the case of stoppages. Proper care, cleaning and operation of the weapon will usually avoid them. To reduce stoppages that do not respond to immediate action, the rifleman should understand the functioning of the weapon and the causes of stoppages. 

81. Failure to Fire. Failure to fire is usually caused by: (1) Defective ammunition. (2) Defective firing pin. (3) Bolt not fully closed.

Dis- 82. If the cartridge primer is deeply indented the cartridge is defective. card it. If the primer is not indented or but slightly indented, the firing pin may be short or broken, or the bolt may not be fully closed. Check for dirt or obstructions, and remove any that are found. If the piece is clean and lubricated, check the firing pin, and replace it if defective. 

83. If the piece fails to fire and the operating handle cannot be moved by a sharp blow of the hand, the firing pin may be broken, and having come out of its seat become jammed between the rear of the bolt and the top of the receiver. 

Remove the trigger housing, and the pin will usually fall out. If it does not, separate the barrel and receiver group from the stock group and remove the firing pin. If the trigger housing is not easily removed do not use force. The firing pin is probably caught under the lug on the left of the hammer. Open the trigger guard as far as possible without force. Turn the rifle upside down, shake it and oscillate the trigger guard, until the housing comes out readily. Then remove the firing pin. 

84. Failure to Feed. Failure to feed results when the bolt does not recoil far enough to pick up a new round. There are several causes of this. They usually are indicated by: (1) Bolt failing to go fully home. (2) Bolt going fully home. (1) Stoppages of the first type may be caused by a battered cartridge, dirt in the locking recesses, and obstruction on the face of the bolt, a dirty chamber, or part of a ruptured cartridge case remaining in the chamber. Investigate, and apply the necessary remedy. Sometimes this stoppage is caused by a clip which has lost its spring tension and does not hold the cartridge in line. The cartridge will be found with its point on one side of the entrance to the chamber. Remove the cartridge, and remove and discard the clip. (2) Sometimes in a stoppage of the second type the empty case is not ejected, and is fed back into the chamber. This is due to lack of lubrication, excessive friction, or insufficient gas pressure due to carbon in the gas port. The bolt has not moved far enough to the rear. Remove carbon and lubricate all parts, as heretofore explained. 

85. Failure to Extract. Failures to extract are usually caused by: (1) Very dirty chamber. (2) Very dirty ammunition. (3) Improper assembly (failure to replace the extractor plunger and spring). (4) Hot barrel. (5) Broken extractor. 

86. The bolt may be found fully locked with an empty case in the chamber. 

Most failures to extract can be remedied by pushing the operating rod fully forward and then pulling it sharply to the rear. If this does not extract the case, use the combination tool or cleaning rod. 

87. Sometimes the extractor rips through the base of the case, leaving it in the chamber, and the bolt tries to feed in a fresh cartridge. This must be removed to get at the empty case.

MRIFLE. STOPPAGES. SPARE PARTS 

101 88. When a dirty chamber or ammunition is evident, clean the chamber and clean or discard the ammunition. 

89. Faulty assembly or a broken extractor will cause recurring failures to extract. Replace missing or broken parts. 

90. Table of Stoppages. The following table of stoppages, causes and remedies, will be found helpful. It includes those that have been discussed, and others that occur less frequently.

Spare Parts and Accessories 

91. Spare Parts. All parts of the rifle eventually become unserviceable by wear and breakage. A set of spare parts and assemblies is furnished with each rifle. 

They include the parts which fail most frequently. They will be kept clean and lightly oiled. Assemblies must be complete and ready to use. Whenever any part or assembly is used for minor repairs it will be replaced, to keep the spare parts set complete at all times. The allowance of spare parts is prescribed in regulations. 

Riflemen are forbidden to make repairs other than replacements with the spare parts issued. 

92. Bayonet and Scabbard. These are not essential to the operation of the piece, but are furnished with each rifle. as- 93. Accessories. Accessories include the tools for disassembling and sembling, cleaning and reducing stoppages; also the gun sling, arm locker and rack, spare parts container, etc. The tools and more important accessories are described below. 

94. Barrel Reflector. This is a small device equipped with a mirror. When inserted in the chamber it gives a reflection of the bore. 

95. Brush and Thong. These are used in cleaning the rifle. The case is partitioned, one end containing oil and an oil dropper, the other the tip, weight, thong and brush. 

96. Cleaning Rod. Cleaning Brush. The cleaning rod has a handle at one end and is threaded at the other to receive the patch holder section or brush. It is of such length as to avoid damage to the bolt or follower. The cleaning brush is used to clean the bore. 

97. Combination Tool. This tool consists of three parts: the handle, the chamber cleaning tool, and the screwdriver blade. The slot in the chamber cleaning tool is for the attachment of a patch. The movable screwdriver blade is used for the gas cylinder plug screw and various other screws. The notched blade of the handle is used on the rear sight nut. The small cylindrical stud is used to drift out pins, and in connection with the V-shaped groove in the handle, to assemble the extractor

RIFLE TRAINING 

and ejector. The curved undercut lug or hook, called the hand extractor, is used for necessary hand extraction from the chamber. 

98. Ruptured Cartridge Extractor. This tool has the form of a cartridge, and consists of a spindle, head and sleeve. To use the tool, clear all live cartridges from the rifle. Insert the tool through the ruptured case, and push it into the chamber. 

Close the bolt gently. The extractor then engages the tool. When the operating handle is drawn back, the tool is extracted, bringing with it the ruptured case. 

99. Gun Sling. The sling, attached to the swivels on the rifle, consists of a long and a short strap, both adjustable to suit the needs of the individual user. 

CHAPTER IV. RIFLE MARKSMANSHIP. KNOWN-DISTANCE TARGETS Scope of the Course 

100. It is easy to shoot well when one has been shown how. It requires no talent, but only a normal physique and intelligence. It cannot be learned by merely practicing on the range. Left to himself, the average man instinctively does the wrong things, and "practice" in doing them over and over is a waste of time and ammunition. We teach soldiers to shoot, not on the range, but before they go on the range. The prospective rifleman is shown exactly how to perform each of the operations in rifle shooting, and is thoroughly drilled in doing them correctly. This is his preparatory training, by far the most important part of the course. After that a very moderate amount of practice on the range enables him to apply the principles that he has been taught, and to satisfy himself that if he does so intelligently and conscientiously, he will be a good shot from the very beginning. He thus gains a very pleasant and well justified confidence in his ability to shoot. 

Finally he has a test, record practice, as a result of which he is classified according to his proficiency. The complete course, then, includes very thorough preparatory training, instruction practice and record practice on the range. During the course the soldier learns the nomenclature of his rifle and how it functions, the effects of weather conditions (especially wind), how to set and change the settings of his sights, how to keep a record of his firing in his score book. He is also taught how to care for and clean his rifle, a very important part of his duty. The purpose of the course is not to develop a small number of expert shots, but to make every man a good shot and a competent instructor or coach. 

101. Method of Instruction. A single instructor can teach rifle shooting to as many men as can see and hear him. The instruction proceeds one step at a time. 

The instructor first explains the operation to be done. He then demonstrates how it is done. He then causes each man to practice until he can do it properly. In each operation he examines (questions) and tests the men to make sure they understand the operation. The final test is record practice on the range. 

102. Coach and Pupil. In every step of the training, except when firing for record, each pupil has a coach beside him. The coach takes the same position as his pupil, on his right. [14] He carefully watches every detail of the pupil's performance, notes his mistakes and helps him to correct them. The coach need not be a specially qualified man, unless the pupil is very backward. Ordinarily he is simply one of the men taking instruction. The coach and pupil occasionally change places, that is, the men work in pairs, alternately as coach and pupil, and the method is helpful to both. 

103. Blackening the Sights. For all preparatory exercises involving aiming, and for all firing, both sights will be coated with a deposit of dull carbon by holding them for a few seconds in a small flame produced by a candle, wax match, bit of camphor or shoe paste.

PREPARATORY TRAINING 

104. Every man who is to fire must take the entire course of preparatory training before firing on the range. He must be proficient in mechanical training before taking marksmanship training. It is in the preparatory training especially that the men are taught the principles of good shooting. Its importance cannot be exaggerated. 

105. The Six Steps. Preparatory marksmanship training includes six major steps as follows: (1) Sighting and aiming exercises. (2) Position exercises. 

(3) Trigger squeeze exercises. (4) Rapid fire exercises. (5) Instruction in the effects of wind, sight changing, and the use of the score book. (6) Examination before starting range practice. All steps are taken in the sequence given, except (5) which may be given at any time preceding the examination (6). 

106. Each step begins with a talk by the instructor, and a demonstration of the instruction to be given. He fully explains each operation, and the reasons why it is necessary. The instruction that follows is individual and thorough. Each man must fully understand each step and be able to explain it in his own words. All mistakes must be corrected and proper habits acquired during preparatory training.

RIFLE MARKSMANSHIP. PREPARATORY TRAINING 

103 107. Equipment. The necessary equipment, all readily improvised, includes the following items for each four men: 1 sighting bar [9]; 1 rifle rest; 1 small sighting disc; 1 10 in. sighting disc; 2 small aiming targets (A or D, 1,000 in.); 1 small box; 

1 frame covered with white paper (for long range shot groups); 2 sandbags; 2 pencils; 20 rounds practice dummy ammunition (32 rounds for M1); 4 score books; 

1 form showing state of training; material for blackening sights; 4 ft. of twine for tying back triggers (for M1903 only). Practice dummy ammunition only is used in preparatory training. For general use the following equipment is provided: one rapid fire target with curtain for each 3 squads; one each A, B, and D targets on frames for score book exercises; one aiming device per squad; cleaning and preserving materials.

First Step. Sighting and Aiming 109. First Sighting and Aiming Exercise. The correct alignments of the front and rear sight, both open and peep, and of the sights with the bullseye, are shown in 

คง 

Plate 8. Sight Alignments 

Plate 8. The middle point of the top of the front sight is centered in the aperture of the rear sight. The bottom of the bullseye just touches the middle point of the top of the front sight. This is the only correct alignment. In this exercise the sighting bar [9] is used. The instructor sets the sighting bar to show correct alignments and has each pupil observe them. He then sets on the bar various small errors in alignment, and has the pupils endeavor to detect such errors. The exercise then continues by the coach and pupil method. 

110. Second Sighting and Aiming Exercise. A rifle is placed on a rest improvised from a box, about half filled with sand, and pointed at a sheet of paper mounted on another box about 50 feet away. [10] The instructor takes position as shown, and without touching the rifle, looks through the sights. He directs a marker to move a small sighting disc until its bullseye is in correct alignment with the sights, and commands: HOLD. The pupil then looks through the sights and observes a correct aim. The disc is then moved, and the pupil by commands to the marker, endeavors to bring it back into alignment. The instructor or coach then sets the disc with various small errors and has the pupil endeavor to detect them. In aiming the eye is focused on the target.

Plate 9. The Sighting Bar 

The dimensions of the bar are 1" wide x 2" deep x 4'6" long. The target and rear sight are set in slits in the bar and are movable. The target is 5½", the front sight 6", and the rear sight 26″ from one end. The target and rear sight are of thin metal or stiff cardboard. 3" square. The target is white with a black bullseye, 4" diameter, in the center. The rear sight is black, with a circular aperture, " diameter, in the center. The front sight is an L of thin metal ½" wide and " to " high. The eyepiece is of thin metal with a peep .03" in diameter, ½" below the top. The purpose of the peep is to insure that the eye will be placed always at the same point. The bar or top of the bar, and all parts except the target are painted a dull black. 

111. Third Sighting and Aiming Exercise. Short range shot groups. With the same set-up as in the preceding exercise, the pupil looks through the sights, brings the disc into correct alignment, and commands: HOLD. The instructor then looks through 

Plate 10. Rifle Rest the sights, notes the error of alignment if any, and commands: MARK. The marker makes a mark on the paper with a pencil, through the small hole in the center of the disc, and then moves the disc out of alignment. The procedure is twice repeated and the result is 3 dots forming a triangle. The size and shape of the triangle indicate to the pupil the amount of his error and lack of consistency in aiming. 

The triangle formed by the three dots should be small enough to be covered by the blunt end of a pencil. 

112. Long range shot groups. The exercise is repeated at 200 yards with a 10″ bullseye, and at 500 yards with a 20" bullseye. The resulting triangles are traced on thin paper and sent to the instructor for examination. At 200 yards the triangle should fall within a circle 1½” in diameter, and at 500 yards in one 3½" in diameter. 

113. The Sighting Bar. The sighting bar, used in the first exercise is illustrated in Plate 9. 

114. Sighting Discs. Sighting discs are of three sizes; ins., 10 ins., and 20 ins. in diameter, for ranges of 50 ft., 200 yds. and 500 yds. The 50 ft. disc has a black bullseye, %" diameter, mounted on white paper. The longer range discs are painted black with handles of suitable length. Each disc has a hole large enough to admit the point of a pencil, exactly in its center. 

Second Step. Positions 

115. Correct and comfortable positions are essential to accurate shooting. Position exercises include practice in aiming, but the trigger is not squeezed. The prescribed positions are illustrated in Plate 11. Instruction in positions includes use of the sling, taking up slack in the trigger, holding the breath, aiming, and use of the aiming device. Small bullseyes, set at about 1,000 inches range, and at proper heights for the various positions, are used as aiming points. The authorized positions are shown in Plate 11. 

116. The Aiming Device. This is an instrument which may be mounted on the rifle just in rear of the rear sight, by means of which the coach at the pupil's side can see a reflection of the sights, and check the sight alignment and the steadiness of holding. It is used in all preparatory exercises involving sighting except rapid

RIFLE MARKSMANSHIP. POSITIONS 

105 fire, and may also be used in gallery practice, and even on the range, if the light on the target is good. 

117. The Gun Sling. The gun sling is employed in all firing or simulated firing to steady the aim and reduce fatigue. It should be adjusted as tightly as possible, so that at first it will be uncomfortable. There are two adjustments. [11-3, 4] 

118. The Loop Adjustment. Loosen the lower loop so there will be no pull on it; adjust the upper loop. Twist the upper loop a quarter turn left, pass the left arm through it. Push the loop well up above the biceps, close to the shoulder, and secure by pulling down the leather keeper. Pass the left hand between the sling and the rifle, and regrasp the rifle just below the lower band swivel (M1903) or stock ferrule swivel (M1), so that the sling lies flat along the back of the hand and wrist. 

The lower loop being properly adjusted will not thereafter be changed. 

119. The Hasty Sling Adjustment. Loosen the lower loop. With both hands on the rifle, flip the sling to the left and catch it well up on the left arm. Pass the left hand under and then over the sling and regrasp the rifle so that the sling lies flat along the back of the hand and wrist. 

120. Taking Up the Slack. The slack of the trigger is its first easy movement. It is taken up by a continuous movement of the trigger finger before careful aiming begins. 

121. Holding the Breath. It is most important that the breath be held properly to insure a steady aim. To do this draw in a full breath, let out a little, and then close the throat and press with the air against the closed throat. 

122. General Rules for Positions. (1) To assume any position first face the target and then face half right. In any position the rifle makes an angle of about 1 2 

1. Prone. 2. Sitting. 

Plate 11. Positions 

3. Kneeling. 4. Standing.

NOTE. The hasty sling adjustment is clearly shown in Fig. 4, the loop sling adjustment in Fig. 3. 

45° with the front of the body or the spine. (2) In any position the rifle should point naturally and comfortably at the bullseye. If it does not, change the position. (3) The right hand grasps the small of the stock with the thumb around or along it (M1903), or around it or over the top of the stock (M1), and second joint of forefinger against the trigger. (4) The left hand is just in rear of or against the lower band swivel (M1903), or the stock ferrule swivel (M1), the rifle resting on the palm of the hand, and in the crotch between the thumb and first finger. (5) The left elbow is as nearly under the rifle as possible. (6) The butt of the rifle is held firmly against the right shoulder. (7) The trigger is squeezed (to take up slack only, Par. 

120) with the second (or first) joint of the right forefinger. (8) The right cheek is pressed firmly against the stock, and as far forward as possible. (9) Left-handed men should learn to shoot from the right shoulder, but shooting from the left shoulder may be permitted. (10) The breath is held while aiming. (11) These rules may be modified as necessary to suit the size and conformation of the man. (12) The final rule is that the position must be natural and comfortable, with the rifle pointed at the target. 

123. The Prone Position. The body is flat on the stomach, feet well apart, insides

RIFLE TRAINING 

of feet flat on the ground; shoulders and chest off the ground and supported by the elbows, which are well under the body. [11-1] 

124. The Sandbag Rest Position. The pupil takes the prone position and aims at the target. The coach then places a half filled sandbag, so as to support the forearm, wrist and hand of the pupil. The sandbag is adjusted to the rifleman-not the converse. This position, being very steady, is used during instruction in trigger squeeze and in instruction practice on the range. 

125. The Sitting Position. The rifleman sits with his feet well forward and well apart (16 to 24 inches), heels dug into the ground. The body leans forward, with the upper arms, just above the elbows, braced firmly against the insides of the knees, which press against the arms. The sitting position is used in the field on ground that slopes downward to the front. [11-2] 

126. The Kneeling Position. The rifleman kneels on his right knee and sits on his heel or the side of his foot. The left lower leg is vertical. The left arm rests on the left knee, with the point of the elbow just in front of the knee. The right elbow is approximately at the height of the shoulder. [11-3] 

127. The Standing Position. The body is erect and well balanced, knees straight, feet well apart (12 to 24 inches). The right elbow is approximately at the height of the shoulder. Positions in which the left hand is drawn back against or under the trigger guard with the left elbow against the body are unsteady, and are prohibited. [11-4] 128. Positions in the Field. In the field the soldier takes advantage of any features of the terrain which can afford support for his body or arms, or for the rifle. Trenches should be constructed with berms to provide a rest for the elbows, the rifle being supported by the parapet. 

Assuming Positions Rapidly 129. The Prone Position. Two methods of assuming the prone position rapidly from standing are shown in Plates 12 and 13. 1 2 Plate 12. Assuming the Prone Position Rapidly;

Skirmisher's Method The soldier takes the prone position, marks the points at which his elbows rest, and rises to his feet, keeping them as nearly as possible in the same position. 

1. Sling being adjusted (loop sling), stand at READY. 

2. Throw right foot well back; bend left knee as far as possible; place butt of piece on ground 4 or 5 inches to left and a little in front of spot where right elbow is to rest, retaining grip on piece with both hands. 

3. Lower right elbow to selected spot on ground. 

4. Place left leg alongside right; lower belly to ground and slide body back, spreading feet as necessary. 

5. Raise butt of piece from ground and place firmly against right shoulder. 

6. Lower left elbow to ground. 

130. Assuming the Sitting Position Rapidly. The rifleman takes the sitting position, rises without moving his feet and marks the spot on which he was sitting. 

At the command or signal he drops to the sitting position without moving his feet, easing the fall by placing his right hand on the ground to the right rear of the spot on which he is to sit. Other methods may be used. 

131. Assuming the Kneeling Position Rapidly. If standing, kneel, aim at the target and mark the positions of the feet and the right knee. Stand with the feet in the places marked, and as the target appears, kneel with the right knee in the place marked. 

132. The foregoing methods of taking positions rapidly are used in rapid fire exercises and rapid fire on the range, where the rifleman drops from standing position to sitting, kneeling or prone, as the targets appear. Other methods of assuming

133. Antiaircraft Fire Positions. These are illustrated and explained in Chapter V, Fire on Aerial Targets. 

134. Taking the Prone Position After a Rush. Methods of rising rapidly from the prone position, and assuming the position rapidly after a rush are illustrated and explained in Chapter VII, Field Target Firing. Rushes are not made in range firing on known distance targets. 

135. Position Exercises. The instructor causes the men under instruction to blacken their sights. He explains and demonstrates the sling adjustments and assists the men to adjust their slings. He then explains and demonstrates taking up the slack of the trigger and holding the breath, and how to tell when a man is holding his breath by watching his back. He explains and demonstrates the use of the aiming device. He explains the rules that apply to all positions, and demonstrates each position. He then causes the men to practice these details in each position, and corrects their mistakes. The instruction then proceeds by the coach and pupil method. Small aiming bullseyes are used. They are placed at various heights so that the line of aim will be horizontal for each position. The trigger is not squeezed, except to take up the slack. Coaches check the aim occasionally, with the aiming device. If the aim becomes unsteady the pupil is given a short rest. 

Third Step. Trigger Squeeze 

136. To squeeze the trigger so as to set off the rifle while the sights are correctly aligned on the bullseye or target, is the secret of successful shooting. The tendency of a beginner is to align the sights and then give the trigger a quick jerk, hoping to get the shot off before the sights can get out of alignment. This he never succeeds in doing. In the first place jerking the trigger deranges the aim. In the second place the firer knows that when he jerks the trigger the piece will go off. And inevitably he flinches in anticipation of the severe shock of recoil, which he knows is coming. His muscles unconsciously contract, which spoils his aim, and he closes his eyes so that he does not see the sight alignment at the instant of discharge, and does not know where his rifle was pointed. The correct way to squeeze the trigger, which is by far the most important thing in rifle shooting, is as follows: 

The rifleman first takes up the slack, or easy first motion of the trigger. He then aligns his sights on the bullseye and holds them there while he squeezes the trigger with a gradual steady motion. If the sights get out of alignment he stops squeezing the trigger, but holds it where it is. He brings the sights back into alignment and then continues the trigger squeeze. That is, the trigger is squeezed only when the sights are properly aligned. Finally, perhaps on the second or third squeeze, the rifle goes off. The firer does not know just when this will happen, and so he does not flinch because he does not know when to flinch. When the recoil comes he is knocked backward and he winks, but this does no harm because the bullet is already out of the bore. When he has learned to squeeze the trigger in this way a man is a good shot. 

137. Calling the Shot. If the rifleman squeezes the trigger properly his eye will be open at the instant of discharge and he will see the final alignment of his sights. 

He is thus able to call the shot, or announce where it should strike. If it strikes where

RIFLE TRAINING 

he says it will that shows that he is squeezing the trigger properly and that his sights are correctly set or if not he has the data for an intelligent change of sight setting. Calling the shot is thus an essential accompaniment of a correct trigger squeeze. If a man cannot call his shots he is not squeezing the trigger correctly. 

138. Trigger Squeeze Exercise. The instructor explains the trigger squeeze and calling the shot, emphasizing their importance, and questions the men. The instruction then proceeds by the coach and pupil method, first with the sandbag rest and then in the other positions. The trigger is squeezed and the shot is called in all simulated fire. Small aiming targets are set up, as for position exercises. (Par. 135). 

Fourth Step. Rapid Fire 

139. In rapid fire there is a time limit, normally 10 (M1903) or 16 (M1) aimed shots per minute. Each shot must be aimed and the trigger squeezed as in slow fire, but more rapidly. Speed is acquired by rapidity in operating the bolt (of the M1903) and loading the magazine. The rifleman does not take his eye off the target, except to reload. He should count his cartridges, and not remove his eyes from the target to look into the chamber. This causes loss of time in finding the target again. 

140. Rapid fire exercises are conducted from standing to prone, sitting and kneeling. (Par. 132). 

141. Bolt Operation Exercise. The instructor demonstrates how the bolt is opened and closed in one continuous smooth motion, instead of four separate jerky motions, keeping the rifle at the shoulder and the eye constantly on the target while so doing. 

Instruction then proceeds by the coach and pupil method, in all positions. The piece is not aimed and the trigger is not squeezed, but the right hand regrasps the small of the stock, the piece is raised to the horizontal position, and the cheek is pressed against the stock, each time the bolt is closed. The trigger is tied back to the trigger guard to simulate the squeezing of the trigger for each shot. In the prone and sitting positions the elbows remain in place during the operation of the bolt. Proficiency requires that the bolt be operated 20 times in 20 seconds in prone position. 

This exercise applies to the M1903 rifle only. 

142. Reloading. Reloading is done with a clip from the belt, smoothly and confidently, with the right hand. The cutoff is turned up, to ON, and the bolt is drawn back (M1903). The piece is held firmly in the left hand, muzzle slightly elevated. 

The cartridges are pressed into the magazine with the thumb, the fingers being under the piece. For the M1 rifle the thumb is then swung to the right to clear the bolt, and the operating handle is released. Closure may be assisted by a forward push on the operating handle with the heel of the right hand. 

143. After the pupil is properly instructed in taking positions rapidly, bolt operation, and reloading, additional instruction is given in these items in the rapid fire exercises. 

144. Timing Exercises. With the M1 rifle it is most important that the rifleman learn to time his shots. Timing varies from about 5 seconds per shot for beginners to 

2 seconds for the experienced man. For timing exercises the instructor explains the importance of correct position which keeps the piece pointed at the target, of correct aiming and keeping the eye always on the target, of the coach pressing back the operating handle to cock the piece and then releasing the pressure and permitting it to go forward, of correct trigger squeeze, and of correct timing, and how speed in timing is increased. Timing exercises in all positions, except standing, are then conducted by the coach and pupil method. Following the timing exercises additional practice is given in rapid fire exercises with dummy cartridges, as described in the following paragraphs. 

145. Rapid Fire Exercise with Practice Dummy Cartridges. This exercise simu lates the entire procedure of rapid fire, prone and sitting. The instructor explains and demonstrates how to load the magazine without fumbling and operate the bolt in both positions, while keeping the rifle at the shoulder, the elbows in position and the eye on the target. He cautions against the mistake of "chamber gazing" while operating the bolt, and explains the necessity for counting cartridges, so as to know when the magazine is empty. Otherwise time is wasted in pressing down the follower which locks the bolt when the last cartridge is fired (M1903); or pulling the operating rod handle to the rear until the rod is caught (M1). 

146. Procedure. The group under instruction is paired as coach and pupil, and placed on line. Full size targets are set up at 200 and 300 yds., with arrangements for exposing them to view for the prescribed periods. The instructor announces the range and position. He then commands: 1. With dummy cartridges LOAD 

(or LOCK AND LOAD). 2. Ready on the right. 3. Ready on the left. 4. READY ON THE FIRING LINE. 5. Cease firing. 6. UNLOAD. At the fourth command the pieces, which have been locked, are unlocked. As the targets are exposed each man takes position rapidly and simulates the firing of 10 rounds (16 for the M1). Any cartridges remaining after cessation of fire are unloaded or cleared at command. 

Bolts are left open and sights laid.

RIFLE MARKSMANSHIP. WIND. SIGHT CHANGES 

109 147. Duties of the Coach. The duties of coach in rapid fire exercises are most important. In bolt operation exercises (M1903) the coach sees that: (1) The bolt is operated properly and rapidly. (2) The eye is kept on the target. (3) The right hand and cheek are brought to position and the rifle raised to the horizontal each HVWc bb "મન 

Plate 14. Position of Coach in Rapid Fire Exercise, M1 Rifle time the bolt is closed. (4) The butt cf the rifle is kept against the shoulder. (5) The elbows are kept in position. (6) No attempt is made to aim or squeeze the trigger. 

During rapid fire exercises the coach sees that: (1) The sights are both blackened. (2) The sling is properly adjusted. (3) The correct position is assumed. (4) Slack of the trigger is taken up promptly. (5) The breath is held while aiming. (6) The trigger is properly squeezed. (7) For the M1903 rifle: The bolt is operated properly and rapidly. For the M1 rifle: Each time the pupil squeezes the trigger he promptly presses back the operating rod handle with a sharp motion, ejecting the dummy cartridge, and then releases the pressure to permit the operating rod handle to go forward. (8) The eye of the pupil is on the target, the elbows in place, and the butt against the shoulder, except while reloading. (This is of special importance during bolt operation.) (9) The rifle is reloaded quickly and smoothly without fumbling. 

Fifth Step. Effects of Wind. Sight Changes. Use of the Score Book 

148. Wind is the only weather condition that seriously affects rifle shooting. It blows the bullet out of its path by an amount depending on the direction and velocity of the wind, and the range. The direction of the wind is described by assuming that the firing point is at the center of a large clock dial, face upward on the ground, the target being at 12 o'clock. A 12 o'clock wind is one blowing from the target to the firing point. It has no appreciable effect. A wind blowing square across the range from the right (left) is a 3 (9) o'clock wind. It has a maximum effect on the bullet. 

A wind from 2, 4, 8, or 10 o'clock has almost the effect of a 3 or 9 o'clock wind. A 

1, 5, 7 or 11 o'clock wind has about half the effect. The velocity of the wind is judged by noting the movement of paper or grass tossed into the air, or of dust, dry leaves, or the trees and flags on the range. A wind of less than 4 miles an hour can barely be noticed. A wind which constantly changes direction is called a fishtail. 

149. Having judged the direction and velocity of the wind the rifleman corrects for his first shot by setting off on his wind gauge the windage indicated in the table of wind allowances or wind gauge diagram in his score book. Thereafter he makes corrections according to where his bullet strikes, by the following rules. 

150. The Wind Rule. The range in hundreds of yards multiplied by the velocity of the wind in miles per hour and divided by 10, gives the number of quarter points (clicks) to set on the wind gauge for a 3 or 9 o'clock wind. This is the same value as shown in the wind gauge diagrams in the score book. 

151. The Wind Gauge Rule. One point on the wind gauge moves the point of impact of the bullet 4 inches laterally for each hundred yards of range. This is the same value as shown by the vertical lines of the ruled targets in the score book. Each click of the wind gauge knob of the M1 rifle corresponds to a quarter point of windage. 

152. The Elevation Rule. Changing the elevation on the sight by 100 yards at any range raises or lowers the point of impact on the target by the number of inches equal to the square of the range in hundreds of yards. This is the same value as shown by the horizontal lines of the rule targets in the score book. For the 

M1 rifle one click of the elevating knob moves the strike of the bullet one inch for each 100 yds. of range, up or down as indicated by the arrow and lettering on the knob. 

153. The Zero of the Rifle. See Par. 162. 

154. Sight Setting and Sight Changing Exercises. These exercises are conducted on the standard targets, using spotters to show assumed positions of hits. Each pupil has a rifle, score book and pencil. The instructor explains the effects of wind, how to determine its direction and velocity, and how to set the sights, using the data in the score book and the rules in Pars. 150-1-2. The instruction then proceeds

RIFLE TRAINING 

by the coach and pupil method. The instructor announces certain conditions and causes the pupils to set their sights. He has spotters placed to indicate assumed positions of hits, and makes changes in the assumed conditions from time to time. 

The pupils set and change the settings of their sights according to the data given. 

The coaches verify the sight settings of their pupils, and inform the instructor when called upon. 

155. The Score Book. Each pupil keeps a record of his shooting in his own score book. He should carefully study the book until he understands the information as to sight setting, and how to plot his shots on the recording targets and enter the other data required. From a study of his entries he learns the actual effects of range conditions and his own mistakes, and how they may be compensated. He determines the zero of his rifle for each range. Score book exercises are a part of preparatory training. The instructor announces the assumed range and weather conditions and causes the students to set their sights. He then indicates with spotters 10 successive assumed hits on a B target, causes them to plot these shots on a recording target, and after each one to make the necessary changes in sight settings. From time to time he announces assumed changes in weather conditions. Each pupil is assisted by a coach. The rifleman should study his score book until he understands everything in it.

Sixth Step. Examination Before Starting Range Practice 

156. Preparatory training concludes with a practical examination on every detail of the instruction. The instructor asks questions and the pupils answer in their own words, or demonstrate the procedure. See the questions at the end of this course.

RANGE PRACTICE 

Courses to be Fired 157. The various courses to be fired, the personnel to fire them, scores and qualifications, are set forth in Army Regulations and Field Manuals. They are subject to change from time to time. 

158. Range practice includes small-bore (.22 caliber) firing, and .30 caliber firing, both indoors at diminished distances, and outdoors at full distances. 

Each course includes instruction practice, and record practice for qualification. The various grades of marksmanship, in order of excellence, are: expert rifleman, sharpshooter, marksman, and unqualified. 

159. The courses include slow fire and rapid fire, in all positions, and at ranges of 50 ft., 1000 inches, 50 yds., 100 yds., 200 yds., 300 yds., and 500 yds. They are thus greatly varied, to simulate battle conditions. In addition to known distance firing, instruction is given also in firing on landscape targets, field targets, and moving targets, both ground and aerial. These courses are later discussed. 

160. Purpose. A rifleman does not learn to shoot on the target range. The purpose of range firing is to enable him to apply the principles and methods he has been taught in his preparatory training. Too long training without actual firing becomes dull. Firing is stimulating to interest, the rifleman can see the practical results of his training, and gain a necessary confidence in his own ability to shoot. 

Accordingly range practice should promptly follow the completion of preparatory training.

111 target, 2. A shot cutting a boundary line between areas is given the higher score. 

The 1,000 inch range targets A and D, are miniatures of the full sized A and D targets, reduced in the proportion of 1,000 inches to 200 yds. Values of hits are the same as for the full-sized targets. 

162. Zeroing the Rifles. The zero of a rifle at any range for any individual is the sight setting that, when there is no wind, will cause shots to hit the point of aim. 

It may not correspond to the graduations on the sight. It is determined for each range by each man during his instruction practice, by a study of his score book, in which the zero for each range should then be recorded. In the case of the M1903 rifle the sight cannot be adjusted to read the correct range. In the case of the M1 rifle the elevation should be correctly adjusted at 200 yds. range. The rifleman sets the elevation up 15 clicks of the elevating knob from its lowest elevation. He then fires groups of 2 or 3 shots, and corrects his elevation, and also his windage, to bring the center of his shot group into the center of the bullseye. This having been accomplished he loosens the screw of the elevating knob with his combination tool, pulls it away from the receiver until its teeth are disengaged, sets the 200 yd. mark exactly opposite the index line on the sight base, and tightens the screw. The sight is now correctly set for 200 yds. elevation. The zero windage for all ranges, and the zero elevation for all other ranges, must be determined and recorded. For zeroing of rifles for landscape target firing see Par. 238. 

163. Safety Precautions on the Range. The individual precautions noted in Pars. 

10 and 25 will be observed on the range, and also the following. Rifles in rear of the firing line will not be loaded or unloaded, and their bolts will be kept open. In loading and unloading, on the firing line, rifles will be kept pointed in the general direction of the targets. Loaded rifles will likewise be pointed towards the targets, and will not be unlocked until the firers are in position on the firing line. At the command CEase firingG on the range, all pieces on the firing line will be locked.

If so ordered, they will be unloaded (cleared) and bolts left open. The M1 rifle is not positively safe until it has been cleared (chamber and receiver emptied of cartridges). On completion of firing and before leaving the range, an inspection will be held to make sure that no cartridges remain in rifles or in possession of personnel. 

Ammunition should be shielded from the direct rays of the sun. Range dummy cartridges will never be used except on the firing line in instruction practice. ridges should not be left chambered in a piece heated from firing. Cart- 164. Pads. Riflemen are required (or permitted with M1 rifle) to wear pads on the shoulder and, if the ground is hard, also on the elbows for three or four days at least. Thereafter the muscles are harder and pads may be unnecessary. 

The use of pads which afford any artificial support for the piece is prohibited. Coaching 165. Coaching is of extreme importance during instruction practice. The details which the coach must observe have been explained. He watches the pupil and not the target at which he is firing. He gives most of his attention to the trigger squeeze. Flinching is detected by watching the eye of the pupil for the inevitable wink. If the pupil squeezes the trigger properly he proves to his own satisfaction that his shots will hit the mark when he does so. If he is doing poor shooting the coach first checks the aim with the aiming device. He will then occasionally squeeze the trigger between his right forefinger and thumb; as the pupil aims. Shots fired in this way are nearly always well placed, and serve to convince the pupil that his poor shooting is due to faulty trigger squeeze. The coach also occasionally may slip into the chamber a range dummy cartridge, which so closely resembles a live cartridge that the pupil cannot recognize it. The resulting flinch, when there is no discharge or recoil, will be apparent, even to the pupil himself. In rapid fire the tendency to flinch is increased. An effective method of curing it is to mix range dummy cartridges with the live cartridges in about equal proportions. The pupil can then see very plainly that he is flinching, and this makes him look and feel foolish, and causes him to determine to squeeze his trigger correctly for every shot. 

166. Usually the coach takes the same position as his pupil, beside him, on his left. [14] Sometimes he observes the pupil's conduct from a little in his rear. 

167. Duties of the Coach in Instruction Practice. During slow fire the coach observes his pupil, and corrects all errors. In particular he sees that: (1) The sights are blackened and set at proper range. (2) The ammunition is clean. (3) The pupil takes the correct position, including proper adjustment of the sling. (4) The magazine is loaded from a clip correctly and quickly. (5) The slack of the trigger is taken up promptly. (6) The bolt is operated smoothly and rapidly. (7) The pupil holds his breath while aiming (by watching his back). (8) The pupil does not flinch while aiming (by watching his eye). (9) The aim is correct (by using the aiming device occasionally). (10) The pupil calls each and every shot, immediately after firing. (11) The pupil keeps his score correctly in his book. 

168. In rapid fire errors cannot be corrected during the firing of a.string, but

RIFLE TRAINING 

should be called to the pupil's attention immediately thereafter. Sights cannot be changed and shots are not called. The coach watches particularly to see that the pupil works his bolt quickly and smoothly, and keeps his eye on the target and the piece against his shoulder while so doing; and that he loads quickly without fumbling. (See also Par. 147). 

169. Final Instructions as to Slow Fire. The following instructions will be read and recited to all men under instruction before they go to the firing line. (1) Be sure both sights are blackened. (2) Take your score book to the firing point. From it obtain elevation and windage for your first shot. (3) Fire your first shot carefully and then, if it be necessary, change the sights to bring the next shot into the bullseye. (4) Aim always at the bottom edge of the bullseye. (5) Never attempt to make the bullet strike closer to the bullseye by changing the point of aim. Make all changes on the rear sight. (6) Do not change the windage until you have looked in the score book to see what you need, and at your wind gauge to see what you already have. (7) Plot all shots on the blank recording target in the score book. Note where the shot group falls, and try to bring the center of the group to the center of the bullseye by making the necessary changes in sight setting. (8) Do not change sights unnecessarily. If you have made several good shots with a certain setting do not change it because of one bad shot, which is most probably your own fault. (9) Call your shots, and do not change the sights, unless you are certain where the rifle was pointed at the instant of discharge. If you cannot call your shots you have nothing to guide you in making an intelligent change in your sight setting. 

170. Rapid Fire. Rapid fire on the range is conducted in the same manner as the simulated fire exercises in preparatory training. (Par. 146). 

171. Marking the Targets. The value and location of each hit at slow fire are indicated by a marking disc of appropriate size and color centered in front of it; white for a five (bullseye), red for a four, black and white for a three, and black for a two. Misses are indicated by waving a red flag. Ricochet hits are scored as misses. A score at rapid fire is similarly marked. Spotters are placed in the bullet holes and the marking disc is swung slowly across the face of the target until opposite the spotter, and then clear of the target. The hits of highest value are marked first. Any cartridges remaining unfired at the end of the limit count as misses. 

After each shot or score the bullet holes are covered with small pasters. In 1,000” firing the targets are withdrawn, marked, and replaced by fresh targets after each score. Spotters and marking discs are unnecessary. 

172. Small-Bore Practice. Small-bore practice is conducted with a .22 caliber 

5-shot magazine rifle, at ranges of 50 feet (or 1,000 ins.) and 50, 100 and 200 yds.

Special small-bore targets are provided. The courses are conducted in the same manner as full distance firing. The effect of recoil is lacking, as also wind effect on indoor ranges. Small-bore practice has the advantages, however, that it is easily conducted (indoors) in any season or weather, and is economical of ammunition. 

CHAPTER V. 

MARKSMANSHIP, MOVING TARGETS Introduction 

173. During combat riflemen must frequently fire on more or less rapidly moving targets, and must be trained in the technique of such fire. Moving targets include vehicles and personnel on the ground, and aircraft. 

174. Basic Principles. If a moving target is at point blank range (100 ft. or less), or if it is moving directly toward or directly away from the firer, he aims directly at the target. If the target is at more than point blank range, and is moving at an angle to the line of sight it is called a crossing target. To hit a crossing target the rifleman must aim at a point on the line of movement or flight of the target, at such a distance in front of it that the bullet and the target will reach such point at the same time. This procedure is called "leading the target," and the distance in front of it that the rifleman aims, is called the "lead." 

175. The lead necessary to hit a moving target depends on the range to the target, its speed, its direction of movement with reference to the line of fire, and the time of flight of the bullet. Moving targets are usually exposed to fire for only a short time. The rifleman cannot estimate the effects of all these circumstances, nor can he remember any elaborate statistics concerning them that may have been determined mathematically. So leads must be determined by a very few very simple "rules of thumb" or approximations. 

176. The principles of marksmanship, accurate sighting and aiming, holding, and trigger squeeze, are at least as important in firing at moving targets as at fixed targets. The rifleman must hit the point at which he decides to aim. If a group of riflemen aim correctly the natural dispersion of their shots will produce some hits on the target. 

177. Experience shows that fire at moving targets at ranges beyond 600 yds. is usually a waste of ammunition. So the fire is limited to that range.

MARKSMANSHIP. MOVING TARGETS 

113 178. The rifleman will have no time to set his sights. Therefore in all fire on moving targets the battle sight is used, except that with the 1903 rifle when firing at ground targets, the peep sight already set at 300 yds. may also be used. (Battle sight range is 547 yds. for the 1903 rifle, and 300 yds. for the M1). Further allowance for range is made by variations in the lead and aim. 

179. There is no unit of measure for leads that the rifleman can quickly apply except the target itself. That is all he sees. So the unit of measure for leads is the actual target. It is interesting to note here that the target may be used as a measure whatever the angle at which it crosses the line of fire, because the length of the target and its apparent lateral speed are both reduced or foreshortened in the same proportion when it moves at an acute angle to the line of fire. 

180. When the trigger is correctly squeezed the rifleman does not know when the piece will go off. Accordingly, when he has obtained the correct lead, the rifle must continue to be swung smoothly and uniformly to maintain the lead while squeezing the trigger. The tendency to stop swinging the piece when the lead has been obtained, and fire instantly by jerking the trigger, must be avoided. This is of utmost importance. The rifleman begins to squeeze the trigger as soon as he has his lead, and maintains the lead by swinging his piece while pressing the trigger. 

181. Firing on Moving Vehicles. The following table of leads is furnished as a guide:

183. Rate of Fire. As moving targets are usually exposed a relatively short time, rapid fire, as prescribed in the course of marksmanship training, is used to attack them. The rate of fire is from 10 to 15 shots per minute for the M1903 rifle, to as high as 30 per minute for the M1 rifle. Rates in excess of these result in a waste of ammunition.

Air Targets 

184. Any low-flying airplane is a suitable target for rifle fire. The propeller, the engine, the bombs, and the pilot are all vulnerable. Any hit will necessitate repair, if it does not disable the plane. 

185. Range Estimation. The range to an airplane can be determined with fair accuracy by its appearance at key ranges, thus: At 1,000 yds. the general outline is recognizable. At 700 yds. the wheels, rudder, wing struts, and tail skid are visible. At 500 yds. the antennae and small projections from the fuselage are discernible. At 200 yds. symbols and lettering can be read. Fire is opened as soon as the targets are within effective range and continued until they are beyond such range. 

186. Target Designation. Fire Distribution. The aerial targets of a single small unit, as a rifle platoon, will be few in number and clearly visible when within effective range. Designation consists in the warning of the approach of hostile planes. Riflemen prepare to fire, and wait until the planes are within effective range. Orders for distribution of fire on a flight of airplanes must be a matter of routine. The target may consist of a single plane, two planes, or a flight of three planes in the form of a V with one plane leading. The first squad of the platoon may be assigned to the leading plane, the second squad to the right plane, the third squad to the left plane. If there be less than three planes, units having no assigned target fire on 

Plate 16. Leads in Antiaircraft Fire 

Four target lengths is an average lead for battle ranges.

RIFLE TRAINING 

the leading plane. This distribution is not changed except for cogent reasons. 

187. Target Leads. Leads for aerial targets are at best approximations. For an airplane 30 feet long, moving at 200 miles per hour, they vary from one target length at a range of 100 yds., to 9 lengths at a range of 600 yds. If there is opportunity the lead may be announced in a fire order. But it changes rapidly, and in its application by the rifleman there is still further approximation. Also there will seldom be opportunity to announce leads. Accordingly an average lead for all ranges is used. This is four target lengths, corresponding to a range of about 300 yds. 

Hence the rule: Aim and fire each shot with a lead of four target lengths. [16] 

This applies in all cases except when the target is diving directly at or climbing directly away from the firer, or is closer than 100 yds. In these cases the rifleman aims directly at the target. 

188. Rate of Fire. The rate of fire at aerial targets is as rapid as careful aiming and correct trigger squeeze permit. For a well trained rifleman this rate may be from 15 to 20 shots per minute for the M1903 rifle, and from 25 to 30 for the M1 (semiautomatic). Anything above this will usually cause a waste of ammunition. 

189. Firing Positions. Firing positions must be such that they can be rapidly assumed, and must permit maximum flexibility of the body in manipulating the rifle. They are accordingly limited to the standing and kneeling positions. The h.. 

HV.WEBR Plate 17. Antiaircraft Firing Position, Kneeling kneeling position used in antiaircraft fire is shown in Plate 17. In antiaircraft fire the sling is not used, and the arms are not supported. The rifle is swung with a smooth motion, the body pivoting at the waist; the rifle, arms, head, and body above the waist moving as a unit. These remarks apply equally to targets that are directly or almost directly overhead, as well as to nonoverhead targets. The two positions prescribed are very suitable for firing directly overhead. 

Marksmanship Training 

190. Effective fire on moving targets requires a considerable degree of judgment and skill, which comes only from training and practice. Accordingly quite elaborate courses of training, involving the use of special equipment, have been devised. They include firing on miniature moving targets, and outdoor firing on towed ground and aerial targets. As the principles of good rifle shooting are applicable, training in fire on moving targets should follow the full course of marksmanship training, hereinbefore prescribed, during which the rifleman is fully instructed in the fundamentals of good shooting.

CHAPTER VI. TECHNIQUE OF RIFLE FIRE Introduction 

191. Fire and Movement. Infantry is the decisive element in battle, and the rifle is the principal weapon of the infantry. Rifle fire superior to that of the enemy is usually essential to victory. Battle ordinarily involves an attack by one side against a position occupied by the other. The attacking infantry, under cover of its own fire and that of its supporting weapons, advances against the enemy's position, to drive him out and occupy his ground. The issue may be determined by hand-to-hand combat within the position, or the defender may retreat in the face of a relentless advance. Only inferior troops can be driven from a position by fire alone. 

The

TECHNIQUE OF FIRE 

115 attack accordingly involves coordinated fire and forward movement; usually culminating in an assault. 

192. Fire Superiority. If the attackers' fire is more effective than that of the defenders, they will be able to advance against the position, and are said to have fire superiority. If the defenders' fire is so effective that the attackers will not advance against it, fire superiority rests with the defense, and if this continues the attack will fail. Fire superiority is thus the keynote of success in battle. All weapons of both combatants will be utilized in the struggle to establish and maintain it. In this struggle the rifle plays an important part. 

193. When subjected to effective fire the average soldier, unless he is well trained, loses his will power. He is apt to be more concernel with his own safety than with firing at the enemy. He seeks such cover as is available, and is reluctant to expose himself in order to take careful aim, or to leave his cover and advance. His fire either ceases, or becomes wild and ineffective. Fire superiority is the attacker's best protection, often his only protection, during his advance. He takes advantage of fire superiority to push forward to a position where still more effective fire can be delivered. Such fire, accompanied by a steady and relentless advance, is highly demoralizing to the defender. On the other hand the defender enjoys the advantages of being in a prepared position, concealed, and perhaps protected by trenches and obstacles. He does not have to leave his cover and move in the open. He has opportunity to clear his field of fire and determine ranges to his front, etc. Accordingly the attacker endeavors to mass a greater number of troops and weapons at the decisive point, to overcome these natural advantages of the defense. 

194. Fire superiority is essentially a moral phenomenon. But generally the moral effect will be proportional to the physical effect, that is, the number and distribution of actual hits. The physical elements of fire superiority are large volume; accuracy; and effective control, direction, and distribution of fire; all of which are discussed in this course. Fire superiority having been established, it must be continuously maintained, or else the ascendency passes to the enemy. This requires that every part of the defenders' position, or every element of the attacking force, be subjected to an effective and continuous fire, properly distributed. Otherwise any troops that are relieved, even temporarily, from the pressure, will fire with increased effect, or take advantage of the opportunity to push forward. The moral effect of the attacker's fire depends also on a steady and relentless advance. Success in attack requires fire and movement. 

195. The Fire Unit. The unit of rifle fire in battle is the rifle squad, which can deliver combined or collective fire under the direct control of a single leader. Collective fire is most effective when it is the result of teamwork. Instruction in the technique of fire, as set forth in this course, is designed to train the rifle squad to act effectively as a team in the application of collective fire.¹ 

196. Method of Training. Training in technique of fire is first conducted without reference to tactical situations, but is later applied to tactical training. The instructor explains and demonstrates each step, making clear to his pupils its importance and its relation to the subject as a whole. The troops then practice the successive steps, and proficiency is obtained by repetition. Exercises for each step are prescribed. These can be used for demonstration, practice, and test. 

197. Scope of Training. On the battlefield, unlike the target range, ranges are usually unknown and targets indistinct or even invisible, and widely dispersed. 

To deliver effective fire we must be able to estimate ranges, designate or point out indistinct targets, and distribute fire so that the entire target is covered. The leader must be able to control and direct fire, shift it from one target to another, disperse or concentrate it, and regulate the rate of fire. The members of the team must respond promptly and intelligently to the commands or signals of the leader. The training covers the technique of these operations, and includes six steps: (1) Range estimation. (2) Target designation. (3) Rifle fire and its effects. (4) Application of fire. (5) Landscape target firing. (6) Field target firing.

Range Estimation 

198. The methods of range estimation used in battle are: (1) By eye; (2) By tracer bullets; (3) By observation of fire. 

199. By Eye. Ranges are usually estimated in this way. Untrained men will make large errors, and at ranges exceeding 300 yards the effects of errors are serious. 

The average of several estimates by different men will usually be closer than a single estimate, but it will often be necessary for each man to estimate the range for himself. The rifleman mentally applies a "hundred yard ruler" to the range to be determined. He must be familiar with the appearance of this unit of measure at various ranges and on various terrains. To measure a range exceeding 500 yards the rifleman selects the half-way point, or a point nearby which he estimates to be at half 1Training in the application of collective fire by fire units was formerly known as Musketry, and this term will still be found in current literature.

RIFLE TRAINING 

the range of his target, measures the range to this point with his mental ruler, and 

200. Often part of the terrain between the rifleman and his target will be invisible. It is then necessary to estimate the distance across such terrain by the appearance of familiar objects (men, animals, buildings) on the far side. Allowance 350 +300 800, 100 300 

400 HALF-WAY POINT 

200 100 

Plate 18. Range Estimation by Fire. Applying the Unit of Measure must be made for certain conditions which affect the apparent range. An object seems nearer: (1) When in bright light; (2) When the atmosphere is clear, especially in high altitudes; (3) When its colors contrast sharply with its surroundings; (4) When looking across a uniform surface, such as water or snow; (5) When looking downward; (6) When looking across an invisible depression. An object seems farther: (1) When in dim light or mist; (2) When only a part of it is visible; (3) When looking upward; (4) When looking across a visible depression. 

201. Tracer Bullets. A scout or leader estimates a range by eye and fires tracer bullets, correcting his sight setting according to the strike of the bullets until they appear to hit the target. He then announces the range. 

202. Observation of Fire. When the ground is such that the strike of bullets can be seen, ranges may be determined by firing ordinary ball cartridges instead of tracers.

Target Designation 

203. Long ranges, smokeless and even flashless powders, inconspicuous uniforms, wide deployments, careful selection of ground, skillful use of cover, and camouflage, combine to render modern battle targets exceedingly indistinct or quite invisible. 

The terrain must be searched to discover them, and their positions and extent must be indicated to the riflemen. The methods of target designation are: (1) Tracer bullets; (2) Pointing; (3) Oral description. Combinations of these methods are used, the easiest and simplest method being always employed. 

204. Tracer Bullets. This is the simplest, quickest and surest method of designating targets. A scout or leader announces: Range 500; watch my tracers. If the target is a point he fires one or more tracers at it. If it has frontage its flanks are thus designated and announced (Right flank, Left flank). This method may be used by a leader, or by scouts out in front or off to a flank, and the tracers can be seen by all.

The disadvantages are that it gives warning to the enemy and sacrifices part of the surprise effect of a burst of fire; and may disclose the position of the fire unit. 

205. Pointing. Targets may be designated by pointing with the arm, or aiming a rifle at them. The rifle is canted to the right so that a man looking over the shoulder of the rifleman can see the target through or over the rear sight. Or the rifle may be supported by a rest, such as a bayonet or forked stick, stuck in the ground. Pointing may be used in connection with oral description. The disadvantage of this method is that it is slow. Only men near at hand and only one at a time can look through the sights. 

206. Oral Description. Oral description is commonly employed, especially by squad leaders, when tracer bullets are not available or their use is inadvisable; when the target is so indistinct that pointing will not serve; for fleeting targets; and in cases where it is the simplest method. Oral description includes the following elements, in the order named: (1) Distance (range); (2) Direction; (3) Description. 

The remainder of the fire order (COMMENCE FIRING, FIRE AT WILL, or Fire at my signal) then follows. The formal description should be the simplest possible, for example: Range 400; to right front; enemy group. This may be supplemented by informal conversation and by pointing. To designate or recognize a target all men

When the direction is announced each looks in the indicated direction and selects a point which he estimates to be at the announced range. When the description is given he searches the immediate locality for a target of that description. 

207. Reference Points and Lateral Measurements. If the target is not easily identified or close to some prominent feature of the terrain, its direction may be designated by means of a reference point, a conspicuous and unmistakable feature as close as possible to the target laterally, and preferably at the same or greater range. The lateral interval between the reference point and the target and the width of front of a lineal target extending from the flank nearer the reference are measured in a unit called a "sight." This is the apparent width of the raised rear SIGHTS

sight leaf, which subtends a lateral angle of 50 mils when held at a distance of about 14 inches from the eye. Finger widths may be used instead of sights when each man has learned how far in front of his eye to hold his fingers so that each will subtend a lateral angle of 50 mils.
