Example (lineal target): [20] Range 500; to right front; reference water tank; left one sight; edge of woods; target, skirmish line extending left two sights. Successive reference points may be employed instead of or in addition to sight measurements; as when a conspicuous_reference is used to identify one less conspicuous but closer to the target.

209. If one end of the target is nearer than the other, the average range is announced. Often the leader can designate the target to only one or two men, so that all men must be trained to designate targets to each other. The simplest method of designation is always preferred. 

RIFLE FIRE AND ITS EFFECTS 

210. Trajectory. The trajectory is the path of the bullet. [21] The speed of a rifle bullet on leaving the muzzle is about 2700 feet per second, called the muzzle velocity. 

Because of this high velocity the trajectory at battle ranges is nearly a straight line. 

But after reaching the summit of its trajectory the bullet falls to earth under the influence of gravity. The range is evidently increased by elevating the muzzle. 

211. Danger Space. Danger space is that part of the trajectory in which the height of the bullet above the ground does not exceed the height of the average man, 5 feet 8 inches. Within this space a man erect in the line of fire will be struck. 

Danger spaces for various ranges are shown in Plate 21. 

212. Cone of Dispersion. Shot Group. Beaten Zone. Danger Zone. Even when a rifle is aimed at the same target with the same sight setting the bullets do not all -5 YDS

Plate 21. Elements of the Trajectory; Rifle or Automatic Rifle

The vertical scale is exaggerated 30 times. The height of the summit for any range, indicated on the plate, is the maximum ordinate for that range. That part of the trajectory between the muzzle and the summit is the ascending branch. From the summit to the point of impact is the descending branch. 

The angle of fall is the angle between the trajectory and the horizontal at the point of impact. follow the same path. They are dispersed, forming a sheath or cone, called the cone of dispersion. [22] Dispersion is due to many causes, including variations in rifles and ammunition, aiming, and weather effects. The dispersion of a group of men is naturally greater than that of one rifleman. When the bullets in the cone of dispersion strike any surface they form an elliptical pattern, called a shot group. The shot group formed on the ground is a very long, narrow ellipse, called the beaten zone. [23] 

213. The shots are not uniformly distributed, most of them being close to the center of the ellipse. The length of the beaten zone, decreases from about 400 yds.

TECHNIQUE OF FIRE. CLASSES OF FIRE 

119 at range 200 yds., to about 100 yds. at range 1,000 yds. Its width increases from about 2 ft. at range 200 yds. to about 72 ft. at range 1,000 yds. The length of the beaten zone is greatly influenced by the slope of the ground. [23] Rising ground shortens it, producing plunging fire; falling ground lengthens it, producing grazing fire. 

214. In Plate 22 it will be noted that a man standing in the beaten zone or in the adjacent danger space is apt to be hit. These two spaces, taken together, constitute the danger zone for the particular range, which may extend over several hundred yards. [21; 22] In addition there is a danger zone beyond the beaten zone due to ricochets, or bouncing bullets, but these rapidly lose velocity. 

215. Classes of Fire. According to its direction with reference to the target [24] fire is classified as: frontal, oblique, enfilade, flanking, and reverse. Converging fire is simultaneous fire from several directions. According to its relation to the ground 1 2 3 

Plate 23. Effect of Slopes on Beaten Zone 

1. On level ground. 2. On rising ground zone is shortened, plunging fire. 3. On falling ground zone is lengthened, grazing fire. If the ground on a reverse slope is steeper than the angle of fall of bullets clearing the crest, the bullets cannot strike the reverse slope, which is then said to be defiladed from such fire. A slope defiladed from flat trajectory fire can usually be reached by mortar fire. fire is classified as grazing, [23-3] and plunging [23-2]. Overhead fire is that delivered over the heads of friendly troops, and is exceptional for rifle fire. Assault fire may be employed during the last rush, within 150 yards of the defenders' position, in order to keep it under fire at this time. Riflemen load while advancing, halt individually, aim and fire straight to the front. Bayonets are fixed before beginning assault fire. Assault fire can seldom be successfully employed unless the enemy is badly demoralized or the attacker enjoys a decided superiority in the fire of all his weapons. 

216. The conditions conducive to maximum fire effect are flanking, enfilade, reverse, converging, and grazing fire, delivered by surprise, at close range, against an enemy in an exposed position and vulnerable formation. These favorable conditions can often be created by skilful maneuver and utilization of the ground, and prompt advantage should be taken of every opportunity to profit from any one of them, or a combination of two or more. [24]

217. Rifle fire is seldom employed against material. It is, however, very effective against low flying airplanes. Each rifleman and automatic rifleman carries a few rounds of armor piercing ammunition. The AP bullet will penetrate a 14" steel

RIFLE TRAINING 

armor plate at 500 yards, or a ½" plate at 100 yards. It is used in emergency against armored airplanes, tanks and trucks. 

218. Ranges in Attack and Defense. The effective range of the rifle is limited by the vision of the rifleman and not by the power of the weapon. Experience shows that in attack rifle fire is seldom effective beyond 600 yards. By use of cover and invulnerable formations, and by the aid of supporting fires, the attacking infantry endeavors to approach within 400 and not over 600 yards before opening rifle fire. The defenders, by reason of the advantages mentioned in Par. 193, can sometimes open effective fire at ranges of from 800 to 1000 yards.

APPLICATION OF FIRE 

219. The members of a fire unit must be able to place aimed fire on any target that is designated. The leader must be able to apply the collective fire of his unit to good effect. He must be able to control the fire, and this in turn demands fire discipline. 

The proper application of rifle fire in battle calls for high standards of training, discipline, judgment, skill and leadership. 

220. Targets. The targets presented will ordinarily be individuals or small groups of the enemy, moving or stationary. Sometimes they will be exposed for only a brief time (transitory or fleeting targets). But except at very close range, individuals and groups are usually invisible. Their positions must be determined by noting localities they would naturally occupy, by the noise and flashes of firing, movement, and other indications. 

221. Positions for Delivery of Fire. The selection of firing positions is a most important duty of all leaders. In defense, positions can be carefully selected and deliberately occupied, but this will seldom be possible in attack. Firing positions should be selected in the light of the following requirements: (1) Good observation and field of fire to the front, and usually also to right and left front; (2) Cover and concealment; (3) An irregular and inconspicuous formation; (4) Control by the leaders.

Positions fulfilling all these requirements will seldom be available, and leaders must use their judgment as to their relative importance. Thus in defense a good field of fire at short and mid range is of first importance; in a delaying action a good field of fire at close range is unnecessary or even undesirable. Cover and concealment are most important, but their use must be subordinated to effective delivery of fire. 

Within each position subordinate units and individuals must dispose themselves to best advantage in the light of the above requirements. 

222. Distributed and Concentrated Fire. Concentrated fire is that which is delivered against a single point target, as a hostile machine gun, or a small area. 

Distributed fire is that which is applied as uniformly as possible to the entire length of a lineal target, for the purpose of keeping all parts of it continuously under fire. 

Rifle fire in both attack and defense is usually thus distributed. Riflemen as a rule do not deliver fire over the heads of friendly troops in their front. Sometimes, however, the terrain makes possible the delivery of overhead fire. 

Fire Distribution 

223. The platoon leader will, as necessary, assign to each squad a lineal target or frontage to be covered by distributed fire. He may divide the platoon target into sectors or parts, usually overlapping, and assign a part to each squad on the firing line. Or he may cause some or all of the squads to cover the entire platoon target. 

In any event, having been assigned a lineal target, the squad distributes its fire thereon by a routine procedure which insures that every part of the target will be covered. 

224. Whatever the number actually firing, each rifleman fires his first shot at a point corresponding approximately to his position in the squad. He then distributes his shots at approximately equal intervals to the right and left of his first shot, over as wide a sector of fire as he can cover accurately without changing his position or endangering other men who may be farther forward. The width of the sector thus depends on the range, the nature of the terrain, and the position of the rifleman. 

Sometimes he will cover the entire squad target. Fire is not limited to observed enemy individuals. Often these cannot be seen. But each shot should be directed at a definite aiming point in the target. This procedure is routine, that is, done without specific command. It insures that every part of the target will be subjected to continuous fire. All squads and individuals distribute their fire in the same manner. 

225. Rate of Fire. The average rate of fire of infantry in battle is a matter of ammunition supply rather than the capacity of the rifleman and the characteristics of his weapon. The rate while actually firing should be that most effective under existing conditions. To exceed such rate results in a waste of ammunition. The

TECHNIQUE OF FIRE. DISTRIBUTION AND CONTROL 

121 greater the range the less the rate of fire. Well trained soldiers can attain a rate of rapid fire of 10 to 15 properly aimed shots per minute with the M1903 rifle, and as high as 30 shots per minute with the M1 rifle. These are maximum rates which should not be exceeded. In the excitement of battle men have a tendency to fire too fast. Leaders should be watchful to prevent this. For moral effect or to fire on specially remunerative or fleeting targets, rates may be increased for short periods. They may be decreased to steady the men, to regain control, or to prevent waste of ammunition if the shooting is wild. Leaders command or signal: FIRE 

FASTER or FIRE SLOWER. 

226. Fire Discipline. Fire discipline is a state of order, steadiness and obedience among troops engaged in a fire fight. It is indicated by the efficiency with which the men perform their duties and react to the control of their leaders. It is the ultimate test of discipline and training. Fire discipline is maintained by the leaders, chiefly by their own example of coolness, courage, and immunity to excitement or panic. 

All training which develops the soldierly qualities is an aid to fire discipline. Replacement of casualties is an element of fire discipline. If any group finds itself without a leader one should assume command, carry on the mission of the group, or attach it to the nearest functioning unit. Individuals who find themselves alone carry on their missions or attach themselves to the nearest squad. Fire discipline is the special function of platoon and squad leaders, who are assisted by their seconds in command. Squad leaders in particular, are immediately responsible for the maintenance of fire discipline. 

227. All orders and signals are repeated back to the sender, and passed along as necessary. A continuous effective fire, at proper rates must be maintained, and waste of ammunition prevented. Advantage is taken of every opportunity to replenish ammunition. Leaders see that men take proper positions, utilize available cover, and avoid unnecessary exposure. 

Fire Control 

228. Fire control is usually a function of the squad or a smaller unit leader. It implies the opening and cessation of fire, and its application to proper targets by squad leaders.. If squad leaders are unable at all times to exercise personal control the responsibility falls upon the individual riflemen. 

229. Fire control is exercised by means of fire orders, commands, and signals. 

When practicable fire orders are issued to the entire squad by its leader. But after the opening of the fire fight this will often be impossible, and control must then be exercised by orders and signals, or by the leader going to the men to whom he wishes to give orders. The signals most commonly employed in fire control are: RANGE; 

COMMENCE firing; fire faster (slower); CEASE FIRING; ARE YOU

READY? I AM READY; FIX BAYONETS. These signals are described and illust rated in Infantry Drill Regulations. 

230. Sights must be changed as ranges decrease. Beyond 500 yds. they should be reset every 50 yds., and between 500 and 300 every 100 yds. Below 300 yds sight changes are unnecessary. 

231. Fire Orders. The desired targets are engaged and fire upon them is controlled by means of fire orders issued by the leader of the fire unit. A formal fire order includes three elements, which are stated or understood in the following sequence: (1) The target designation element. (2) The fire distribution element. 

(3) The fire control element. (1) The target may be designated by any one or a combination of the prescribed methods. (Par. 203). (2) Fire distribution is usually a matter of routine (Par. 223), and in such cases is understood without specific orders. But the squad leader may wish to attack simultaneously two or even three point or lineal targets. Or he may wish to divide a lineal target, assigning a part of the squad to each portion. In such cases he must give orders for the desired distribution. (See examples following). (3) The fire control element usually consists initially of the command or signal for opening fire, as COMMENCE FIRING or FIRE AT WILL. It may prescribe a number of rounds, as: FIVE rounds, firE AT WILL. In preparation for movement, to designate a new target, to regain control of the squad, or for other reason, the leader commands or signals: CEAse firing. 

232. The leader may engage two targets by placing part of the squad under command of his assistant, with orders to engage a certain target, while he engages another target or another portion of a target with the remainder of the squad. To switch the fire of one or more men to a new target the leader goes to the particular men, causes them to cease firing, and gives the fire order for the new target. Or he may direct his assistant to do this. 

233. Examples of Fire Orders. (1) Range, 500. Front. Machine gun at left corner of barn. At my signal. Five rounds, fire at will. (2) Range, 450. Reference, fence corner to left front. Right one sight. 

Target, enemy line extending two sights right. Commence firing.

RIFLE TRAINING 

(3) Range, 500. Left front. Machine gun at base of lone, dead tree.

Adams (asst. squad leader), Brown, Cooper, Davis, Ellis, Farley, your target. Range, 550. Left front. Enemy group to left of right haystack. Gregg, Harris, Irwin, Jackson,

Keller, your target. At my signal. Ten rounds, fire at will. 

234. Duties of Leaders in Technique of Fire. Squad Leader. (1) Carries out the orders of the platoon leader, maintains fire discipline, and controls the fire of his squad. (2) Selects firing positions. (3) Designates targets and issues fire orders. (4) Observes targets and effects of fire of both sides. 

235. Assistant Squad Leader. (1) Carries out the orders of the squad leader, and assists him in maintaining fire discipline and control of fire. (2) If the squad leader is absent for any reason he assumes command of the squad. (3) He may participate in the firing if so directed by the squad leader. 

CHAPTER VII. LANDSCAPE AND FIELD TARGET FIRING LANDSCAPE TARGET FIRING 

236. Landscape target firing affords opportunity for applying practically many of the principles of rifle firing. Individual instruction is facilitated, and the accessibility of the targets makes it possible to apply fire and observe its results in a minimum of time. Instruction may be conducted indoors, when range facilities are lacking or weather conditions are unfavorable. Firing is preferably done with .22 caliber rifles, equipped with Lyman sights, but service rifles may be used. Field target firing is preferred to landscape target firing when it is possible to conduct the former. Landscape target firing is thus not a required part of rifle training. The prescribed range for landscape target firing is 1,000 inches (833 ft.). If room be lacking, as in indoor training, the range may be reduced to 600 inches (50 ft.). 

237. Description and Preparation of Targets. A landscape target is a panorama of terrain on such scale that the important features can be distinguished at 1,000 inch range. The standard target, issued by the Ordnance Dept., consists of 5 connecting sheets, in black and white, each 2 ft. high by 5 ft. wide. Sheets can be colored by hand if desired. Each is mounted on a light, open wooden panel of same size, covered عالم ران 

Plate 25. Landscape Targets with target cloth, to which the paper sheets are carefully pasted, with flour paste; care being taken to avoid wrinkles. The panels are then mounted in order on a frame of 4" x 4" posts set vertically, 5 ft. center to center, with 2" x 4" longitudinals. 

The panels are attached with cleats or pins, so as to be easily set up or taken down. 

The panels or sheets are designated and marked with cards, CENTER, RIGHT 

FRONT, LEFT FRONT, RIGHT FLANK, and LEFT FLANK. For purposes of target designation only, ranges in hundreds of yards are indicated by cards tacked to the ends of the target. Features of the terrain on a level with any range card are assumed to be at the range indicated. This set-up is shown in Plate 25. 

238. Zeroing of Rifles. Before firing on the landscape target rifles must be zeroed. (Par. 162). For this purpose a blank target panel is used. A line of 1 inch square black pasters is placed parallel to and about 6 inches from the lower edge of the panel. Rifle sights are blackened. The squad to fire is deployed 1,000 ins. from the target, prone with sandbag rests. Sights are set at zero windage and elevation (or 100 yds. elevation for the M1903, or 200 for the M1). Each man is assigned the paster corresponding to his position in the squad. M1903 rifles are loaded with 

3 rounds only, M1 rifles are issued 3 rounds to be loaded and fired separately. Each man fires 3 rounds at his paster. The instructor then commands: OPEN BOLTS (CLEAR RIFLES, for M1). The instructor and squad leader check the target and give each man the corrections for his next 3 shots. This is continued until each man hits his paster. 

239. Changes in Sight Settings. As approximate rules: at a range of 1000 ins. 

5 minutes change of elevation on the Lyman sight will move the strike of the bullet about 1½ inches. A change of one point windage moves the strike about 

14 inches. For the M1903 rifle a change of 25 yds. in elevation or of one point

LANDSCAPE AND FIELD TARGET FIRING 

123 windage moves the strike about 1 inch. For the M1 rifle one click of elevation or of windage moves the strike about 4 inch. If the range at which firing is conducted be 50 feet (instead of 1000 ins.) the movement of the strike corresponding to any change in elevation or windage will be 0.6 its value at 1000 inches. 

240. Landscape Target Firing Exercises. Rifles being zeroed, the squad at the firing point, is faced to the rear. The instructor takes the leader to the landscape panel and points out a target. The squad then take firing positions. Pieces are loaded, for example with 3 rounds per rifleman. The squad leader gives his fire order, including oral designation of the target. Reference to panels to indicate direction is not allowed. The fire order concludes with the command FIRE AT WILL 

or COMMENCE FIRING. 

241. On the conclusion of firing, bolts are opened. Rifles are left at the firing line, and the squad goes forward to witness the results of firing. A short critique is held, and the panel is scored. Additional and varied exercises may be conducted as follows: (1) The instructor indicates several point targets, and the squad leader applies a portion of his fire to each; (2) While the squad is firing the instructor indicates a new target, assumed to have appeared suddenly, the squad leader switches part of his fire to the new target; (3) Firing on lineal targets, including fire distribution, will also be practiced. 

242. Scoring. To grade the results of firing scoring devices are utilized. The scoring device for a point target consists of two concentric rectangles, the inner being 2 inches wide by 2½ inches high, and the outer of double these dimensions. The inner rectangle, called the 50% area, should inclose 50% of the shots by average marksmen at 1000 inches, when the center of the rectangle coincides with the point aimed at. The outer rectangle similarly should contain 75% of the shots. For distributed fire on a lineal target the scoring device consists of two concentric zones, whose axis is the line of the target, and which extend beyond the limits of the target one inch at each end. The 50% zone is 2½ inches high and the 75% zone 5″ high. 

The zones are divided by vertical lines into a number of equal distribution spaces, as 4 or 10, according to the length of the target or the number of men firing. Scoring devices may be wire frames of the prescribed dimensions, or may be drawn on sheets of transparent celluloid. They are applied to the target in scoring. If the range be 50 feet all dimensions of scoring devices will be 0.6 those prescribed for a range of 1000 inches. 

243. For a point target the score is the aggregate value of hits in the 50% and 

75% rectangles. For example: (1) Number of rounds fired 25; (2) Hits in 50% rectangle 15, score 60; (3) Hits in 75% rectangle outside 50% rectangle 9, score 

18; (4) Aggregate score 78; (5) Score for average marksmen 64; (6) Maximum possible score 100. For a lineal target the score for hits and distribution might be as follows: (1) Number of rounds fired 25; (2) Number of hits in 50% zone 15, score 60; (3) Number of hits in 75% zone outside 50% zone 9, score 18; (4) Number of distribution spaces hit 8, score 80; (5) Aggregate score 158÷2=79; (6) Maximum possible score 200÷2=100.

FIELD TARGET FIRING 

244. Purpose and Scope. The purpose of this firing is further instruction of leaders in the control of their units under simulated battle conditions, and of riflemen in their duties as members of the fire unit. The training is similar to that given in landscape target firing, but with the added realistic features of scattered, moving, and concealed targets, selection of firing positions and use of cover, wind, estimation of unknown ranges, and fire control under more nearly battle conditions. Firing is first conducted on exposed targets, followed by fire on targets concealed from view but not covered from fire. The training is given to squads or smaller groups. Preferably the terrain should be varied and unfamiliar to the men under instruction, but existing rifle ranges must often be utilized. 

245. Targets. Targets for field firing may be obtained from the Ordnance

Department, or easily improvised. They are shown in Plate 26. The E and F targets are silhouettes of prone and kneeling riflemen, of heavy paper board, mounted on stakes which can be driven into the ground. Appearing and disappearing targets and moving targets are simulated by mounting the E or F targets on I beams (of wood), and on sleds. The suddenly appearing targets are operated by men in deep, nearby pits, preferably in front of or to a flank of the targets to evade ricochets. 

Moving targets are towed by men, horses, or winches, placed at a safe interval to the flank. These expedients are shown in the plate. 

246. Targets should be placed in logical positions, not too conspicuous nor regularly spaced. Some are placed where they can be seen with field glasses but are invisible to the naked eye. Suddenly appearing targets may be signalized as they appear by firing blank cartridges, or some other noise or smoke producing apparatus nearby. 

247. Safety Precautions. The safety precautions prescribed for known distance

Simple pits are sufficient to shelter the target operators, but they should be deep enough and carefully placed. No man firing should be farther to the front than a man next to him by more than half the interval between them. The rule that all rifles should be kept pointed towards the targets at all times is rigidly enforced. 

248. Fire Exercises. The exercises should include movement from an approach march formation or place of concealment to selected firing positions, thus affording instruction in individual movement, use of cover, and selection of firing positions. 

The principles of marksmanship, as taught in the marksmanship course, are observed whenever applicable. The prescribed firing positions are modified to suit the terrain, comfortable positions being taken and any available support for the rifle or body being utilized. The loop sling is used when there is time and opportunity to adjust it, except in the standing position or when bayonets are fixed. Battle sight is commonly used. Some of the exercises should be fired in gas masks. In general every effort should be made to simulate the actual conditions of the battlefield, and 1 2 3 

Plate 27. Rising and Moving Forward from Prone Position 

Being in prone firing position, the command is: 1. Cease firing, 2. Prepare to rush; 3. UP (or 

FOLLOW ME, or other command or signal requiring a movement forward). 

1. At the preparatory command. load and lock piece, lower sight leaf, and take the prone position, body and left cheek flat on the ground, arms extended to the front. piece grasped in the right hand at the balance, lying on the ground diagonally across front of body, muzzle to left. 

2. Draw both hands back slowly until opposite chin, palms down, elbows down and away from body. 

3. At the command of execution, extend both arms, raising body. 

4. Shift weight of body to left arm and leg, and throw right leg forward. 

5. Jump off to right front in crouching position; left shoulder slightly advanced; piece grasped in both hands, right hand at small of stock, left hand at balance. make the exercises as realistic as possible. On the completion of an exercise the targets are examined and scored by any suitable method, for hits and distribution.

250. Critique. At the conclusion of each exercise the instructor conducts a

RIFLE TRAINING 

critique with the unit. He stresses the following points: (1) Purpose and importance of the exercise. (2) Orders given by the squad leader. (3) The approach to and occupation of the firing position. (4) Conduct of individuals. (5) Rates of fire. Fire discipline. Fire control. (6) Effects of fire on the targets. (7) Performance of the unit as a whole-satisfactory or unsatisfactory.
